# The Formula of Becoming

## A Year at McCall-Hart University

# Week One: Two Kinds of Plans

The first thing Malik Baptiste discovered about Bellwether, Alabama, was that the heat did not simply sit on you. It negotiated.

It followed him from the rideshare drop-off to the limestone steps of Eliza Moss Hall, offering sweat in exchange for every box he carried. It fogged the edge of his glasses while he tried to balance a laptop bag, a duffel, and the promise he had made before leaving Gulfport: *I have the money part handled.*

Inside, the Quantitative Summer Bridge welcome room was cool enough to make him forgive the entire city.

Malik chose a table near an outlet, opened his laptop, and pulled up the family budget he had finished three nights ago. He only meant to check one thing. Then he found the mistake.

The repair estimate for his mother's car had been entered in the monthly column instead of the emergency-expense column. Correcting it pushed the August balance below zero.

Not far below zero. But zero did not care how close you came.

He stared at the red number as students entered around him in bright shirts, fresh sneakers, and the nervous confidence of people pretending not to look for somewhere safe to sit.

"That spreadsheet looks like it just told you a family secret."

Malik looked up.

The girl beside the chair across from him wore copper-colored headphones around her neck and carried a notebook thick with tabs. Her smile was curious, not cruel.

"It made an accusation," Malik said.

"Serious one?"

"Misplaced expense."

She pressed a hand to her chest. "In broad daylight?"

He almost smiled. Almost.

She pointed at the chair. "Can I sit here, or is it reserved for the audit committee?"

"Go ahead."

She slid into the seat. "Nia Reynolds. Mathematical sciences. Huntsville. I like graphs when they tell the truth and people when they try to."

"Malik Baptiste. Actuarial science. Gulfport. I like complete introductions."

"That was complete. You know my major, my hometown, and my entire moral philosophy."

This time, he did smile.

Around them, the room filled with the sound of new beginnings: chairs scraping, families calling with last instructions, staff passing out indigo folders stamped with a copper heron. Through the tall windows, McCall-Hart University glowed honey-gold in the morning light. Malik had studied the campus map for two weeks, but a map had not prepared him for how old and alive the place felt.

Nia tilted her head toward his screen. "Do you need help?"

His first instinct was no. His second was also no, but with better manners.

"I can fix it," he said.

"I didn't ask if you could."

That answer stayed with him longer than it should have.

## Friday, August 1: The Red Number

The Bridge program opened with introductions, placement details, and a challenge from Dr. Camille Brooks, a calculus professor with silver-framed glasses and the calm voice of someone who could hear weak reasoning from across a building.

"This program is not here to prove whether you belong," she told the room. "Admission settled that question. We are here to learn how you think, where your preparation is strong, and where we need to build."

Malik wrote the sentence down exactly.

Nia drew a box around *where we need to build* and added a tiny crane beside it.

Their first activity was simple: examine a table showing hours worked, transportation costs, and weekly take-home pay. Identify the variables. Describe a possible relationship. State what the table did not reveal.

Malik listed the inputs, output, units, and likely errors before Nia had finished drawing arrows.

"Why are you decorating the data?" he asked.

"I'm showing the situation."

"The situation is in the columns."

"No, the numbers are in the columns. The situation is a person trying to get to work."

Malik glanced at the prompt. "It doesn't say that."

"Exactly. We don't know whether the transportation cost is gas, a bus pass, rideshare money, or paying somebody's cousin. That changes what choices are possible."

He disliked how quickly her answer improved his.

When Dr. Brooks asked for observations, Malik explained the numeric pattern. Nia followed by naming the missing context. Dr. Brooks nodded at both of them.

"A clean calculation built on an invisible assumption is still an unstable model," she said. "Make your assumptions visible."

Malik underlined that sentence too.

At lunch, Nia found him correcting the family budget.

"You fixed the accusation?"

"I moved the expense, reduced two categories, and added a warning rule."

"So your spreadsheet can yell before the problem gets bad."

"It does not yell. It changes color."

"Corporate yelling."

He showed her the repaired balance. He did not show her the text from his mother asking whether everything still worked. Some truths felt easier in formulas than in conversation.

## Saturday, August 2: Room at the Table

Saturday's Bridge session was optional, which meant Nia arrived ten minutes late carrying iced coffee and insisting that optional mornings should begin at noon.

Malik had already claimed the same table.

"Look at us," she said, sitting across from him. "A tradition."

"It has happened twice."

"Every tradition starts before somebody puts it on a T-shirt."

They worked through readiness problems until Nia noticed Malik checking the budget between questions. He minimized it.

"You do that every time somebody walks by," she said.

"Do what?"

"Hide the sheet."

"It is private."

"Private is fine. Panicked is different."

Malik's shoulders tightened. Nia saw it and leaned back.

"Sorry," she said. "That came out like I had earned access. I haven't."

The quick apology surprised him more than the question.

He reopened the file. "My family budget was balanced when I left. Now it isn't. I told them it was."

"Was that a lie?"

"No."

"Then it was a model with new information."

"That sounds like something people say to feel better about being wrong."

"Sometimes. It can also be true."

She took out her notebook and drew two boxes. In the first, she wrote *what changed*. In the second, *what can change next*.

Malik looked at the page. "That is not a budget."

"Correct. It is me stopping you from staring at one red number like it has been elected president of your life."

He laughed loudly enough that two students turned around.

Together they found a duplicate textbook estimate and a subscription Malik could pause. The balance returned to black, but that was not the part he remembered. He remembered that Nia had known when to ask, when to back up, and when to make him laugh.

## Sunday, August 3: Draw It First

Their diagnostic modeling task arrived Sunday afternoon.

A neighborhood food cooperative wanted to offer produce boxes at several prices. Students had to create a model showing how the number of subscribers could affect weekly revenue. The prompt included a tiny data table and just enough ambiguity to start an argument.

Malik built formulas immediately.

Nia turned the page sideways and drew three families standing near a bus stop.

"We have forty minutes," he said.

"I know."

"Then why are there people?"

"Because people buy the boxes."

"The model needs variables."

"And the variables need meaning."

He pointed to his screen. "Subscribers, price, revenue."

She pointed to her drawing. "Distance, pickup time, household size. If the pickup site is hard to reach, lowering the price might not increase subscribers the way we expect."

"None of that is in the data."

"Then we write that it is not in the data."

For five stubborn minutes, they worked beside each other instead of together. Malik's table became neat and uselessly confident. Nia's sketch became thoughtful and mathematically unfinished.

Finally, Malik rotated his laptop toward her. "Suppose we label my formula a first model and list your factors as limitations."

Nia rotated her notebook toward him. "Suppose we also explain what information we would collect next."

"That is outside the prompt."

"It is also a good idea."

He sighed. "Fine."

Their final answer was the strongest in the room.

Dr. Brooks praised the explicit function, then tapped the limitations section. "This is where calculation becomes judgment."

Nia nudged Malik with her elbow.

"Do not celebrate," he whispered.

"I am celebrating us. Different thing."

He pretended to review the equation so she would not see how much he liked the word *us*.

## Monday, August 4: Why Math Matters

On Monday, Dr. Brooks closed the classroom door and asked everyone to put away their calculators.

"Today," she said, "I want the answer nobody can compute for you. Why does mathematics matter?"

The room went quiet in the special way classrooms do when a question becomes personal.

Students spoke about engineering, medicine, money, teaching, technology, and being the first in their families to pursue a technical degree.

When Malik's turn came, he folded his hands on the desk.

"My father can repair almost anything," he said. "Engines, roofs, appliances. But one unexpected bill can undo months of planning. I want to understand risk well enough to help families and businesses prepare without pretending every loss can be prevented."

The answer came out more honest than he intended. He looked down after finishing.

Nia did not make a joke.

When her turn came, she said, "My aunt runs a neighborhood tutoring program. Every year she has to prove the same thing to different people: that kids are learning, that the program matters, that the money should continue. I want to use math to make important things harder to ignore."

After class, they walked down Eliza Moss Hall's broad staircase.

"You want to make risk visible," Nia said.

"You want to make people visible."

"That sounded like the end of a movie trailer."

"I regret saying it."

"Too late. I heard the music."

## Tuesday, August 5: The Campus Has a Memory

The campus tour began at Jubilee Library, moved through the glass-walled North Star Learning Commons, and ended beneath the live oaks along Cypress Walk.

Nia photographed everything: the copper-framed windows of Eliza Moss Hall, the old reading room inside Jubilee, the wall of whiteboards at North Star, even a campus cat sleeping under a bench as though it had tenure.

Malik photographed the map.

"You know they give us that online," Nia said.

"Phones lose signal."

"Paper maps exist."

"Paper gets wet."

"Do you prepare for joy like this too?"

He considered it. "I have not had to."

Their guide led them to Founders' Bell, greened with age and suspended beneath a limestone arch. It had once called students to assembly, she explained. Now it rang at Opening Convocation and commencement, marking the entrance and exit of a McCall-Hart education.

Nia looked up at it. "Imagine hearing that on your last day here."

Malik looked down Cypress Walk, where indigo banners shifted in the humid breeze. Four years seemed both enormous and dangerously brief.

"We have to make it to the first day first," he said.

"You really refuse to skip steps."

"Steps exist for a reason."

"So do leaps."

He glanced at her. "Leaps are just steps with poor documentation."

Nia laughed so hard the tour guide turned around.

## Wednesday, August 6: Indigo Night

Indigo Night transformed Hart Student Union into a festival of music, food, club tables, and first-year students trying out possible versions of themselves.

Nia moved through it like a spark in dry grass. She signed an interest card for the Association for Women in Mathematics, took a flyer from Math Circle, promised to visit campus radio, and nearly volunteered for a service event before Malik removed the pen from her hand.

"You cannot join the whole university tonight."

"Watch me."

"I am watching. It is concerning."

Across the terrace, an alto saxophone climbed above the crowd. A small group from the Marching Herons was performing beside the radio booth. The saxophonist played with his eyes half closed, then opened them at the exact moment Nia stepped closer.

After the set, he introduced himself as Julian Cross, second-year media arts student, campus radio host, and, according to him, "occasional rescuer of boring events."

"This event was not boring," Nia said.

"Then I can take the night off."

His confidence should have been irritating. Somehow, it was not.

Julian told her about a radio showcase on Friday and invited her to stop by. Nia said yes with the speed she used for nearly every interesting opportunity.

At another table, Malik found a sign for the Heron Analytics Challenge. Fourth-down decisions. Player performance. Ticket demand. Real datasets, competitive teams, regional presentations.

He read every line twice before writing his name.

When he and Nia met again near the Copper Cup Cafe, they both had news.

"I got invited to the radio showcase," Nia said.

"I signed up for an analytics information session."

"Look at us choosing things."

"You chose six things."

"Seven, technically."

He shook his head, but his attention drifted to Julian across the room. Julian saw Nia and lifted two fingers in a casual salute. She smiled back.

Malik did not yet have a name for the small drop inside his chest. He treated it like a number too minor to enter.

## Thursday, August 7: Accountability

Their final Bridge presentation returned to the budget model Malik had repaired, expanded into a scenario for a first-year student managing books, meals, transportation, and emergencies.

Malik explained the spreadsheet logic. Nia told the story of what happened when one assumption changed. They showed the original error, the revised categories, and a sensitivity table that made visible how quickly a small surprise could become a larger problem.

"A budget is not a promise that nothing will go wrong," Nia told the room. "It is a way to notice change early enough to make choices."

Malik advanced the slide. "And a model should show what it assumes, so the person using it knows when the answer no longer applies."

Dr. Brooks smiled from the back row.

Afterward, Malik and Nia carried their laptops to the Founders' Bell. The campus was copper in the late sun, and somewhere near the union a drumline practiced the same eight measures until they became part of the air.

"We work well together," Nia said.

"After arguing."

"During arguing. The arguing is part of the system."

"That sounds unhealthy."

"Only because you hate being challenged by someone delightful."

Malik leaned against the limestone arch. "We should keep each other on schedule when classes start. Weekly check-in. Assignments, deadlines, major commitments."

Nia held out her hand. "Accountability partners. Academic honesty, honest schedules, and emergency snack intervention."

"Define emergency."

"If you have to ask, you are already in one."

He shook her hand.

Then Nia mentioned that Julian had sent her the details for Friday's showcase.

"I think he might actually be asking me out," she said, trying and failing to sound casual.

Malik released her hand and adjusted the strap of his laptop bag.

"You should go," he said. His voice was kind, steady, and almost completely convincing. "You wanted to see the radio station."

Nia studied him for half a second, then smiled. "I did."

They walked away from the bell together, carrying two kinds of plans into the next week: the ones they had said aloud and the ones they had not yet admitted, even to themselves.

---

# Week Two: The Shape of an Invitation

By Friday afternoon, Nia Reynolds had learned that the campus radio booth was much smaller than it sounded.

On air, Julian Cross's voice seemed to occupy its own weather system: warm, smooth, and confident enough to convince listeners that he had never once walked into the wrong classroom. In person, the booth on the second floor of Hart Student Union was a narrow room with two microphones, three chairs, a control board covered in glowing buttons, and a handwritten sign that said **DO NOT SPIN IN THE GOOD CHAIR**.

Julian was spinning in the good chair.

"You came," he said when Nia appeared behind the glass.

"You invited me."

"People say yes to things all the time. Showing up is the impressive part."

She liked that answer. She also suspected he knew she would.

The radio showcase gathered new students around the Media House and union booths for short demonstrations. Julian's segment was called *First Week Frequencies*, a mix of music, interviews, and confessions collected from students who were excited, lost, homesick, overdressed, underprepared, or some combination of all five.

Nia stood at the back while Julian leaned toward the microphone.

"Today's public-service announcement," he said, "is for every first-year who looked at a campus map, calculated the shortest distance between two buildings, and still arrived late because McCall-Hart added a staircase as a character-building exercise."

Nia laughed.

Then Julian continued. "In mathematical terms, the domain is the campus, the range is wherever you accidentally end up, and the function is confusion."

Nia stopped laughing.

When the music came up, she tapped on the glass.

Julian opened the door. "Good confusion joke?"

"That is not how domain and range work."

He put a hand over his heart. "I have wounded mathematics."

"Publicly."

"Can it be saved?"

Nia stepped into the booth. "Move over."

## Friday, August 8: Fact-Checking the Flirt

During the next break, Nia rewrote Julian's joke on the back of a station schedule.

"The domain could be all the starting locations students choose," she explained. "The function maps each starting point to the place they reach after following your directions. The range is the set of places they actually end up."

Julian stared at the paper. "That is both more correct and less funny."

"Accuracy has a price."

"What if the function maps every student to the wrong building?"

"Then your directions are consistent, which is somehow worse."

He grinned. "There it is. That's the joke."

When the microphone went live again, Julian introduced her as "Nia Reynolds, first-year mathematical sciences major and defender of functions everywhere."

She corrected the explanation in twenty seconds, added the line about consistently bad directions, and earned a burst of laughter from the students outside the booth.

Julian watched her with open admiration.

Afterward, he walked her to the terrace. "You make ideas sound like stories."

"Ideas are stories. They have relationships, rules, and consequences."

"I might need you to fact-check the whole show."

"That sounds suspiciously like unpaid labor."

"Then I will pay in coffee."

"Copper Cup coffee?"

"I respect professionals."

Nia tried not to smile too widely and failed.

## Saturday, August 9: The Test Before the Team

While Nia replayed the radio clip more times than she planned to admit, Malik sat in Porter Center for Enterprise learning that signing an interest sheet did not make him a member of Heron Analytics.

Dr. Isaac Bennett stood beside a screen displaying a football field overlaid with numbers. He was a statistics professor, actuarial adviser, and the kind of speaker who could turn a room quiet without raising his voice.

"Sports analytics is not decorating opinions with decimals," he told the group. "A model has to help someone make a decision under uncertainty. If your answer only works after the game is over, you have built a very expensive replay."

Malik sat straighter.

The competitive team studied questions such as fourth-down choices, fatigue, roster use, and attendance. Experienced students would form the core squad, but first-years could earn developmental positions through a screening test and a short explanation exercise.

Then Simone Hart took the front of the room.

She was a second-year data science and finance major with a copper clip holding back her braids and a look that suggested she had already found three flaws in everyone's assumptions.

"The screening is not a trivia contest," she said. "We need people who can calculate, interpret, and revise. If you love your first answer more than you love the evidence, this team will be uncomfortable for you."

Malik wrote that down, then immediately wondered whether writing it down was evidence that it applied to him.

After the session, he studied the sample problem taped near the exit. Simone stopped beside him.

"You have been looking at that table for four minutes," she said.

"Three and a half."

"That correction tells me more than you intended."

"The average gain is hiding the distribution."

"Good. What else?"

"Small sample. Different opponents. Unknown field position."

Simone nodded once. "Screening is the Wednesday after move-in. Bring that suspicion with you. Leave room for other people to be right."

Malik watched her go. He was not sure whether he had been encouraged or warned.

## Sunday, August 10: Who Counts

The Bridge class returned to domain and range on Sunday with a scenario about a tutoring center that assigned one appointment time to each student request.

Malik defined the domain as submitted requests and the range as available appointment slots. His answer was fast, clean, and incomplete.

Nia circled the phrase *submitted requests*.

"What about students who need tutoring but cannot use the request form?"

"They are not in the domain."

"That is my point."

"It is the domain described by the problem."

"And if we report that the center served ninety percent of its domain, it sounds excellent. But maybe the form excludes students with unreliable internet, screen-reader problems, or schedules outside the listed times."

Malik rubbed his forehead. "You keep adding people the problem does not mention."

"Real life keeps doing that too."

The sentence landed harder than she meant it to.

Malik closed his notebook. "A model has boundaries. That does not make the model cruel."

"I didn't call it cruel."

"You looked at me like I was removing people from campus."

Nia crossed her arms. "And you looked at me like context was an annoying hobby."

For the rest of the exercise, their table became painfully quiet.

Dr. Brooks eventually stopped beside them. She looked at Malik's formal definition and Nia's notes in the margin.

"Both of you are protecting something useful," she said. "Malik is protecting a precise claim. Nia is protecting the difference between the observed population and the population we care about. Can your answer do both?"

Neither spoke immediately.

Then Malik reopened his notebook. "We can define the given domain and add a limitation about unobserved demand."

Nia uncrossed her arms. "And recommend more ways to request help."

"As a next step, not part of the current function."

"Fine."

They revised the answer.

Outside class, Nia bumped his shoulder. "For the record, I do not think you are cruel."

"For the record, I do not think context is a hobby."

"You think it is an aggressive side quest."

"Sometimes."

That was close enough to peace.

## Monday, August 11: Saying Yes Too Fast

Julian found Nia outside Jubilee Library on Monday with a saxophone case on his back and two cold drinks in his hands.

"One is coffee," he said. "One is a science experiment pretending to be tea. Choose wisely."

She chose the tea.

"Marching Herons rehearse tonight," he said. "You should come."

"Tonight?"

"That is generally when tonight happens."

Nia checked neither her planner nor the Bridge assignment waiting in her bag.

"Yes," she said.

Julian's smile made the decision feel excellent for almost six minutes.

Then her phone reminded her about a residence-life meeting. Another notification announced a Math Circle interest session. Malik had also sent the time for their accountability check-in.

She stared at the screen.

"Problem?" Julian asked.

"No. Just a very popular evening."

"You can say no."

"I know."

But Nia had spent years believing that good opportunities were doors that might lock if she did not sprint through them. By dinner she had moved the residence meeting, skipped Math Circle, and asked Malik to push their check-in later.

His reply came quickly: **Okay. Send a time you can actually keep.**

The message was reasonable. Somehow, it irritated her.

She typed three different responses before sending: **9:30. I will keep it.**

## Tuesday, August 12: Absolutely Not About Nia

Malik met his future roommate, Darius "DJ" Cole, during a residence-hall orientation on Tuesday.

DJ wore a McCall-Hart football training shirt, carried no pen, and borrowed Malik's before the first slide ended.

"You look like a man who travels with backup ink," DJ whispered.

Malik handed him a second pen.

"I knew it. We are going to thrive."

They discovered they had been assigned the same room. DJ immediately proposed putting a television on the largest wall. Malik immediately explained that the largest wall offered the best placement for two desks.

"We have not moved in and you are already taking square footage from joy," DJ said.

"A desk supports multiple activities."

"Not Madden."

After orientation, they ate at the union. Nia passed the table, waved, and hurried toward the Media House.

DJ watched Malik watch her.

"Who is that?"

"My accountability partner."

"That phrase has never sounded less academic."

"We met in Bridge. We work well together."

"You like her."

"I am interested in the Heron Analytics Challenge."

DJ blinked. "I asked about a person."

"And I answered with the thing I am interested in."

"That is the verbal version of leaving through a bathroom window."

Malik took back the spare pen. "I need this."

"Truth tax?"

"You have your own pen now."

## Wednesday, August 13: Under the Sound

Marching-band rehearsal did not resemble the loose jam session Nia had imagined.

It was repetition under pressure. Musicians reset a section again and again while student leaders corrected timing, spacing, posture, and breath. The August air stayed thick even after sunset. Julian's shirt darkened with sweat, but each time the altos returned to the phrase, his sound came out clear.

Nia sat high in the empty side of Henry Boyd Stadium, watching discipline transform into spectacle one measured piece at a time.

When rehearsal ended, Julian climbed the steps and dropped beside her.

"Still think I just wander around being charming?" he asked between breaths.

"I did not say *just*."

He laughed. "Fair."

They shared fries from a paper tray while the stadium lights clicked off section by section.

Julian told her that media arts had given a name to something he had always done: notice what changed when a story was framed one way instead of another. Campus radio let him ask questions. Documentary work let him keep the answers.

"And band?" Nia asked.

He looked toward the field. "Band is where nobody cares how naturally talented you think you are. Either you hit the mark with everybody else or the whole shape bends."

That answer made her see him differently. Beneath the easy smile was someone who practiced until the public moment looked effortless.

"Why math?" he asked.

Nia told him about her aunt's tutoring program and the reports that determined whether it received support. She told him she wanted to make evidence understandable without stripping away the people inside it.

"So you want numbers to testify," Julian said.

"Exactly."

He tapped his fry against hers like a toast. "I knew you were good radio."

When he walked her back toward the residence hall, their shoulders touched twice. Neither apologized.

## Thursday, August 14: Range and Limits

Nia met Malik at North Star Learning Commons the next morning, nine minutes late and still smiling about the night before.

"It felt like a date," she said before sitting down.

Malik's pencil stopped.

He could have asked whether Julian had called it a date. He could have pointed out that walking someone home and sharing fries did not constitute formal evidence. He could have done several things that would have made Nia's happiness smaller.

Instead he said, "Did you have a good time?"

"A really good time. He is different when he is working. Focused. Not fake-focused for two minutes, either."

"Then it sounds like you learned something useful."

She watched him. "You okay?"

"Yes."

It was not exactly a lie. He was healthy. Seated. Fully conscious.

Nia opened her notebook. "We can talk about something else."

"We should talk about your schedule. You moved three commitments for one invitation."

"I handled it."

"You skipped the interest session you said mattered to you."

Her smile faded. "I know what I skipped."

Malik heard the edge in her voice and stopped. This was not the moment to turn disappointment into authority.

"You're right," he said. "It is your schedule."

They completed their check-in, but the conversation never fully recovered.

That evening, Malik stayed at North Star after the tables emptied. The analytics screening test was less than a week away. He worked through probability questions, interpreting every mistake as evidence that somebody else wanted the position more.

Across campus, Nia sat on her bed and added Julian's rehearsal to her planner in copper ink. Then she moved her missed Math Circle session to a page titled **Find Another Way In**.

Two invitations had taken shape that week. One opened toward romance; the other toward competition. Both looked exciting from the outside. Neither had yet revealed what it would cost.

---

# Week Three: Rooms, Rules, and First Impressions

Malik had drawn the room before he moved into it.

Not artistically. He had used the residence hall's posted dimensions to make a scale diagram, then tested three furniture arrangements. Layout B gave each roommate equal desk access, kept the vent unobstructed, and created a clear walking path from the door to both beds.

Layout B survived for eleven seconds.

"Television goes here," DJ announced, setting a large box directly in the planned walking path.

Behind him came two duffel bags, a laundry basket, a foam roller, three pairs of football cleats, and an auntie carrying enough snacks to provision the floor through midterms.

Malik looked from the television to his diagram.

DJ looked at the diagram. "Is that our room?"

"It was."

"Why does it look like a tiny courthouse?"

"It is to scale."

"Where is the joy to scale?"

DJ's auntie laughed, handed Malik a container of lemon cookies, and told him he was now responsible for making sure her nephew attended class. Before Malik could object, she hugged both of them and joined the stream of families filling the hall.

DJ picked up a cookie. "Congratulations. You have been hired."

"I did not accept."

"Family appointment. Very powerful."

Malik moved the television box six inches so the door could close.

Early move-in for Bridge students and athletes had begun. The rest of the first-year class would arrive Monday.

## Friday, August 15: Layout B

By noon, Malik understood that sharing a room meant surrendering the fantasy that every object would remain where logic placed it.

DJ's football schedule appeared on the wall above his bed. His alarm sounded at 5:15 each morning, including Saturdays. He needed space to review film, stretch, and store team gear. He also received visitors with the casual frequency of a campaign office.

Malik needed quiet, a reliable sleep schedule, and surfaces that did not become permanent clothing habitats.

They negotiated.

"Headphones after eleven," Malik said.

"Unless it is game film."

"Game film also produces sound."

"Educational sound."

"Headphones."

DJ pointed to the empty section of floor in Malik's diagram. "Television stand here. Both desks on that wall."

"That blocks part of the window."

"Sunlight has had the planet for billions of years. It can share."

They eventually built Layout D, a compromise that existed nowhere in Malik's original file. The desks sat side by side, the television remained visible from both beds, and a narrow strip near the window became DJ's stretching area.

Malik hated how well it worked.

That evening, DJ returned from a team meeting and found labels on two shelves.

"Did you assign me storage?"

"I assigned both of us storage."

"Malik, my brother, we are going to get you through college."

"That is why I came."

DJ grinned. "See? Dry jokes. I knew you had range."

## Saturday, August 16: The Six-Club Emergency

Across campus, Nia's new roommate was saving her from herself.

Keisha Morgan arrived with one suitcase, a desktop computer packed like medical equipment, and a wall calendar showing class blocks, track practices, coding time, and exactly two blank evenings each week.

Nia arrived with four bags, a plant named Katherine Johnson, and a stack of organization flyers collected at Indigo Night.

"How many of those did you join?" Keisha asked.

"Join is a strong word."

"How many gave you a signup confirmation?"

Nia counted. "Six."

Keisha held out her hand. "Phone."

"Why?"

"Because this is an intervention."

Keisha was a first-year computer science major and sprinter from Mobile. She spoke with the calm efficiency of someone who knew exactly how long 200 meters felt at full speed and had no interest in wasting energy elsewhere.

Together they reviewed Nia's list: Association for Women in Mathematics, Math Circle, service council, campus radio volunteers, residence programming board, and a general first-year leadership group.

"You also have Bridge, classes starting soon, and whatever is happening with Saxophone Man," Keisha said.

"His name is Julian."

"That sounds ongoing."

Nia moved campus radio into the maybe column. "I just do not want to miss the thing that could matter."

Keisha sat on the edge of her bed. "Everything matters to somebody. That does not make all of it yours."

The sentence quieted the room.

Nia crossed out two commitments and postponed a third. It felt less like freedom than failure.

Keisha handed back the phone. "You did not close doors. You chose which room to stand in first."

"Do you always talk like a very fast life coach?"

"Only during emergencies."

Nia looked at the reduced list. "Six clubs counts as an emergency?"

"Six clubs before classes absolutely counts."

## Sunday, August 17: The Missing Riders

The Bridge capstone began with a problem that looked made for Malik.

Move-in traffic had increased wait times for McCall-Hart's campus shuttles. Student teams had to estimate demand and recommend temporary routes. The packet included residence-hall counts, parking information, shuttle capacity, and scheduled arrival windows.

Malik built a demand table. Nia sketched the campus. They had practiced combining those methods, and for the first hour they moved with satisfying speed.

Their model sent extra shuttle service to the largest residence halls during the busiest arrival windows. It minimized average wait time and stayed within the available driver hours.

"We are good," Malik said.

Nia traced the route with a copper marker. "We are suspiciously good."

During the preliminary review, another Bridge student named Tasha studied their map.

"Where is the accessible stop for Douglass Hall?" she asked.

Malik pointed to the nearest marked stop.

"That path has stairs."

He looked at the map again.

Tasha used a forearm crutch. She tapped the western side of the building. "The ramp entrance is back here. Your shuttle drops people on the opposite side."

Nia lowered the marker.

Their optimization had treated every route between a stop and a residence hall as equally usable. The average wait looked excellent because the model did not count the extra distance, slope, or barriers faced by some students.

"We did it again," Nia said quietly.

Malik knew what she meant: the food boxes, the tutoring requests, the habit of letting missing context hide outside clean columns.

"The packet did not include accessibility data," he said.

Tasha's expression changed.

Nia looked at Malik. "Then we should have asked."

He heard accusation, even though she was accusing both of them.

"We modeled the information provided."

"And called ourselves done."

Malik closed the laptop harder than necessary. "If you knew the map was incomplete, you could have said so before review."

"I didn't know either. That is the point."

They had built the best-looking first draft in the room. It was also the first draft that embarrassed them most.

## Monday, August 18: Ask Before Defending

Residence halls officially opened Monday, bringing vans, trailers, grandparents, coolers, lamps, rugs, and several impossible attempts to fit queen-size mattresses into twin-size spaces.

Malik and Nia spent the busiest part of move-in outside with clipboards.

Dr. Brooks had given every team a choice: defend the assumptions in the original model or collect better information.

For once, Malik did not need long to decide.

They interviewed students, family members, drivers, and residence staff. They timed routes instead of estimating from the map. Tasha showed them the accessible entrance and identified two curb cuts that were technically present but difficult to use when unloading vehicles blocked them.

A parent pushing a cart explained that the nearest stop was not always the easiest stop. A student carrying an instrument could walk farther but boarded more slowly. A residence assistant pointed out that demand surged after families returned from shopping, not only during assigned arrival windows.

The problem became messier with every answer.

It also became real.

Back in Eliza Moss Hall, Malik added route penalties for stairs, steep slopes, and blocked access. Nia revised the campus diagram to distinguish shortest distance from usable distance. They recommended a temporary west-side stop, an accessible shuttle loop, and two flexible driver periods based on observed demand.

"Average wait goes up in our revised model," Malik said.

"Because we are counting more of the actual waiting."

He nodded. "The first number was better because it was less honest."

Nia looked at him. "Can I put that in the presentation?"

"No."

"Can I put a more professional version?"

"Yes."

She wrote: **A lower reported average can conceal unmeasured access costs.**

Malik read it twice. "That is better."

"We are better," she said.

This time, he let her celebrate.

## Tuesday, August 19: Through Julian's Lens

Julian arrived at their capstone rehearsal carrying a camera and an apology.

"Campus media wants a short Bridge feature," he said. "Dr. Brooks said I could film if the team agrees. If anybody says no, the camera stays in the bag."

Malik appreciated the clarity. Nia appreciated the camera angle Julian found near the route map.

During the interview, Julian asked why their model had changed.

Nia began with the missing accessibility information, but she spoke so quickly that the answer tangled itself.

Malik stepped in. "Our first model optimized the experience we had measured. We mistook that for everyone's experience. Interviews changed the variables and the recommendation."

Julian lowered the camera slightly. "That is the line. Say it again."

Malik did, less comfortably the second time.

After filming, Julian asked Nia if she wanted to attend the Heritage Kickoff with him after the first week of classes. This time, she checked her planner.

He noticed. "Should I be nervous?"

"No. This is personal growth."

She had one evening open.

"Yes," she said. "I would like that."

Julian smiled, and the room seemed to brighten around him.

Malik pretended to adjust a chart that was already straight.

## Wednesday, August 20: One Bad Question

The Heron Analytics screening test included data interpretation, probability, short calculations, and one written response about model limitations.

Malik moved confidently through the first section. He checked his units. He marked assumptions. He even forced himself to leave one question and return later instead of wrestling it into submission.

Then he reached the probability problem.

A team could choose between two fourth-down strategies under changing field conditions. Malik calculated the expected outcomes, reconsidered whether the events were independent, erased half a page, and watched twelve minutes disappear.

When time was called, his final answer sat in a box without the explanation he had meant to add.

Outside Porter Center, Simone leaned against the railing.

"How bad?" she asked.

"One probability problem."

"That was not my question."

"I may have treated dependent events as independent, corrected it, then failed to justify the correction."

"How was the rest?"

"Irrelevant if the cutoff is high."

Simone shook her head. "You are doing that thing."

"What thing?"

"Turning incomplete information into the worst available conclusion, then calling it realism."

Malik looked back through the glass doors as if the test might still be visible.

"Results come next week," she said. "Until then, you do not know."

"Uncertainty is not comforting."

"It is not supposed to be. It is supposed to be accurate."

He wanted to dislike her. Instead he stored the sentence beside all the other useful things people had said that week.

## Thursday, August 21: The First Bell

Opening Convocation began under a sky the color of washed denim.

First-year students gathered at the end of Cypress Walk in academic dress clothes, fresh MHU shirts, polished shoes, and sneakers chosen by people who had correctly predicted the amount of standing involved.

Malik found DJ near the live oaks, answering messages from teammates. Nia arrived with Keisha, who had convinced her not to carry three organization folders to a ceremony. Julian stood farther ahead with campus media, filming the procession.

The four first-years joined the crowd.

DJ leaned toward Malik. "This is a lot of symbolism before breakfast."

"It is ten thirty."

"Athlete breakfast rules. I have lived two days already."

Keisha looked at Nia. "Your people are strange."

"They are growing on me."

At the signal, the Founders' Bell rang.

The sound rolled over Cypress Walk, deeper than Malik expected. Conversations stopped. Phones lowered. For a few seconds, thousands of individual worries shared one silence.

They began walking.

Nia looked toward Eliza Moss Hall, where she and Malik had met over a red number and an empty chair. DJ pointed out an upperclassman receiver he hoped to learn from. Keisha counted how many minutes remained before track paperwork. Malik noticed all of it: the different ambitions moving together, not yet coordinated but no longer separate.

At the midpoint of the walk, Julian stepped backward with his camera raised.

"First-years," he called. "Look like you know where you are going."

"Historically inaccurate," Malik said.

Nia laughed just as Julian took the picture.

The image caught them in motion: DJ pointing, Keisha half smiling, Nia turned toward Malik, Malik looking more hopeful than he felt. Behind them, indigo banners lifted in the breeze.

Classes would begin next. Julian's invitation had become a real date. Malik's analytics decision remained beyond his control. Roommate rules would be tested, schedules would collide, and the mathematics would stop waiting for them to feel ready.

But on Cypress Walk, beneath the fading echo of the bell, readiness seemed less important than movement.

They kept walking.

---

# Week Four: The First Limit

On the first morning of Calculus I, Dr. Camille Brooks drew a graph that looked like it was lying.

The curve traveled smoothly toward an open circle at a height of four. Directly above that empty point, at the same horizontal position, sat a solid dot at seven.

Dr. Brooks placed the chalk on the tray and faced the class.

"What is the answer?"

Malik's hand rose before the final word stopped echoing.

"Seven," he said. "The filled point gives the function value."

"Correct," Dr. Brooks said.

Malik lowered his hand.

"And incomplete."

His hand stopped halfway to the desk.

Around him, the room shifted into attention. Eliza Moss Hall had traded its Bridge-program looseness for the official energy of a first college course. New notebooks lay open. Laptops glowed. Everybody had chosen a seat that might become theirs for the semester.

Nia sat beside Malik, turning a copper pen between her fingers. Two rows ahead, a quiet student named Amara Lewis studied the graph without looking up.

Dr. Brooks circled the empty point. "The function's value is seven. But what value does the graph approach?"

"Four," Nia said.

"Also correct. So which answer tells us what the function is doing?"

Malik looked from the solid dot to the open circle. He disliked questions that allowed two correct answers to stand in the same room without fighting.

Dr. Brooks smiled as if she could see the discomfort crossing his face.

"Welcome to limits," she said.

## Friday, August 22: The Answer Beside the Answer

Dr. Brooks did not begin with notation. She began with approach.

She traced the graph from the left, then from the right. Students described what the outputs did as the input moved closer to the marked value. The function could be defined at seven, undefined entirely, or assigned almost anything at that single point without changing the value the surrounding graph approached.

"A limit asks about behavior nearby," Dr. Brooks said. "Not necessarily what happens at the destination itself."

Nia leaned toward Malik. "Math has trust issues."

"Math has definitions."

"Same thing with better symbols."

Malik copied the formal notation, but his attention remained on the graph. In most of his work, arriving at the answer ended the problem. Here, the course was asking him to study what happened before arrival, to separate a visible point from the pattern surrounding it.

At the end of class, Dr. Brooks assigned a short reflection: explain the difference between a function value and a limit to someone who had never taken calculus.

Malik wrote three precise sentences.

Nia drew a bus approaching a stop, then added a passenger who appeared at a completely different location.

"That is absurd," Malik said.

"That is why people will remember it."

"The passenger represents the function value?"

"Exactly. The bus approaches one place. Somebody drops the passenger somewhere weird. Related location, different story."

Malik stared at the drawing. "Your bus metaphor is mathematically reckless."

"But you understood it."

He hated when she won with evidence.

## Saturday, August 23: Too Ready to Speak

Saturday's review workshop was crowded with students still deciding whether Calculus I belonged on their schedules. Add-drop week had made every seat temporary.

Dr. Brooks displayed three graphs and asked the class to compare their left-hand and right-hand behavior. When silence lasted more than two seconds, Nia filled it.

"On the first graph, both sides approach three, so the limit is three even though the point is missing. On the second, the left side approaches negative one and the right side approaches two, so the two-sided limit does not exist. And on the third--"

"Pause," Dr. Brooks said gently.

Nia stopped.

Amara's hand was halfway raised in the row ahead.

"Amara," Dr. Brooks said, "would you take the third?"

Amara explained that the graph grew without bound from one side. Her voice was soft, but her reasoning was exact. She named the one-sided behavior and carefully avoided calling infinity a function value.

"Well done," Dr. Brooks said.

Nia sank lower in her chair.

Afterward, she caught Amara near the stairs.

"I am sorry," Nia said. "I saw your hand and kept talking anyway."

Amara adjusted the strap of her bag. "You probably did not mean to."

"I didn't. But I still did it."

Amara's expression softened. "Sometimes I take longer to decide how I want to say something. By the time I am ready, the class has moved on."

Nia nodded. "Next time, I can leave some air in the room."

"You do not have to stop answering."

"Good, because that might require medical supervision."

Amara laughed.

When Nia told Keisha about it later, she expected reassurance. Keisha was lacing her running shoes and offered none.

"You apologized?"

"Yes."

"You know what to do differently?"

"Yes."

"Then do that. Guilt is not community service."

Nia threw a pillow at her. Keisha caught it without looking.

## Sunday, August 24: Alternate

The Heron Analytics email arrived at 3:17 Sunday afternoon.

Malik read the subject line twice: **Fall Team Selection Decision**.

DJ sat on his bed reviewing formations on the television. "Open it before your face cracks."

Malik clicked.

The message praised his screening performance, especially his interpretation and written discussion of model limits. Due to roster capacity and his limited experience, he had been selected as an alternate. He could attend open reviews, complete practice assignments, and step into a trial if a position became available.

Alternate.

Not rejected. Not chosen.

The word occupied the most frustrating possible location between two outcomes.

"Well?" DJ asked.

"Alternate."

"Is that good?"

"It is what I expected."

DJ paused the film. "That sentence did not answer me."

"It is a reasonable placement for a first-year."

"Still not an answer."

Malik closed the laptop. "I can attend reviews."

"And you wanted the team."

"I wanted access to the work. I have access."

He repeated the same version to Nia, to his parents, and later to Dr. Bennett when he confirmed the alternate position. Each repetition became smoother.

Only Simone refused to let it pass.

"You can be disappointed and still accept the role," she told him outside Porter Center. "Those facts are not mutually exclusive."

"I am not disappointed."

"Then you are doing an incredible impression of somebody swallowing a stapler."

Malik looked away.

Simone did not press. "Open review is Monday evening. Alternates are allowed to contribute. Contribution is not the same as auditioning every second you are in the room."

He heard the warning. He immediately began planning an audition.

## Monday, August 25: Useful Work

Fall classes officially began Monday, turning McCall-Hart's pathways into fast-moving rivers of students.

DJ returned from practice with two printouts and dropped them on Malik's desk.

"I need a second brain."

"What happened to the first one?"

"It has been awake since five."

The printouts showed two sets of play-call results from practice. One suggested a short route worked best from a certain formation. The other suggested it failed more often than it succeeded.

"Coach wants us to learn tendencies," DJ said. "These tables look like they are arguing."

Malik compared the labels. The first dataset included all practice repetitions. The second included only plays against the starting defense.

"Different domains," he said.

"In human language?"

"The first tells you what happened against everyone. The second tells you what happened against the strongest unit. Neither is wrong. They answer different questions."

DJ sat beside him. "So if I am trying to earn game snaps, which one matters?"

"Both. The full set shows whether the play is generally reliable. The starter set shows where the defense exposes it. We should compare the route depth and coverage too."

They worked until Malik had a small chart separating opponent unit, coverage type, and result. DJ carried it to his position meeting.

That evening, he texted: **Coach said this was useful. Do not become unbearable.**

Malik saved the message.

At analytics review, he did not lead with a grand model. He showed Simone the same comparison and explained what the data could and could not support.

"Useful," she said.

The word should have felt smaller than *selected*. It did not.

## Tuesday, August 26: Define the Date

Nia met Julian at Copper Cup Cafe between classes. He had saved her a seat beside the window and ordered the iced tea she had chosen during their first coffee negotiation.

"You remembered," she said.

"I conduct interviews. Details are the whole job."

"That answer almost ruined the sweetness."

"Almost?"

"You remain under review."

They talked about first classes. Julian's documentary course had assigned a one-minute portrait without dialogue. Nia described limits with enough enthusiasm to make the people at the next table glance over.

"So the graph can be going toward one answer while the actual point is somewhere else," Julian said.

"Yes."

"That sounds emotionally dangerous."

"Calculus is full of warnings."

Julian stirred his coffee. "Speaking of clarity, I want to make sure we are describing Thursday the same way. Heritage Kickoff. You and me. A date."

Nia's smile arrived before she could manage it. "An official date?"

"Unless there is paperwork I missed."

"No paperwork. I might make a rubric."

"I am suddenly less confident."

"Good. Healthy uncertainty."

They agreed to meet outside the union after Nia finished her homework set. She entered it in her planner while Julian watched.

"Personal growth again?" he asked.

"I am becoming a woman who checks her calendar before creating problems for herself."

"I support her."

Nia liked the ease between them. She liked even more that he had called the date what it was.

## Wednesday, August 27: Leave Some Air

The group limit problem began with four students and one piecewise graph.

Malik handled the algebraic simplification. Nia explained the left-hand behavior. When the group reached the right-hand limit, she looked at Amara.

"Do you want this part?"

Amara studied the expression. "Give me a second."

Nia gave her one.

Then another.

The silence felt much longer than it was. Nia resisted the itch to rescue it.

Amara pointed to the denominator. "We cannot substitute yet, but after factoring, the behavior from the right is positive. The magnitude grows because the denominator approaches zero."

Malik leaned closer. "So the one-sided limit is positive infinity, while the left is negative infinity."

"Which means the two-sided limit does not exist," Amara finished.

Their argument was complete.

When the groups presented, Malik surprised Nia by saying, "Amara should explain the final step. She caught the sign change."

Amara did.

Outside class, Nia bumped Malik's arm. "Look at you sharing airtime."

"She had the strongest explanation."

"You could let this be growth."

"I am reporting evidence."

"Growth with a clipboard. Still growth."

## Thursday, August 28: What Malik Does Not Say

Nia emerged from the residence hall wearing copper earrings, an indigo top, and the expression of someone trying not to look like she had thought carefully about looking effortless.

Malik was crossing the courtyard on his way to Porter Center when he saw her.

"Too much?" she asked, turning once.

"For an outdoor event in August? Possibly."

"I meant the outfit."

He looked at her properly. "You look nice."

"Nice?"

"Very nice."

"Better."

Julian approached from Cypress Walk with his saxophone case over one shoulder. He had changed out of rehearsal clothes and carried two event passes.

"Ready?" he asked Nia.

She looked back at Malik. "Good luck at analytics review."

"Have fun."

He meant it. That was the irritating part.

Nia and Julian walked away together, their conversation easy before they reached the path. Malik stood still until DJ called from an upstairs window.

"My brother! That is what feelings look like when you refuse to process them!"

Malik looked up. "Close the window."

At Porter Center, he opened a blank project document and created a work plan for his alternate period: opponent tendency tables, fourth-down conversions, field-position splits, weather variables, player availability, model comparison, and a dashboard no one had requested.

Simone read the list over his shoulder.

"This is six weeks of work," she said.

"I can finish a first version in ten days."

"Why?"

"To contribute."

She studied him. "You are allowed to have a life outside proving you should be in this room."

Malik added a deadline beside the dashboard.

The first week of calculus had taught him that the value at a point and the behavior around it could disagree. Officially, he was an alternate. Nearby, he was working like a starter. Nia was his accountability partner. Nearby, she was walking into a new relationship.

Malik understood the mathematics.

Understanding what to do with it was another problem.

---

# Week Five: Kickoff

The first college homework deadline arrived wearing football-weekend colors.

By Friday morning, indigo banners hung from the balconies of Hart Student Union. Copper herons appeared in shop windows across Bellwether. Alumni tents rose along Cypress Walk, and the Marching Herons rehearsed often enough that drumbeats seemed to travel through the sidewalks.

Inside North Star Learning Commons, Malik and Nia stared at Problem Twelve.

"The graph says the limit is negative infinity," Nia said.

"The graph suggests it. We still have to justify the sign."

"The function falls off the bottom of the page like it owes somebody money."

"That is not justification."

"It is visual evidence with personality."

Malik pointed to the denominator. "Approaching from the left makes this factor negative and extremely small. The numerator stays positive. Positive divided by a small negative number gives a large negative output."

Nia wrote his explanation beneath her sketch. "See? Teamwork. I provide vision. You provide bail money."

Their first limit set was due before the holiday weekend. Both had other places to be: Nia had plans with Julian around the Heritage Kickoff Classic, and Malik had an analytics data review that alternates were technically invited to observe.

Still, neither packed up when the last problem was finished.

Outside, the campus was warming into celebration. Inside, they checked each other's work one unnecessary time.

## Friday, August 29: The Hour They Do Not Name

Add-drop ended Friday, making everyone's schedule feel less like a proposal and more like a contract.

Nia submitted the limit set at 2:06. Malik submitted at 2:08 after correcting a comma in his written explanation.

"We are free," Nia said.

"We have reading for Tuesday."

"You are spiritually exhausting."

They carried their bags to the terrace outside North Star. Students crossed Cypress Walk in game shirts and sundresses, greeting relatives who had returned for the weekend. The air smelled like food trucks, cut grass, and rain that had not yet decided whether to fall.

Nia's phone lit up with a message from Julian: **Band call changed. Meet tomorrow at the stadium gate? I owe you the full tour.**

She smiled, then turned the screen facedown.

"Analytics?" she asked.

"Data review at four."

"Are you presenting the enormous work plan Simone told you not to build?"

Malik looked offended. "She did not tell me not to build it."

"She called it six weeks of work."

"That was an estimate."

"Malik."

"I reduced the scope."

"To five weeks?"

He almost laughed.

For one quiet hour, they sat on the terrace and talked about everything except where they were expected next. Nia described Keisha's first track conditioning session. Malik repeated DJ's complaint that special-teams meetings involved "a disrespectful quantity of sprinting." They debated which Copper Cup pastry was least likely to collapse in a backpack.

Neither said, *I wanted this afternoon with you.*

At three thirty, Nia stood. "We should go."

"Yes."

Neither moved for another minute.

## Saturday, August 30: Waiting for the Moment

Bellwether Civic Stadium, the neutral-site venue for the Heritage Kickoff Classic, filled hours before kickoff.

Indigo covered the stands. Copper flashed in pom-poms and band plumes. Alumni hugged across rows, children wore oversized heron wings, and the public-address announcer stretched every name until it sounded historic.

Julian met Nia at the gate in his Marching Herons uniform. He had ten minutes before report time and used nine of them explaining where she should sit, when the band would move, and which cues would be easy to miss.

"Watch the drum major after third down," he said. "If the defense gets a stop, we go immediately. If the offense converts, we hold."

"So the music is conditional."

"Everything is conditional."

"That is the most mathematical thing you have said voluntarily."

Julian lowered his voice. "Also, when the altos turn toward your section, I am absolutely playing to you."

"How will I know which one you are?"

He looked wounded. "Copper wrist tape. Handsome posture."

"That narrows it to at least twelve people."

He laughed and hurried toward the tunnel.

On the sideline, DJ waited.

He was dressed, taped, warmed up, and ready for a special-teams call that never came. Each time the return unit gathered, he moved closer to the coordinator. Each time, another name was called.

From the student section, Malik watched DJ bounce on his toes to stay loose. The crowd saw a player standing near the bench. Malik saw the 5:15 alarms, the route notes, the foam roller beneath the window, and the chart taped above DJ's desk.

McCall-Hart won by ten.

The stadium erupted. The band played. Nia screamed until her voice cracked and found Julian's copper wrist tape when the altos turned toward her section.

DJ celebrated with the team, helmet raised, smile visible from the stands.

Back in their room, he set the helmet on his desk and sat without turning on the light.

"You okay?" Malik asked.

"We won."

"That was not my question."

DJ gave him a tired look. "You have been waiting to use that on me."

"A little."

DJ leaned back. "I was ready. Nobody needed me to be."

Malik understood that feeling too well.

"They will," he said.

"You know that?"

"No."

DJ nodded. The honest answer helped more.

## Sunday, August 31: The Tendency

Sunday's analytics review opened with game data and closed, according to the agenda, after ninety minutes.

It did not close after ninety minutes.

The team was already preparing for the next opponent. Simone assigned sections of film and data while Malik sat at the end of the table with the other alternate, listening harder than some people spoke.

One chart showed fourth-down attempts by field position. Another separated play direction. A third listed personnel groupings.

Malik noticed that the opponent's apparent aggression near midfield changed when its tight-end-heavy package entered the game. On fourth-and-short, that package strongly predicted a run to the wide side, but the tendency disappeared inside the red zone.

He checked the sample size. Small, but not useless. He checked two seasons to see whether the pattern survived. It weakened but remained visible.

The room had emptied before he finished.

Malik drafted a message to Simone, deleted it, rebuilt the chart, and drafted again.

**Possible tendency, not a rule. On fourth-and-2 or less between the opponent 40 and our 35, heavy personnel has preceded a wide-side run in 8 of 10 available cases. Two clips have missing formation labels. I would use this as a film-review flag, not a standalone prediction.**

He attached the chart and sent it.

Three dots appeared almost immediately.

**Good limitation note. I will check it with Coach Bennett and football staff. Sleep.**

Malik looked at the time. 1:14 a.m.

He closed the laptop.

Then he reopened it to make sure the attachment had been correct.

## Monday, September 1: A Quiet Kind of Official

Labor Day closed the university, but nobody had informed the Marching Herons, the football staff, or the students who considered a free day an invitation to reorganize their entire lives.

Nia and Julian met near the closed radio booth after his morning rehearsal. The union was unusually quiet. Without the usual traffic, their footsteps echoed along the second-floor hall.

They had already spent Thursday evening at the kickoff festival and Saturday at the game. The date had stretched around practices and responsibilities instead of unfolding in one perfect block. Nia liked that. It felt less staged.

They walked outside to Cypress Walk, where rain from an early shower darkened the brick paths and left the leaves shining.

"Did you enjoy the game?" Julian asked.

"I understood at least forty percent more because of you."

"That is a passing grade in some departments."

"Not mine."

He smiled. "I saw you when we turned toward the student section."

"Copper wrist tape. You were right."

"About the handsome posture too?"

"The evidence was inconclusive."

They stopped near the side entrance to the union. Nia could hear the soft hum of the building's air system and a distant drumline practicing somewhere it was probably not supposed to.

Julian's confidence changed when the moment became private. He looked at her, then away, then back.

"Can I kiss you?" he asked.

Nia's heart performed something no limit law had prepared her to explain.

"Yes," she said.

The kiss was gentle, brief, and just awkward enough to be real. Julian's saxophone strap slid from his shoulder halfway through, and Nia caught the case before it hit the ground. They pulled apart laughing.

"Smooth," she said.

"The case is jealous."

When he kissed her again, they both knew where to put their hands.

## Tuesday, September 2: The Trial

Malik received Simone's message Tuesday morning while DJ was searching for a clean practice shirt.

**Your tendency helped staff flag the wide-side run during halftime review. They adjusted the edge alignment in the second half. Dr. Bennett approved a two-week trial seat for you. Meeting at 6.**

Malik read it once.

Then twice.

"Something happen?" DJ asked from inside a pile of laundry.

"I have a trial seat."

DJ emerged holding two shirts. "On analytics?"

"Two weeks."

DJ dropped both shirts and grabbed Malik by the shoulders. "My roommate is moving up in the world!"

"Please stop shaking me."

"Did your chart help us win?"

"It helped identify one second-half adjustment. Football is not a controlled experiment."

"So yes, but nerdy."

At the meeting, Simone placed Malik between two full members instead of at the end of the table.

"A trial seat is not a reward," she said quietly. "It is more responsibility with less certainty."

"Understood."

"And you do not have to bring a new discovery every day to justify the chair."

Malik opened his notebook. "Understood."

Simone looked unconvinced.

## Wednesday, September 3: The Hole in the Graph

Nia arrived at calculus on Wednesday with twelve unanswered club messages, two calendar conflicts, and the dreamy distraction of someone who had replayed a kiss often enough to edit its lighting.

Dr. Brooks gave the class a continuity problem. The function was piecewise, and students had to choose a missing constant so the graph joined without a break.

Nia understood the limit. She set the left and right expressions equal. Then she made an algebra error so basic that she did not notice it until Dr. Brooks returned the warm-up with a question mark beside the line.

"I distributed correctly," Nia whispered.

Amara leaned over. "You changed the sign here."

Nia stared at the page.

The mistake was not mysterious. It was an old weakness she had meant to review during the holiday. Instead, she had answered organization messages, adjusted volunteer schedules, helped draft a radio post, and floated through several hours thinking about Julian.

At North Star, Malik found her attacking algebra exercises with personal resentment.

"What happened?"

"I have been betrayed by negative signs."

He examined the paper. "This is review material."

"I know what it is."

He heard her tone and changed direction. "Do you want help or company?"

Nia looked up, surprised.

"Company," she said. "For ten minutes. Then help."

Malik sat across from her and opened his own work. He did not mention Julian. Nia was grateful, though she did not know whether the silence protected her or him.

## Thursday, September 4: Congratulations

Nia decided Malik should hear the news from her rather than from a photo, a rumor, or DJ performing an unauthorized relationship announcement in the dining hall.

They met beside Founders' Bell after class.

"Julian and I are seeing each other," she said. "Officially, I think. We have not filed anything with the registrar."

Malik's expression barely changed, but Nia knew him well enough now to notice the small stillness in his shoulders.

"Congratulations," he said.

"Thank you."

The space after those words became delicate.

Malik could have lied brightly. He could have acted as if the news meant nothing. Instead he looked down Cypress Walk, where students moved between classes beneath the indigo banners.

"I am happy you are happy," he said. "I may also need a little time to adjust."

Nia's chest tightened. "Adjust to what?"

He met her eyes. For one dangerous second, she thought he might answer fully.

"Our schedule," he said. "You have more commitments. I have the analytics trial. We should make sure the accountability check-ins still work."

It was true. It was not the whole truth.

Nia nodded slowly. "We can move them. Not cancel them."

"Agreed."

"And you can tell me if something is weird."

Malik gave a small, tired smile. "Something is weird."

She laughed, and the tension loosened without disappearing.

That week, Nia had received the relationship she wanted. Malik had received the chance he wanted. Both discoveries carried joy. Both also came with new conditions, hidden gaps, and no guarantee that the next step would be easier than the last.

In calculus, continuity meant the pieces met.

In life, meeting was only the beginning.

---

# Week Six: Continuity Under Pressure

Malik's first mistake as a trial member looked beautiful.

The chart used McCall-Hart indigo for successful plays, copper for failures, and pale gray for neutral outcomes. Labels aligned. Axes began at defensible values. The title stated exactly what the image claimed to show: third-down conversion rate by distance and formation.

Malik had checked every calculation twice.

He had not checked why one stretch of the season contained almost no data.

Simone enlarged the chart on the Quantitative Futures Lab screen. The team sat around the long table with laptops open and Friday-afternoon fatigue settling over the room.

"What happened here?" she asked.

Malik followed the cursor to the gap.

"Low sample size," he said.

"Why?"

"The source file has fewer tagged plays for those games."

"Why?"

He opened the raw table. Several rows included play descriptions and outcomes but no formation label. His filter had removed them automatically.

"Missing category values," he said.

"How many?"

Malik counted. The room seemed to grow quieter with each row.

"Forty-three."

Nearly one fifth of the relevant plays had disappeared from the visualization. The chart was mathematically accurate about the records it included and dangerously silent about the ones it did not.

Simone stepped away from the screen.

"Explain that to the team."

Malik wished she had yelled. Anger would have let him defend himself. Her calm left him alone with the facts.

## Friday, September 5: The Missing Fifth

Malik stood beside the screen.

"I filtered out rows without formation labels," he said. "I treated the result as a complete subset instead of reporting how much information the filter removed. The conversion rates are correct for labeled plays, but the chart does not show whether the missing plays differ systematically."

Dr. Bennett folded his arms. "What could make them systematic?"

"Tagging changed between staff exports. Certain game files may be less complete. If the missing labels cluster by opponent, game situation, or personnel group, the displayed rates could be biased."

"And did you check?" Simone asked.

"No."

The word hurt more than a complicated explanation would have.

A senior team member named Andre asked whether the chart should be discarded.

Malik wanted to say no. He wanted to defend the hours behind the colors and labels.

"Not necessarily," he said. "But it cannot be used as submitted. I need to map the missing rows, recover labels from film where possible, and show uncertainty where they cannot be recovered."

Dr. Bennett nodded. "Do that."

After the meeting, Malik closed his laptop with careful hands.

Simone remained by the screen. "You think I embarrassed you."

"You asked me to explain an error in front of the team."

"Yes. Because the team might use your work. They need to know what failed and whether you understand it."

"You could have told them."

"Then they would know that I understand your mistake. The point was to find out whether you do."

Malik looked at the blank screen.

"You explained it clearly," Simone said. "Now rebuild it clearly."

That was not forgiveness. It was something more useful: another chance with conditions.

## Saturday, September 6: Before the Shortcut

On Saturday, Dr. Brooks filled the board with secant lines.

She began with two points on a curve and the familiar formula for average rate of change. Then she moved one point closer to the other. The secant line shifted. The interval narrowed. The average rate approached something students recognized but had not yet been allowed to name casually.

"The derivative," someone said.

Dr. Brooks turned. "Eventually. First, tell me what has to happen."

The class worked through the difference quotient. Every time someone tried to jump to a memorized rule, Dr. Brooks returned them to the limit.

Malik's patience thinned.

"If we know the power rule is coming," he said, "why spend this long deriving it?"

Dr. Brooks rested the chalk against the board. "Because a shortcut is only trustworthy when you understand the road it shortens."

The sentence struck the sore place left by his chart.

She reduced the interval again. The secant line approached the tangent line, and average change approached instantaneous change.

"We are not plugging in zero," Nia said, thinking aloud. "We are asking what the quotient approaches as the interval becomes arbitrarily small."

"Exactly," Dr. Brooks said. "The expression at zero may be undefined while the limiting behavior gives us the rate."

Malik copied each step. His chart had offered a shortcut too: a polished picture that skipped the road from raw data to conclusion. He had trusted the destination because it looked right.

After class, Nia waited while he packed.

"You were quiet today."

"Analytics error."

"Bad?"

"Visible."

She nodded as if that answered the real question. "Want help?"

"Not with the chart."

"I can provide company first and help later. I hear that is a service we offer."

Malik smiled. "Company would be fine."

## Sunday, September 7: The Choice Nobody Requested

Nia had reserved Sunday afternoon for calculus review.

At 1:40, Julian texted that the band had unexpectedly gained rehearsal time in the stadium. At 1:42, he added: **No pressure, but if you are nearby, I can see you during break.**

Nia looked at the derivative problems on her desk.

She imagined Julian alone during break, though he had not said he would be alone. She imagined him disappointed, though his message had contained no disappointment. She imagined a good girlfriend would show up.

At 1:48, she closed the book.

The stadium was hot, the rehearsal ran late, and Julian's break lasted nine minutes. He was happy to see her. He shared his water, asked about calculus, and returned to the field when the whistle blew.

Nia sat for another hour waiting for the next break that never came.

By dinner, she was behind, hungry, and irritated.

Julian met her outside the stadium. "I thought you had a study block."

"I moved it."

"You did not have to do that."

"I know."

"Then why do you sound mad at me?"

"I do not."

Julian gave her a look that made clear his audio training could detect tone.

Nia shifted her bag. "I came all the way over here and barely saw you."

"I said I had rehearsal."

"You said I could come."

"I also said no pressure."

The conversation stood at the edge of becoming an argument.

Julian lowered his voice. "I was glad you came. But I did not ask you to give up your afternoon."

Nia knew he was right, which made her feel worse.

"I need to study," she said.

"Okay."

He did not chase her. She resented that for half a block before realizing how unfair the resentment was.

## Monday, September 8: Availability Is Not Affection

Keisha found Nia asleep over an open calculus book Monday morning.

"This is not studying," she said. "This is decorative unconsciousness."

Nia lifted her head. A limit expression had printed itself faintly on her cheek.

"I went to rehearsal."

"Were you performing?"

"No."

"Then why?"

Nia explained the text, the nine-minute break, and the argument that technically had not become an argument.

Keisha listened while packing her track bag.

"You volunteered your study time," she said, "then charged Julian emotionally when the thing he told you would happen happened."

"That sounds harsh."

"Do you want soft or useful? I can schedule soft tonight."

Nia dropped her face into her hands. "Useful."

Keisha sat across from her. "You keep treating availability like proof of affection. If you are always reachable, always helping, always rearranging, then nobody can say you did not care."

"I do care."

"I know. But care is not the same as having no boundaries."

Nia looked at her crowded planner. "What if I say no too often and people stop asking?"

"Then they wanted access, not you."

The answer was simple enough to frighten her.

Keisha tapped the missed study block. "Restore it. Tell Julian. Do not make it dramatic."

Nia moved the review session to Thursday evening and marked it in indigo.

"Why indigo?" Keisha asked.

"Protected time."

"Good. Now wash the equation off your face."

## Tuesday, September 9: Number Thirty-Seven

McCall-Hart's first road game unfolded badly.

The offense stalled. The defense spent too long on the field. By the third quarter, the Herons trailed by thirteen and the visiting section had reduced its optimism to stubborn noise.

DJ stood near the special-teams coordinator wearing number thirty-seven, waiting through every return call.

Then a second-year player cramped after a long coverage sprint.

"Cole!" the coordinator shouted.

DJ's body moved before his fear could vote.

He ran onto the field for the kickoff. Across the formation, the returner shifted his weight. DJ remembered the film: this unit liked to create a seam behind the second blocker, then cut toward the sideline.

The ball rose beneath the stadium lights.

DJ sprinted, avoided the first block, and saw the cut before it happened. He reached the returner near the numbers and struck through the tackle exactly as coaches had drilled. The ball came loose.

For one impossible second, it rested on the turf with twenty-two people changing direction around it.

A McCall-Hart player recovered.

The visiting section erupted.

The Herons failed to turn the possession into enough points. They lost by six. There was no miracle comeback, no score that transformed the night into a clean victory.

But in the locker room, the coordinator found DJ.

"Good read. Better finish," he said. "Be ready next week."

DJ texted Malik from the bus: **WE LOST BUT I FORCED IT OUT. TELL THE ROOM I AM A COLLEGE ATHLETE NOW.**

Malik replied: **The room was already aware. Your laundry confirms it.**

Then he watched the replay link three times and smiled every time number thirty-seven crossed the screen.

## Wednesday, September 10: Show the Weakness

Malik rebuilt the analytics chart from the raw data outward.
