He recovered eleven formation labels from game film. He separated the remaining missing rows by opponent and date. He added a visible bar showing data completeness for each game and replaced single conversion rates with intervals where sample sizes were small.

The new chart was less beautiful.

It was also more honest.

At Wednesday's review, Malik stood beside the screen again.

"The missing labels cluster in two early exports," he explained. "That means the original omission was not random. The revised view shows observed rates, available sample sizes, and the percentage of plays with usable formation tags. Where the evidence is weak, the chart says so."

Andre asked whether the result could still guide decisions.

"It can guide what film to review," Malik said. "It should not independently determine a play call."

Simone studied the uncertainty labels. "What did you give up?"

"A simpler story."

"And what did you gain?"

Malik looked at the team. "A claim we can defend."

Dr. Bennett nodded.

After the meeting, Simone caught up with Malik on the Porter Center steps.

"That was good work."

"The first version was not."

"I am aware. I was there."

He glanced at her.

"Respect is not believing somebody never makes mistakes," she said. "It is knowing what they do after one."

Malik felt the sentence settle somewhere deeper than praise.

## Thursday, September 11: Protected Time

Nia met Julian outside Bellwether Media House on Thursday with her planner in her bag and Keisha's voice in her head.

"I was unfair Sunday," she said.

Julian nodded slowly. "A little."

"You told me rehearsal might only give us a few minutes. I canceled my study block anyway, then acted like you made the choice."

"I could have been clearer about how busy it would be."

"You said 'no pressure.' That is clear in at least two languages."

He smiled, but let her continue.

"I like showing up for you," Nia said. "But I cannot organize my whole schedule around surprise rehearsal time. I have a protected study block tonight. If you get free, I am still studying."

Julian considered this for so little time that Nia wondered whether he had heard her.

"Okay," he said.

"Okay?"

"Yes. You should study. I have editing to do anyway."

"You are not upset?"

"Nia, I like you. I do not need you sitting alone in stadium seats to prove it."

She stared at him.

"What?"

"I prepared for that to be harder."

"I can argue if it helps."

"No, thank you."

Julian took her hand. "Tell me when time is protected. I will believe you. I need you to believe me when I say I am busy too."

"Deal."

At North Star that evening, Nia completed the review problems she had postponed. Malik sat two tables away, revising analytics notes. Their first calculus exam had been announced, and neither knew yet whether the lessons they had learned outside class had arrived early enough to protect their grades.

For the moment, the room was quiet.

Nia kept the time she had promised herself. Malik labeled the weakness in his model instead of hiding it. Across campus, DJ replayed his forced fumble while pretending not to.

Continuity did not mean nothing changed. It meant that when pressure arrived, the pieces still connected.

---

# Week Seven: What the First Test Measures

By Friday night, DJ's forced fumble had acquired music.

The clip appeared on the stadium screen before McCall-Hart's home opener: number thirty-seven racing downfield, slipping past a blocker, striking the returner, and sending the football loose beneath the lights. Someone in campus media had slowed the moment of impact and added a bass drop dramatic enough to suggest DJ had saved the entire city of Bellwether.

The student section exploded.

Beside Malik, DJ's auntie screamed, "THAT IS MY BABY!" with enough force to create weather.

DJ stood on the sideline pretending not to look at the screen. The grin inside his helmet ruined the performance.

Nia waved from several rows below, where she sat near Keisha and the band section. Julian was already in formation with the Marching Herons, copper wrist tape visible beneath his glove.

Malik let the noise wash over him. For one clean moment, every difficult thing seemed to be moving in the right direction. DJ had earned recognition. Nia looked happy. Malik's corrected analytics chart had held up under team review, and Simone had asked him to attend a roster meeting Monday.

Tomorrow morning, he would take his first calculus test.

Tomorrow afternoon, analytics would review opponent data.

Both mattered. Malik had already decided he could fit them into the same day.

## Friday, September 12: The Highlight

McCall-Hart won the home opener under a storm-blue sky that never released its rain.

DJ played on two coverage units. He did not force another turnover, but he stayed in his lane, beat one block, and made a tackle that drew a sharp nod from the special-teams coordinator.

After the game, students crowded around him near the tunnel.

"Number Thirty-Seven!" somebody called.

"Fumble King!"

DJ pointed at Malik. "That man charted the returner's tendencies. Academic contribution."

"I did not," Malik said.

"He keeps me humble too. Full-service roommate."

Nia and Keisha joined them. Julian had a postgame band responsibility, but he sent Nia a selfie from the field, his uniform collar open and his face shining with sweat.

"Look at him," Nia said, showing Keisha.

Keisha glanced at the screen. "Yes. That is Julian. We have confirmed his identity."

"You are no fun."

"I ran intervals this morning. I used my fun already."

DJ replayed his highlight for the group even though all of them had just watched it on a screen forty feet wide.

Malik laughed with everyone else, but his mind kept counting backward from the analytics meeting. If the calculus test began at nine, he could finish by ten fifteen, cross campus, review his notes over lunch, and arrive at Porter Center before one.

The test window lasted two hours.

Malik planned to need seventy-five minutes.

## Saturday, September 13: The Time He Saves

The first page of the calculus test felt familiar.

Evaluate limits. Compare one-sided behavior. Identify discontinuities. Malik moved quickly, writing enough to satisfy himself and not enough to hear Dr. Brooks asking, *What claim are you making? What supports it?*

Halfway through, he checked the clock.

Forty-three minutes remained in the schedule he had created for himself. Ninety-eight minutes remained in the actual test window.

The next problem asked students to determine whether a two-sided limit existed and justify the conclusion from both sides. Malik wrote **DNE** and one line about unequal values.

On another problem, he used a limit law without stating why the required component limits existed.

He knew how to explain both answers. He could feel the complete arguments waiting just behind the shortened versions.

But the analytics meeting was across campus. A full roster decision might be coming. Every minute spent proving something he already knew felt like a minute taken from the opportunity he had not yet secured.

Malik turned in the test with thirty-one minutes left.

Dr. Brooks glanced at the clock, then at him. "Finished?"

"Yes."

"Or leaving?"

The question stopped him.

"Finished," he repeated.

At Porter Center, he arrived early.

The meeting did not begin for another twenty minutes.

Malik sat outside with his analytics notebook open and remembered the empty space beneath **DNE**. He told himself the answer was correct. He told himself correctness mattered most.

Then Simone arrived carrying coffee.

"You look upset."

"I am early."

"Those conditions can coexist."

He looked back at the notebook. "Test."

"How did it go?"

"Fine."

It was the first time he used the word that week.

It would not be the last.

## Sunday, September 14: Borrowed Strength

Nia left the test feeling lighter than she had entered.

She had slowed down. She checked the algebra that had embarrassed her the previous week. On the final graph problem, she explained that one side approached positive infinity while the other approached negative infinity, so the two-sided limit did not exist.

The explanation sounded clear because it was clear.

It also sounded like Amara.

During Sunday's study check-in, Nia found Amara at North Star.

"I used your sign explanation on the test," Nia said.

Amara looked alarmed. "The exact answer?"

"No! Not copied. I mean the way you separated the sides before naming the two-sided limit. It helped me see what I was doing."

"Oh." Amara smiled. "Good."

"I wanted to tell you because I keep thinking about that first workshop. I was so ready to show I understood that I nearly missed the best explanation in the group."

Amara closed her notebook. "You did miss it. Then you came back."

Nia nodded. "Still embarrassing."

"Maybe. But now you listen longer."

They worked through derivative rules together. Nia talked less than usual. Amara talked more.

Later, Nia texted Malik: **How are you feeling about the test now?**

His reply came after six minutes: **Fine. Analytics meeting tomorrow.**

Nia stared at the word.

Fine was Malik's favorite locked door.

## Monday, September 15: The Chair Is His

The analytics roster meeting lasted twelve minutes.

Simone sat across from Malik in the Quantitative Futures Lab. Dr. Bennett stood near the window, reading through Malik's revised chart notes one last time.

"The team has an open development seat," Simone said. "We would like you to take it for the remainder of the fall cycle. Full access, assigned deliverables, and travel eligibility if the team advances."

Malik kept his expression still. "Based on the tendency report?"

"Partly," Dr. Bennett said. "More because of the missing-data correction."

That surprised him.

Simone folded her hands. "Plenty of students can make a polished chart. Fewer can stand in front of a room, name what they did wrong, and rebuild the claim without pretending the mistake never happened."

"You will still need to manage your coursework," Dr. Bennett added. "A team seat is not permission to neglect the classes that make you useful to the team."

Malik heard the warning and thought of the test he had left early.

"Understood," he said.

Simone slid a team folder across the table. His name appeared on the roster beneath hers.

For the first time since arriving at McCall-Hart, Malik had been chosen for something he was not certain he could earn.

He called his parents from the Porter Center steps.

His mother answered on speaker. His father shouted congratulations from somewhere in the background, then asked whether analytics had benefits.

"Experience," Malik said.

"Experience is what people pay you in before money," Marcel replied.

Celeste laughed. "Let the boy enjoy it. We are proud of you."

Malik closed his eyes.

"How are classes?" she asked.

"Good."

The second lie arrived more easily.

## Tuesday, September 16: Sixty-Four

DJ celebrated Malik's full roster seat with grocery-store cupcakes and a handwritten sign taped above the television: **DATA MAN HAS BEEN ACTIVATED**.

"This font is hostile," Malik said.

"This font is joy."

They ate cupcakes from paper towels while DJ replayed the forced fumble and Malik pretended he had not already memorized the clip.

Then his laptop chimed.

**Calculus I: Test 1 grades posted.**

Malik opened the course page.

64.

For a moment, the number did not belong to him. It looked like a score attached to somebody who had forgotten to study, somebody who guessed, somebody whose parents had not just told the whole family that he made a college analytics team.

He opened the feedback.

Two limit arguments had lost nearly all available credit because the justifications were incomplete. A continuity problem contained a sign error he would have caught if he had checked. Dr. Brooks had written one sentence at the bottom: **Your answers show more understanding than your explanations reveal. Please come to office hours.**

DJ leaned over. "What did you get?"

Malik closed the screen.

"Not what I expected."

"Bad?"

"I know what happened."

"That was also not my question."

Malik stood and began clearing the cupcake wrappers. "I rushed. I can correct it."

DJ watched him turn celebration into cleanup.

"You want me to leave it alone?"

"Yes."

DJ did, though concern stayed in the room after the questions stopped.

## Wednesday, September 17: Fine

Nia caught Malik outside calculus.

"Scores posted," she said.

"I saw."

"How did you do?"

"Fine."

She searched his face. "Number fine or emotional fine?"

"I made avoidable mistakes."

"So did I. What number?"

Students moved around them toward the stairs. Dr. Brooks remained inside, beginning office hours in ten minutes.

Malik adjusted his laptop bag. "I have analytics work."

"Malik."

"It is handled."

He left before Nia could ask again.

At North Star, he opened the test correction form. It required students to identify each error, explain the corrected reasoning, and describe what habit would prevent the mistake next time.

Malik completed the mathematics quickly.

The habit question sat blank.

He knew the answer: he had treated time spent explaining as time wasted. He had valued arriving at analytics over showing what he understood. He had made a choice, not suffered an accident.

Dr. Brooks's office hours came and went.

Malik left the correction sheet beneath a stack of scrap paper and went to the lab.

## Thursday, September 18: The Goal and the Bad News

Nia found the correction sheet while gathering their notes after an accountability session Malik had left early.

She saw the score before she could decide not to.

64.

Below it, Malik's corrected arguments were careful and complete. The blank reflection box looked louder than the red marks.

She found him outside Eliza Moss Hall.

"You left this," she said.

Malik saw the paper in her hand and stopped.

"You said the test was fine."

"I said I made avoidable mistakes."

"You also said fine. More than once."

"It is my grade."

"Yes. And we agreed to be honest accountability partners."

"Accountability does not mean you are entitled to every number."

Nia flinched. "I know that."

Malik heard the hurt and wanted to take the sentence back. Shame had already made it sharp.

She lowered the paper. "You trust me with goals. You tell me what you are applying for, what team you want, what you plan to become. Why do you not trust me with bad news?"

He had no answer that did not expose more than the grade.

His phone rang.

**Mom**.

Malik almost silenced it. Then another alert covered the screen: **Gulf Coast storm system strengthens; watches possible.**

He answered.

Celeste did not begin with hello.

"Baby, your daddy is bringing things in from the yard," she said. Wind scraped across the connection. "The forecast shifted. They are saying we could get more water than they thought."

Malik looked at Nia. The correction sheet trembled slightly in her hand.

The calculus grade, so enormous one minute earlier, became one problem among several without becoming any less real.

"Tell me exactly what the weather service said," Malik told his mother.

He opened his laptop on the limestone steps, already reaching for numbers that might make the fear obey.

---

# Week Eight: No Model for the Storm

By Friday morning, Malik had six weather tabs open.

One showed the storm's projected path. One displayed rainfall totals. Another tracked river gauges near the Mississippi coast. Two offered different wind estimates. The final tab was a local news livestream from Gulfport, muted beneath Dr. Brooks's calculus lecture.

His mother had texted at 7:11.

**Water is already gathering at the end of the street. We moved the photo boxes up. Daddy says do not worry. You know how he is.**

Malik knew exactly how his father was.

Marcel Baptiste believed most problems improved when met with work boots, a good wrench, and fewer people standing around discussing them. If a storm pushed water toward the house, he would lift furniture until his back hurt. If the lights failed, he would check every breaker before admitting the outage extended beyond their walls.

On the board, Dr. Brooks differentiated a product of two functions.

"We cannot treat both factors as fixed," she said. "Each is changing, so each contributes to the rate of change."

Malik refreshed the forecast.

The projected rainfall increased by two inches.

## Friday, September 19: Refresh

Nia noticed the third time Malik looked down during class.

He never looked at his phone during class. He barely approved when other people did.

"Home?" she whispered.

He nodded.

Dr. Brooks moved from the product rule to examples, but Malik's notes became fragments. He copied the derivative of the first factor, forgot the second, and refreshed the river gauge again.

After class, Nia followed him into the hall.

"What do they need?"

"Nothing yet."

"Do they have supplies?"

"Yes."

"Have they moved the car?"

"My mother did. My father says the truck is high enough."

"Is it?"

"That depends on the water."

Malik was already walking toward North Star, where he could use three screens at once. Nia kept pace.

"I can help call--"

"Please do not."

The words came out harder than he meant.

Nia stopped.

Malik turned back. "I am sorry. Everybody keeps asking what they can do. I do not have a job to assign."

"You do not have to assign me one."

"Then I need quiet."

Nia swallowed the ten responses that arrived immediately.

"Okay," she said. "Quiet."

She left him at North Star with the forecasts, the grade he had still not discussed, and a fear too large for either of them to organize.

## Saturday, September 20: A Range Is Not Comfort

Dr. Bennett's Saturday colloquium had been scheduled weeks earlier. Its title, **Decisions Under Uncertainty**, now appeared designed specifically to torment Malik.

The lecture hall filled with students, faculty, and several members of Heron Analytics. On screen, Dr. Bennett displayed a storm cone.

"People often read a projected path as a promise," he said. "It is not. A forecast communicates a range of plausible outcomes based on current evidence. The center line is not destiny, and the edge is not safety."

Malik wrote down the definitions. Confidence interval. Scenario range. Conditional probability. Expected loss.

Each term gave him a more precise way to describe why he could not breathe normally.

None made breathing easier.

During questions, a student asked why forecasts changed so often.

"Because the atmosphere changes and observations improve," Dr. Bennett said. "Revision is not proof that the earlier forecast was dishonest. It is what responsible modeling looks like when new evidence arrives."

Malik thought of his family budget. His missing-data chart. His calculus score. Every model this semester had asked him to revise.

He was tired of revision. He wanted one answer to hold.

After the talk, Simone found him in the aisle.

"I can reassign your opponent-data section," she said.

"No."

"That was an offer, not a demotion."

"I can finish it."

"I did not ask whether you could."

The echo of Nia's first question at the Bridge table caught him off guard.

Simone lowered her voice. "Tell me by Sunday night. If I do not hear from you, I am dividing it. That is a team decision, not a judgment of your character."

Malik nodded, though control was the only thing he did not want divided.

## Sunday, September 21: Help Without Taking Over

McCall-Hart opened a storm-relief supply table in Hart Student Union on Sunday.

Nia arrived early, created category signs, rearranged the donation bins, drafted a volunteer schedule, and began designing a tracking sheet before remembering that nobody had asked her to run the operation.

The actual coordinator, a public-health senior named Imani Cole, arrived carrying two flattened boxes.

"You must be DJ's sister," Nia said.

"And you must be the person who turned my folding table into a logistics center."

Nia looked at the labels. "Too much?"

Imani considered them. "Useful. But ask before you redesign somebody's whole system."

"That advice is following me lately."

Together they sorted batteries, cleaning supplies, shelf-stable food, work gloves, and hygiene items for affected families. Imani explained that useful relief work depended on verified requests, not on collecting whatever made donors feel generous.

Malik stopped by in the afternoon.

Nia started to tell him about the supply categories, transportation list, and emergency grant information. Then she saw his face.

"Quiet?" she asked.

"Please."

She nodded toward an empty chair behind the table.

Malik sat. Nia worked beside him without requesting updates. She handed him water once. When another student recognized his Gulfport hometown and began asking questions, Nia redirected the student toward the volunteer signup.

After twenty minutes, Malik stood.

"Thank you," he said.

"I did nothing."

"Exactly."

Nia watched him leave and understood, perhaps for the first time, that care did not always need to become visible labor.

## Monday, September 22: Verified

Julian canceled his usual music segment Monday evening.

Instead, campus radio carried storm updates, university support information, and interviews with staff coordinating student assistance. Julian repeated only details confirmed through official sources and corrected one rumor about mandatory evacuations before it could spread across residence-hall group chats.

Nia sat behind the booth glass, watching him work.

His public confidence had changed shape. He did not joke to fill every pause. He checked names, links, and times before speaking. When a caller began describing frightening footage from an unknown account, Julian interrupted gently.

"I hear why that worried you," he said. "We cannot verify that video, so we are not going to tell listeners it shows the current conditions. Let me give you the confirmed update we have."

During a break, Nia entered the booth.

"You are good at this."

Julian removed one headphone. "I am trying to be careful."

"That is what I mean."

He looked through the glass toward the students waiting with resource sheets. "Media can make fear move faster than weather. If we add to it, we need to know why."

Nia thought of Malik refreshing forecasts, trying to separate probability from panic.

"Can you share the supply-drive hours again?" she asked.

"Already in the next block."

He took her hand for three seconds beneath the desk, then returned to the microphone.

Nia had liked Julian when he made a room brighter. That evening, she admired him for refusing to make a crisis about his own voice.

## Tuesday, September 23: The Call

The storm crossed the coast before dawn.

Malik slept in intervals of twelve and seventeen minutes, waking each time his phone vibrated. At 5:36, Celeste reported water at the front steps. At 6:02, it entered the utility room. At 6:19, the power failed.

Then messages stopped.

Malik called his mother. No connection.

He called his father. Voicemail.

He called the neighbor across the street. Nothing.

By eight, water had flooded the lower rooms of the Baptiste home.

At 10:14, Celeste finally reached him from a relative's phone.

"We are safe," she said before he could speak. "We got to Aunt Renee's. Micah is with us."

Micah, Malik's twelve-year-old cousin, spent most afternoons and many weekends at the Baptiste house. His school backpack and the game system he had saved for all summer were still there.

"Where is Daddy?" Malik asked.

Silence.

"Mom."

"He went back when the water started dropping. He wanted to move the shop equipment before more rain. Something slipped. He cut his leg bad and twisted his knee. Mr. Landry took him to urgent care."

Malik stood so quickly his desk chair struck the wall.

DJ sat up in bed. "What happened?"

Celeste continued talking: stitches, imaging, no fracture visible, more evaluation needed. Malik wrote every detail on the back of an analytics printout because it was the closest paper within reach.

"Why did he go back?" he demanded.

"Because that equipment is how he works."

"He cannot work if he cannot walk."

His mother went quiet.

Malik pressed his hand over his eyes. "I am sorry. I am sorry. Tell him I am coming home."

"We do not even know which roads are open."

"I will find one."

The same instinct that had built budgets and charts now seized on bus routes, rental cars, airport schedules, and road closures. If he could arrange transportation, perhaps the rest of the disaster would become a list.

It did not.

## Wednesday, September 24: The Quiz He Misses

Malik was still trying to get home when his calculus quiz began.

The earliest bus had been canceled. The next route required two transfers and ended far from Gulfport. Flights cost more than his family had available. A rideshare estimate looked almost insulting.

At 9:10, Nia texted: **Are you coming to class?**

He typed **Yes**, deleted it, and called Dr. Brooks.

She answered from her office.

"Malik?"

For several seconds, he could not force the situation into an orderly sentence.

"The storm flooded our house," he said. "My father was injured during cleanup. I am trying to get to Gulfport. I missed the quiz."

"Are your family members safe now?"

"Yes. My father has stitches and a knee injury. They are staying with my aunt."

"All right. The quiz is not today's emergency."

"I can take it online."

"No. You can document the absence when you have access to the records. We will arrange make-up work after you know your travel plan."

"I already have a sixty-four on the test."

The confession escaped before he could stop it.

Dr. Brooks's voice remained steady. "Which is why I invited you to office hours. We will address that grade. It is not improved by pretending your family crisis is a normal study day."

Malik sat on the edge of his bed.

"I should have come," he said.

"Yes," she replied, without pretending otherwise. "And you called today. That matters."

After they hung up, Malik emailed documentation requests, notified Dr. Bennett, and accepted that the team would redistribute his work.

The list did not make him calm.

It gave him somewhere to begin.

## Thursday, September 25: The Station

Nia found a bus route leaving Montgomery late Thursday and connecting toward the coast. DJ borrowed his sister's car to get Malik to the station.

The three of them drove through Bellwether beneath a sky scrubbed clear after days of threat. The normal weather felt rude.

Malik carried one backpack. Nia had packed snacks. DJ had packed twice as many snacks and called them "infrastructure."

At the station, announcements cracked through tired speakers. Families clustered around charging outlets. A television above the ticket counter showed aerial footage of flooded neighborhoods.

Malik kept his eyes off the screen.

"Simone said the team has your work," DJ told him. "Dr. Brooks knows. I have the room."

"Do not touch the folders on my desk."

"I will guard them with my life."

Nia held out the bag of food. "You do not have to text updates. But if you want to, I will answer."

Malik took the bag. "Thank you."

His bus had not yet arrived. The waiting made every thought louder.

"My mom says they are safe," he said. "My father says his knee is fine, which means it is not."

DJ nodded.

"I keep looking at flood estimates," Malik continued. "But I do not know how high the water got inside. I do not know what insurance will cover. I do not know whether his equipment works."

He looked at Nia then, letting her see the fear without turning it into a question she had to solve.

"I do not know what they will come back to."

Nia's eyes filled, but she did not promise everything would be all right.

"You will not face it by yourself," she said.

The bus pulled beneath the station lights.

Malik hugged DJ first, quick and hard. When Nia stepped forward, he hesitated only a moment before holding her too.

Then he boarded with no model for the house waiting at the other end.

---

# Week Nine: What Water Takes

The house smelled wrong.

Malik noticed it before he saw the waterline. The air held mud, wet wood, spoiled food, cleaning chemicals, and the sour beginning of mold. It did not smell like the lemon oil his mother used on Saturdays or the pepper and onions his father cooked into nearly every breakfast. It did not smell like home.

A brown line crossed the walls of the lower rooms, marking how high the water had risen. Cabinet doors hung open. The living-room rug had been dragged into the yard, where it lay folded over itself like something exhausted. Marcel's tools rested on towels across Aunt Renee's garage, separated into piles labeled **DRY**, **CLEAN**, and **MAYBE**.

Malik stood in the doorway with his backpack still on.

His mother came from the hall wearing rubber boots and work gloves.

"Baby."

She hugged him carefully, though she was not the one injured.

Behind her, Marcel sat in a dining chair with his left leg braced and elevated on a plastic storage bin. A bandage covered the cut along his shin. He lifted one hand.

"You came all this way to inspect my bad decisions?"

Malik dropped his backpack. "I can start with the knee."

His father smiled, but pain tightened the corners of his mouth.

Nothing in Malik's forecasts had prepared him for that expression.

## Friday, September 26: The Inventory

Insurance required an inventory.

Malik built one at the dining table while relatives and neighbors moved through the house in boots and masks. He listed the couch, two bookcases, kitchen cabinets, small appliances, Marcel's damaged equipment, and boxes of documents stored too close to the floor.

Each line needed a description, estimated age, estimated value, and evidence if available.

The work should have suited him.

Instead, every item resisted becoming a number.

The couch was where Micah slept during movie nights. One bookcase had belonged to Malik's grandmother. A ruined plastic bin held old school records, childhood drawings, honor-roll certificates, and the program from Malik's high school graduation.

Celeste lifted damp papers one by one and placed them between towels.

"Take pictures before we throw anything away," Malik said.

"I know."

"The policy may require--"

"Malik." His mother's voice was tired, not angry. "I know."

He stopped.

Across the room, Marcel tried to stand without help. His injured leg buckled, and he caught the chair back.

Malik moved toward him.

"I have it," Marcel said.

"You almost fell."

"Almost is not fell."

"That is not how risk works."

His father gave a humorless laugh. "Do not bring college words into my kitchen."

The kitchen floor squished beneath his crutch.

Malik looked down.

Marcel did too. Some of the fight left his face.

"Help me to the porch," he said.

It was the first task Malik completed all day that felt entirely useful.

## Saturday, September 27: The Rate of Rising Water

Dr. Brooks had sent a make-up packet with flexible dates and a note: **Complete only what you can document time for. Family recovery comes first. Communication keeps options open.**

One assignment covered related rates.

Water poured into a cylindrical tank. Its depth increased at a known rate. Students had to determine how quickly the volume changed.

Malik stared at the problem from Aunt Renee's kitchen table.

He wanted to close the laptop.

Outside, waterlogged furniture lined the curbs. Inside, a textbook had invented polite water that entered a perfect container at a steady rate and damaged nothing.

Micah climbed into the chair beside him. The twelve-year-old had been quieter than usual since the storm. He wore somebody else's slides because his sneakers were still inside a ruined closet.

"Is that flood math?" he asked.

"Tank math."

"Same water."

"Different problem."

Micah looked toward the room where Celeste was speaking with the insurance company. "How fast did the water come in our house?"

Malik began to say they did not have enough information. Then he remembered his mother's texts.

At 5:36, water was at the steps. At 6:02, it reached the utility room. At 6:19, the power failed after water moved farther inside. They had approximate depths from marks on the walls and videos from a neighbor.

Malik drew the house footprint on paper.

"If we estimate how much floor area the water covered," he explained, "and how the depth changed over time, we can estimate how quickly the amount of water inside was growing."

"Can we find when it got to my game?"

The console had been on a low shelf.

Malik swallowed. "Probably within a range. Not an exact minute."

Together they marked times and estimated depths. The numbers suggested the worst increase came after water crossed the threshold, when a small rise outside spread across a large indoor area.

"So it got deep fast," Micah said.

"Yes."

"Could we have moved more stuff?"

Malik heard the real question.

"Maybe a few things," he said. "But nobody should have stayed inside trying to beat the water. Things can be replaced. You cannot."

Micah traced the line representing the wall. "Even the game?"

"Even the game."

The textbook problem still felt cruel. But the mathematics gave Micah a way to ask what had happened, and Malik a way to answer without pretending the loss had been preventable.

## Sunday, September 28: Divided Work

Simone called Sunday afternoon.

Malik stepped onto Aunt Renee's porch, where an extension cord ran from a neighbor's generator and three pairs of cleaned work gloves dried on the rail.

"How is your father?" she asked.

"The knee is badly sprained. The cut needed stitches. He has an orthopedic appointment."

"And the house?"

Malik looked across the street at debris piled along the curb. "Not livable yet."

Simone let the answer stand.

"I divided your analytics section," she said. "Andre has formation tagging. Priya has game-state splits. I am handling the uncertainty notes."

Malik's grip tightened on the phone. "I can work tonight."

"No."

"The deadline did not change because of me."

"Correct. That is why the team adjusted."

"I should at least review the final version."

"When you return, if there is time."

He paced to the end of the porch. "It was my section."

"It was the team's section assigned to you."

The distinction irritated him because it was true.

Simone's voice softened. "Nobody is punishing you. Nobody thinks you disappeared because you are unreliable. We are doing what a team is supposed to do when one person's life catches fire. Or floods. You know what I mean."

Malik looked through the screen door. His father struggled to adjust the brace without asking for help.

"Thank you," he said.

After the call, he opened the shared folder anyway. Andre had already improved the tagging notes. Priya had found a useful game-state split Malik had missed.

Relief and jealousy arrived together.

The work could continue without him.

That was the point of a team. It was also, at that moment, the part he found hardest to accept.

## Monday, September 29: Official

At McCall-Hart, Founders' Cup week opened with a short ceremony near Founders' Bell. Alumni representatives spoke about the university's history, student leaders announced service projects, and the Marching Herons formed an indigo-and-copper line along Cypress Walk.

Nia stood with Keisha near the limestone arch. She had checked her phone twice for an update from Malik and stopped herself from checking a third time.

Julian found her after the ceremony.

"Walk with me?"

They moved away from the crowd, though not far enough to escape the bell's shadow.

Julian had been thoughtful all week: sharing verified resources on air, helping publicize the supply drive, and asking about Malik without behaving as if concern made him part of a competition.

"I have a question," he said.

"Math or media?"

"Relationship administration."

"Very serious department."

Julian smiled, then became nervous enough that Nia stopped joking.

"We have been seeing each other," he said. "I would like to call you my girlfriend. I would like to be your boyfriend. Not because a label makes everything different, but because I do not want to act casual about something that is not casual to me."

Nia looked at him beneath the old bell, surrounded by students beginning traditions they did not yet fully understand.

"Yes," she said. "I want that too."

Julian exhaled. "Good. I had a whole second speech if you asked for time."

"Was it better?"

"Much worse."

She laughed and kissed him.

Later, Nia sent Malik a photo of the supply shipment leaving campus, not a relationship announcement. It did not feel like the moment to add another emotion to his week.

She told herself she would explain when he came back.

## Tuesday, September 30: Three Catches

The Founders' Cup game gave DJ his first real opportunity on offense.

An upperclassman receiver left in the second quarter with a shoulder injury. DJ heard his name and ran into the huddle before anybody could reconsider.

His first target came on third-and-seven. The pass arrived high and behind him. DJ twisted, caught it against his shoulder, and took a hit before securing the ball.

First down.

The second catch required him to drag both feet near the sideline. The third came across the middle with a safety closing fast.

Three difficult catches. Thirty-eight yards. No touchdown, no viral miracle, but enough reliable work to keep him on the field during the fourth-quarter drive that sealed McCall-Hart's win.

Nia, Keisha, and Imani celebrated in the stands. Julian played with the band, turning toward Nia's section after every scoring drive. Malik watched the game on Aunt Renee's television with Micah asleep on the couch and Marcel's injured leg elevated beside him.

When DJ made the sideline catch, Marcel pointed at the screen.

"That is your roommate?"

"Yes."

"Boy has hands."

Malik texted DJ: **Three catches. One missed blocking assignment in Q3. Good work.**

DJ replied after the game: **I LOVE YOU TOO, DATA MAN.**

Malik smiled at the phone.

For the first time since arriving home, campus felt connected to him rather than impossibly far away.

## Wednesday, October 1: Emergency Aid

Malik returned to Bellwether Wednesday morning carrying photographs, medical paperwork, repair estimates, and a new understanding of how quickly a family could become a file.

His emergency financial-aid appointment took place at Porter Center. The counselor reviewed temporary assistance, documentation requirements, meal support, and a possible adjustment while his father's income was interrupted.

"Some funds are limited," she said. "I do not want to promise an amount before review."

Malik appreciated the honest uncertainty and hated needing it.

He submitted copies of the urgent-care bill and storm-damage report. He asked about campus employment.

"There may be evening hours at North Star Learning Commons," the counselor said. "Desk support, equipment checkout, and closing tasks. It will compete with study time."

"I can manage it."

The answer came automatically.

That afternoon, Malik completed the job application. He listed his Bridge experience, technical skills, and availability after classes. He did not list analytics meetings until the form forced him to.

At the bottom, a question asked why he wanted the position.

He typed: **To support student access to academic resources while developing professional experience.**

Then he deleted it.

He wrote: **My family experienced storm damage and a temporary loss of income. I need evening work that can fit around my courses.**

The second answer felt exposed.

He submitted it anyway.

## Thursday, October 2: What He Does Not Hand Her

Malik saw Nia and Julian outside Hart Student Union.

They were holding hands. Not accidentally, not tentatively. Julian said something that made Nia lean into his shoulder, and the happiness on her face was familiar enough to hurt.

Nia noticed Malik first.

Her smile changed. "You're back."

She released Julian's hand and stepped forward, then seemed unsure whether to hug him.

Malik solved the uncertainty by hugging her.

"How is your family?" she asked.

"Safe. My father has more appointments. The house needs a lot of work."

"I am sorry."

"The supply shipment arrived. My mother saw the McCall-Hart labels. She told me to thank everybody."

Julian offered his hand. Malik shook it.

"The station is still sharing recovery resources," Julian said. "If your family has a verified fundraiser or community drive, send it to me. No promises, but I can take it through review."

"Thank you."

The three of them stood in a small triangle of things not yet said.

Nia drew a breath. "Julian and I made it official Monday. I wanted to tell you myself, but you were dealing with--"

"Congratulations," Malik said.

This time, the word cost more. It was also true.

"Are you okay?" she asked.

No. His father's work had stopped. The insurance claim had barely begun. He had make-up calculus assignments, a sixty-four, a job application, and analytics work now improved by other people. He had returned to find the person he wanted holding hands with someone who had shown up responsibly during a crisis.

Malik could have placed all of that in Nia's arms and waited to see what she did with it.

"I am glad you told me," he said instead. "I need to meet Dr. Brooks before class. We can talk later."

Nia studied him. "Do not disappear."

"I will not."

He walked toward Eliza Moss Hall.

The promise was harder than it sounded. Malik's instinct was to hide until the grade improved, the job was secured, the house was repaired, and wanting Nia no longer made him feel disloyal to a friendship he valued.

But water had already shown him what waiting could take.

This time, he entered Dr. Brooks's office with the bad news still visible.

---

# Week Ten: The Cost of Looking Fine

Malik's new job began at the exact place where he was supposed to study.

At six Friday evening, he sat behind the welcome desk at North Star Learning Commons wearing a temporary name badge and learning the checkout system for calculators, laptop chargers, whiteboard kits, and small study rooms. Students passed him carrying calculus books, coffee, and the unmistakable panic of deadlines that had been reasonable until they became tonight's problem.

His supervisor, Ms. Alvarez, showed him the closing checklist.

"Last reservation ends at ten thirty. We close at eleven. You wipe the boards, check equipment, reset the rooms, and call campus safety if anybody refuses to leave."

"Does that happen often?"

"Around finals, students try to claim squatter's rights."

Malik smiled.

The shift fit the availability he had submitted. Friday and two weeknights, mostly after classes and analytics. It offered enough hours to send something home without losing every study block.

That was the model.

The model assumed no delayed buses, family calls, make-up assignments, team emergencies, or human need for sleep.

Malik did not write those limitations down.

## Friday, October 3: Closing Time

The first shift was quiet until nine, then every group room filled at once.

Malik found a missing calculator, reset a student's password request through the proper office, and explained three times that North Star loaned chargers but did not possess magical replacements for unfinished essays.

At ten fifty-eight, the final students left.

Malik completed the closing checklist, clocked out, and chose a table near the darkened tutoring boards. His linear approximation assignment was due Saturday. He only needed to finish four problems.

The first asked him to estimate a square root using a nearby value and a tangent-line approximation.

He wrote the function, chose the convenient point, calculated the derivative, and rested his forehead on one hand.

His phone showed two messages from Celeste about an insurance adjuster's request and one from Simone asking for a model update by noon.

Malik answered all three.

Then he returned to the tangent line.

At 12:43, Ms. Alvarez found him asleep over the problem while returning for the keys she had left in her office.

"Malik."

He woke so quickly his pencil rolled to the floor.

"The building is closed," she said.

"I work here."

"You worked here. Past tense. Now you are unconscious furniture."

He packed his bag, embarrassed.

Outside, Bellwether's night air felt cooler than it had all semester. Malik walked back to the residence hall telling himself the first shift was an adjustment.

He did not change the next shift on his calendar.

## Saturday, October 4: The Narrow Story

The analytics model checkpoint focused on late-game fourth-down decisions.

Malik had built a forecast using field position, yards to gain, score difference, time remaining, and recent opponent tendencies. The main visualization produced one clean recommendation for each scenario: attempt, punt, or kick.

It looked decisive.

Simone studied the screen. "Where is the uncertainty?"

"In the appendix."

"Put it here."

Malik opened a second chart. The recommendation changed across a wide interval when the sample was small or when assumptions about possession value shifted.

Andre leaned back. "So the main chart says go for it, but the range says all three options may be defensible."

"Under certain parameter choices," Malik said.

"How certain are those choices?" Simone asked.

"They use league averages."

"That was not my question."

Malik felt heat rise beneath his collar. "The single recommendation is easier for staff to interpret."

"Easy is not the same as useful."

"If we present every interval, the model looks indecisive."

Simone turned from the screen. "The model *is* uncertain. You are asking the colors to hide that."

The room went still.

Malik heard another meaning beneath her words, though she had not put it there. His family was safe. His job was manageable. His calculus recovery was underway. His analytics work remained on schedule. Every statement was technically defensible if nobody asked for the intervals.

"I will not present the main chart like this," Simone said. "Revise it."

Malik closed the file. "Understood."

He did understand.

He also resented her for making the weakness visible again.

## Sunday, October 5: Two Promises, One Evening

Julian's media showcase had been on Nia's calendar for three weeks.

His documentary class would screen one-minute student portraits at Bellwether Media House on Tuesday. Julian had filmed an elderly barber describing the first haircut he gave after returning from military service, all without spoken narration. He had shown Nia an early cut and asked her to attend the final screening.

"Front row?" he asked Sunday over lunch.

"Front row," she promised.

Two hours later, an Association for Women in Mathematics group chat began filling with panic about Monday's applications exam. The student scheduled to lead the review had lost her voice. Nia knew the material better than most of the first-years and had already made visual notes.

**Can anyone cover Tuesday at six?**

The showcase began at six thirty.

Nia typed **I can do 5:00-6:00**, then stared at the message. If the review ran on time, she could cross campus in fifteen minutes. If students had questions, she could still leave. Somebody else could close.

She sent it.

Within minutes, a flyer appeared in the group chat: **CALCULUS APPLICATIONS EXAM RESCUE REVIEW, TUESDAY 5-6:30, LED BY NIA REYNOLDS.**

"Six thirty?" Nia whispered.

She messaged the organizer to correct the time, but the flyer had already been reposted by two student accounts.

Nia considered telling Julian.

Instead, she decided she would make both.

It did not feel like lying. It felt like optimism with a tight schedule.

## Monday, October 6: The Missing Sentence

The applications exam began after Malik's father called at 12:08 a.m.

Marcel could not sleep. The knee brace rubbed against his stitches, the insurance adjuster had questioned part of the equipment claim, and he wanted to know whether Malik remembered where a receipt folder had been stored.

They talked until one twenty.

Malik studied until two fifteen because stopping felt like letting the call take something else.

On the exam, he handled basic derivatives correctly. Then he reached a linearization question asking him to explain why the approximation should be accurate near the chosen point.

He had practiced the justification: differentiability gives a locally linear model; error generally grows as the input moves farther from the center; the estimate should be evaluated within a reasonable neighborhood.

The words would not arrive.

Malik wrote, **Because the tangent line is close to the function**, then stared at the sentence. Close where? Under what conditions? How did he know?

He could see Dr. Brooks's likely comments before she wrote them.

Across the room, Nia worked steadily. Amara paused over one problem, then continued. The clock moved with cruel consistency.

Malik added a diagram and an incomplete error statement before time was called.

Outside, Nia asked, "How was it?"

He nearly said fine.

"I could not retrieve one justification," he said.

It was a small truth, but at least it was not the locked door.

## Tuesday, October 7: The Flyer

Julian discovered Nia's schedule conflict on a poster outside the union.

His own girlfriend's name appeared in large copper letters beneath **EXAM RESCUE REVIEW**. The end time matched the beginning of the media showcase.

He photographed the flyer and sent it to her with one question: **When were you planning to tell me?**

Nia called immediately.

"The time is wrong," she said.

"So you are not leading it?"

"I am, but only until six."

"The showcase starts at six thirty."

"I can make it."

"That is not what I asked."

Nia looked around the North Star lobby, lowering her voice. "I was going to tell you."

"Before or after I saved you a seat?"

The question felt sharper because she had promised the front row.

"Students need help," she said.

"I am not asking you to abandon them. I am asking why I keep learning your choices after everybody else."

"This is not like rehearsal."

"No. At rehearsal you rearranged your life without telling me. Here you rearranged our plans without telling me. Different direction, same surprise."

Nia's instinct was to list the ways she could still make everything work. Instead she looked at the students beginning to gather.

"You are right," she said. "I should have told you Sunday."

Julian was quiet.

"I will be there," she added.

"I hope so."

The review ended at 6:12. Three students still had questions. Nia handed them to another volunteer and ran across campus.

She entered the showcase as Julian's film ended.

He saw her in the doorway.

The empty front-row seat saw her too.

## Wednesday, October 8: No Rescue Plan

Malik brought the first test, the make-up packet, and his unfinished correction form to Nia.

They sat on a bench behind Jubilee Library, away from the students preparing Cypress Week decorations.

"I got a sixty-four," he said.

"I know. I saw the sheet."

"I have not finished the make-up work. I missed office hours before the storm. After I came back, I told Dr. Brooks about the travel and documentation, but not how far behind I already was."

Nia waited.

Malik had expected questions, schedules, and possibly a color-coded emergency plan.

"That is all?" he asked.

"Do you want me to say something?"

"You usually do."

"I am practicing not turning every hard thing into my assignment."

He looked at the test in his hands. "I rushed because of analytics. Then I hid the score because my parents had just celebrated the roster seat."

"That sounds lonely."

"It was avoidable."

"Those can both be true."

Nia did not tell him whom to email. She did not open her planner. She listened while he explained the midnight calls, the job hours, and the model revision Simone had refused to present.

When he finished, she asked, "What truth have you not told Dr. Brooks yet?"

Malik knew.

"That I cannot do all of it at the level I keep promising."

## Thursday, October 9: Reduced

Dr. Brooks listened without interrupting.

Malik told her about leaving the first test early, missing office hours, the make-up work, North Star shifts, analytics deadlines, and family calls that he kept treating as interruptions rather than part of his actual life.

"What are you asking for?" she said.

Malik had prepared requests for more time. Instead he said, "Help deciding what has to change."

Together they built a documented recovery plan: complete corrections, attend weekly office hours, use one North Star shift break only for food and rest, and schedule make-up work in pieces rather than one impossible weekend.

Then Malik met Dr. Bennett and Simone.

"I need a reduced role until my calculus work stabilizes," he said.

The sentence felt like stepping away from the chair he had fought to earn.

Dr. Bennett nodded. "What can you responsibly own?"

They moved Malik from primary model development to data validation and one smaller decision module. He would remain on the roster but lose some presentation responsibility at the checkpoint.

Simone updated the task board.

"You are not being removed," she said.

"It feels like going backward."

"Sometimes going backward is how you stop walking over a cliff."

Malik looked at the reduced assignment beside his name.

It was not the plan he wanted.

For the first time in weeks, it was a plan that might be true.

---

# Week Eleven: Cypress Week

Cypress Week arrived with a parade banner, three drumlines, seven group chats, and the collective belief that sleep was an event for less spirited universities.

By Friday morning, McCall-Hart had transformed. Indigo and copper ribbons wrapped the lampposts. Alumni posed beneath Founders' Bell. Food vendors lined the edge of Cypress Walk, and every open patch of grass seemed occupied by a service project, reunion tent, dance rehearsal, radio interview, or student selling a shirt that would become vintage as soon as the week ended.

Nia stood outside Hart Student Union studying the official schedule.

Homecoming service day offered five projects: packing pantry boxes, cleaning a river trail, tutoring middle-school students, assembling hygiene kits, and recording oral histories with alumni.

She wanted all five.

Keisha appeared beside her in track warmups. "Choose one."

"I have not said anything."

"Your face signed up for everything."

Nia pointed to the math outreach project. "One. I am choosing one."

"Proud of you."

"That sounded sarcastic."

"It was seventy percent sincere."

Nia wrote the service block in her planner and drew a neat boundary around it. She believed, with the confidence of someone learning the first layer of a lesson, that her overcommitment problem had been solved.

## Friday, October 10: One Project

The math outreach group met at North Star to build game kits for Bellwether middle-school programs.

Nia helped design a coordinate-grid treasure hunt. Students would plot points, solve clues, and uncover a final message. She tested the game with volunteers, caught two ambiguous directions, and resisted joining the neighboring hygiene-kit line when they fell behind.

"Look at me," she told Keisha. "Staying in my lane."

"It has been forty minutes."

"Growth deserves witnesses."

Julian stopped by with a camera crew to collect footage for the homecoming recap. He filmed Nia explaining the game and asked her to hold up the finished kit.

"Perfect," he said after the take.

"Do you have time to eat?"

"Later. Band rehearsal, alumni interview, media check-in, then step-show coverage."

"That is four things."

"Homecoming rules."

Nia started to warn him about overcommitment, then remembered the shape of her own planner.

"We still have Sunday evening?" she asked.

Julian checked his phone. "Absolutely. Seven o'clock. Just us."

He kissed her cheek and hurried toward the next event.

Nia returned to her one project feeling disciplined.

She did not notice that Julian's schedule had become the thing she was trying to manage next.

## Saturday, October 11: Maximum Revenue

Saturday's calculus workshop asked students to design the most profitable homecoming T-shirt order under production limits.

The selling price depended on demand. Printing had a fixed setup cost and a per-shirt cost. Storage space limited the maximum order. Students had to build a revenue function, subtract costs, find critical points, and test the feasible endpoints.

"Finally," Malik said, "a problem willing to identify its constraints."

Nia looked at him. "Was that a joke about our lives?"

"It was a statement about shirts."

"Sure."

With his analytics role reduced and his first make-up block complete, Malik had slept seven hours. The difference showed. He organized the variables, found the derivative, and paused before declaring the critical point optimal.

"We have to test the boundary," he said. "The unconstrained maximum may require more shirts than storage allows."

Amara checked the endpoint values. Nia sketched the profit curve. Their feasible maximum occurred at the storage limit, not at the theoretical peak.

Dr. Brooks asked Malik to explain.

"Optimization does not mean finding the biggest possible value in an imaginary world," he said. "It means finding the best defensible value inside the actual constraints."

The class was quiet for half a beat.

Nia raised one eyebrow. "Still only talking about shirts?"

Malik smiled. "Mostly."

The problem gave him something his real life had refused to offer: boundaries printed clearly enough to respect.

## Sunday, October 12: Homecoming Rules

Sunday evening belonged to Nia and Julian.

At 6:22, Julian texted that an alumni interview was running late. At 6:48, he said the band had added a staging rehearsal. At 7:09, campus media needed him to approve audio before a post went live.

**Can we make it eight?** he asked.

Nia agreed.

At 8:17, she sat alone at Copper Cup with two teas growing watery on the table.

Julian arrived at 8:31 wearing a media credential around his neck and carrying his saxophone case.

"I am sorry," he said, dropping into the chair. "This week is eating me alive."

"You said seven."

"I know."

His phone buzzed. He looked down automatically.

Nia moved the second tea toward him. "Are you here?"

"I am sitting in front of you."

"That was not my question."

Julian put the phone facedown. "Five minutes. I need to answer one production message after that."

Nia almost said no. Then she remembered arriving late to his showcase.

"Fine," she said.

They spent twelve minutes talking about classes and the service project. Then the phone buzzed again. Julian apologized, answered, and began discussing audio levels with someone named Marcus.

Nia watched the ice melt in his tea.

She had assumed Julian would understand her crowded schedule because he was busy too. Julian had assumed Nia would understand his for the same reason.

Understanding did not make either of them feel chosen.

## Monday, October 13: Brilliant and Unreachable

Battle of the Bands filled Ruth Ellis Fieldhouse with sound powerful enough to vibrate the seats.

The Marching Herons entered in indigo uniforms with copper plumes, their opening formation widening like wings across the floor. Julian's alto line cut through the brass. He moved with the same discipline Nia had first admired during summer rehearsal, every step sharp, every turn placed exactly.

Nia stood with Keisha and screamed until speaking became unnecessary.

Julian was brilliant.

After the performance, Nia fought through the concourse toward the band exit. She wanted five minutes: a hug, a private congratulations, proof that the person on the floor and the person in her messages still belonged to the same relationship.

Campus media intercepted Julian first. Then an alumnus asked for a photo. Band leaders called the altos into a debrief. Two first-year musicians pulled him into a group selfie.

Nia waited near the wall.

Julian saw her and mouthed, **One minute.**

Ten minutes later, a media coordinator waved him toward another interview.

He jogged over to Nia, breathless. "You saw it?"

"All of it. You were amazing."

His face lit up. A camera operator called his name.

"I have to--"

"Go," Nia said.

Julian kissed her quickly while people moved around them. A photographer captured the moment. Then he disappeared back into the crowd.

Nia remained against the wall, proud of him and lonely beside his success.

## Tuesday, October 14: Next Man

DJ began the homecoming game fourth on the receiver rotation.

By the second quarter, one player had aggravated a hamstring and another had taken a hard hit. The receivers coach pointed at DJ.

"Cole, X position. Know the adjustment?"

"Yes, sir."

DJ ran onto the field with seventy years of alumni noise rolling down from the stands.

His first route cleared space for another receiver. His second ended with the quarterback scrambling. On the third, McCall-Hart lined up near the twelve-yard line. The corner played inside leverage, expecting the slant.

DJ remembered the adjustment. He released outside, planted, and broke toward the back corner.

The ball arrived over his shoulder.

For a fraction of a second, the entire year narrowed to his hands.

Catch. One foot. Second foot. Boundary.

Touchdown.

Henry Boyd Stadium became sound.

DJ ran toward the student section, then stopped before earning a penalty and celebrated with his teammates instead. Malik jumped from his seat. Nia nearly knocked Keisha's drink over. Imani screamed with both hands around her mouth. Julian, in the band block, played through a grin he could not hide.

McCall-Hart won in the fourth quarter.

In the locker room, DJ received no speech about destiny. The receivers coach moved his magnet two places higher on the depth chart.

"You earned more reps," he said. "Now prove you can block when the ball is not coming."

DJ stared at the board.

Publicly, he had scored the homecoming touchdown. Privately, his reward was more work.

He loved both parts.

## Wednesday, October 15: Full Credit

The game's late fourth-down decision became the subject of campus discussion Wednesday.

With a narrow lead and the ball near midfield, McCall-Hart had attempted the conversion instead of punting. The offense succeeded and drained most of the remaining clock.

At a student analytics event, Dr. Bennett displayed the decision model. Malik's corrected module helped define the range in which the attempt had a favorable expected value.

When a student reporter asked Malik whether his model had won the game, he shook his head.

"No model wins a football game. Simone Hart led the uncertainty revision. Andre and Priya rebuilt missing tags and game-state splits. Coaches combined the analysis with personnel, field conditions, and what they saw during the game. The players executed the play."

The reporter tried again. "But your section supported the call?"

"Our section supported one option within a range. That is different from predicting the result."

Simone stood near the back, listening.

Afterward, she caught Malik by the door. "Good answer."

"It was accurate."

"It also gave people credit."

Malik looked toward DJ, who was retelling the touchdown to anyone who had somehow avoided seeing it online.

"The work got better when it stopped being only mine," he said.

Simone smiled. "Careful. You are becoming tolerable."

## Thursday, October 16: The Perfect Picture

Julian posted the homecoming recap Thursday afternoon.

The video was excellent. Service projects, alumni laughter, band rehearsals, step-show lights, DJ's touchdown, the fourth-down conversion, and Founders' Bell all moved together beneath Julian's narration.

The final image was the photograph from Battle of the Bands: Julian kissing Nia while copper light caught the edge of his uniform.

The caption read: **Homecoming is history, excellence, and the people who make every crowded moment worth it.**

Comments filled with heart symbols and declarations that they were "campus couple goals."

Nia stared at the image. It looked like a perfect week she had not experienced.

She found Julian on Cypress Walk.

"Why did you post that picture?"

His smile faded. "You do not like it?"

"It is beautiful. It is also the only private moment we almost had, and there was a camera in it."

"It was taken at a public event."

"That is exactly the problem."

Julian shifted his bag. "I thought it showed us supporting each other."

"I spent the week waiting for you. The picture makes it look like I got everything I wanted."

"You were late to my showcase."

"I know. I apologized."

"And I understood because you were helping people. Why can you not understand that homecoming is the busiest week of my year?"

"I do understand! I am saying understanding did not make me feel less alone."

Students moved around them, some recognizing the couple from the post as they argued beneath the real trees.

Julian lowered his voice. "Do you want me to delete it?"

Nia hesitated. Deleting the image would not give them the missing time.

"I want you to ask before our relationship becomes content," she said. "And I want us to stop using busy as if it explains everything."

Julian looked down the crowded walk. "I need to think."

"So do I."

They walked away in opposite directions while their perfect photograph continued collecting approval online.

---

# Week Twelve: The Maximum Is Not the Meaning

Dr. Alana Price opened her colloquium with a map and a warning.

The map showed a fictional river town under evacuation. Roads appeared as thin lines. Neighborhoods became colored regions. Small circles represented households, and arrows pointed toward shelters beyond the projected flood zone.

"Suppose I ask you to minimize total evacuation time," Dr. Price said. "What will your model try to do?"

Students offered answers: choose the shortest routes, reduce congestion, distribute traffic, avoid bottlenecks.

Dr. Price clicked to the next slide.

The fastest solution sent most drivers across one bridge, routed residents without cars to pickup points nearly two miles away, and treated a nursing facility like a cluster of identical households.

"The objective function improved," she said. "The evacuation became less humane."

Nia sat forward.

Only a week earlier, she had treated choosing one service project as proof that she understood limits. Then she had spent Cypress Week measuring connection by how many commitments she and Julian could squeeze around each other.

On screen, the route map glowed with an elegant answer to the wrong question.

## Friday, October 17: People Are Not Points

Dr. Price described the information an evacuation model might need beyond distance: vehicle access, mobility, language, medical support, departure timing, road capacity, trust in official messages, and whether a shelter could actually meet people's needs.

"Optimization does not remove values from a decision," she said. "It hides them inside what you choose to maximize, minimize, and constrain."

Malik wrote the sentence in his analytics notebook.

Nia drew three versions of the route map, each centered on a different goal. Fastest total clearance. Fewest households left without transportation. Most robust plan if one road failed.

During questions, she asked, "How do you keep adding human factors without making the model impossible to use?"

Dr. Price nodded. "You do not add everything. You make the choices visible. You involve the people affected. And you resist calling one output *the* optimum when it is only optimal under one set of priorities."

Afterward, Nia and Malik walked down Eliza Moss Hall's steps.

"That felt personal," Nia said.

"Because of the shuttle project?"

"Because I keep optimizing my schedule like every opportunity and every person can be converted into one measure called 'doing enough.'"

Malik looked at her. "That sounds tiring."

"It is. How is reduced analytics?"

"Also tiring. Less impossible."

"A powerful review. Put it on the brochure."

## Saturday, October 18: The Wrong Objective Function

Saturday's calculus workshop began with returned applications exams and the Mean Value Theorem.

Dr. Brooks drew a smooth curve between two endpoints and marked the secant line connecting them. Somewhere between the endpoints, she explained, the theorem guaranteed at least one tangent line with the same slope as that average rate, provided the function was continuous on the closed interval and differentiable inside it.

"The theorem guarantees existence," Amara said. "It does not tell us where the point is."

"Or whether there is only one," Malik added.

"Exactly," Dr. Brooks said. "Conditions matter. Conclusions have boundaries."

Their test-correction forms required more than replacing wrong answers. Students had to identify the first unsupported step. Nia had earned a strong score but lost points on an optimization problem because she found a critical point and never tested one feasible endpoint. Malik had improved from his first test, yet his linearization explanation still assumed that *nearby* automatically meant *accurate enough*.

They corrected the arguments at North Star. Nia wrote that a critical point was a candidate, not a crown. Malik added an explicit error check and the interval over which his approximation remained useful.

Only after finishing did Nia return to the larger optimization problem waiting in her room.

Nia spread her commitments across the dorm-room floor.

Class deadlines. Math outreach. AWM meetings. Accountability check-ins. Residence activities. Julian's band calendar. Julian's media calendar. The private study blocks Keisha had made her protect.

Keisha stepped around the paper without disturbing it. "Are we solving a crime?"

"Possibly mine."

Nia had been proud of choosing one service project. But the paper showed the deeper pattern. She did not merely say yes to too many events. She tried to maximize how useful she was inside each relationship.

If Malik struggled, she wanted to build the recovery plan. If a club needed a leader, she wanted to become the answer. If Julian was busy, she stretched herself around his schedule, then expected him to notice the stretching.

"I keep maximizing commitments," she said. "And minimizing attention."

Keisha sat on her bed. "Attention to what?"

"The work. The people. Myself. Pick one."

"That sounds like the same problem three times."

Nia placed a blank sheet in the center of the floor and wrote: **What is the goal?**

For academics: learn deeply, not appear endlessly capable.

For service: contribute where she had responsibility, not collect proof that she cared.

For Julian: build a relationship they could actually experience, not one that looked full on a calendar.

"Are you going to call him?" Keisha asked.

Nia looked at the last line. "Not until I know whether I am apologizing or trying to win the argument."

"That may be the smartest thing you have said all semester."

"Rude."

"Accurate."

## Sunday, October 19: Honest, Not Certain

Malik and Simone rebuilt the fourth-down model around intervals.

Instead of one bright recommendation, the interface displayed a range of expected outcomes under different assumptions. A broad neutral band showed scenarios where no option clearly dominated. Users could examine how the recommendation changed when possession value, weather, opponent strength, and sample quality changed.

"It is less dramatic," Malik said.

"Good," Simone replied. "We are submitting analysis, not auditioning for a game show."

The regional semifinal rubric rewarded technical quality, communication, and usefulness. A single answer might look stronger in a screenshot, but any judge could expose its fragility by changing one assumption.

Malik added a note explaining where formation tags remained incomplete.

"Do you think this can advance?" he asked.

Simone rotated her chair toward him. "Yes."

"How likely?"

"That is a different question."

"Do you have a range?"

"I have several opinions disguised as one."

He smiled.

They reviewed the limitations again. Malik no longer felt that every uncertainty label weakened his contribution. Some made the work stronger because they showed the user where judgment had to begin.

"You look more rested," Simone said.

"Seven hours last night."

"A revolutionary methodology."

"Results are preliminary."

Their laughter came easily. Neither treated it as more than the growing trust between teammates, though the warmth stayed with Malik after the screen went dark.

## Monday, October 20: No Camera

Julian invited Nia to dinner at a small table behind Copper Cup, away from the main terrace.

There was no camera. His phone stayed in his bag.

"I took the photo out of the homecoming recap," he said.

Nia had noticed. "You did not have to erase the whole moment."

"I know. But I posted it without asking, and after our argument I did not want the image speaking for us."

He looked tired in a way stage lights usually concealed.

"I am sorry," he said. "I turned our relationship into a closing shot because it made the week feel complete. The week was not complete. We barely saw each other."

Nia traced the edge of her napkin. "Thank you."

"I also used your being late to the showcase as evidence that I was allowed to be unavailable all homecoming. I kept score instead of saying I missed you."

"I did miss your film."

"You missed most of it."

"I am trying to apologize."

He smiled slightly. "Sorry. Continue."

Nia explained the review commitment and the moment she chose not to tell him. She admitted that she had believed the schedule would work because she wanted to be both the dependable tutor and the supportive girlfriend.

"Then when it failed," she said, "I treated being helpful as if it made the promise to you less real. It did not."

Julian reached across the table. "I do not want to compete with everybody who needs you."

"You should not have to."

They held hands in the quiet corner, relieved to be speaking honestly and not yet aware that honesty could identify a mismatch without resolving it.

## Tuesday, October 21: One Evening

Nia and Julian met again Tuesday to make the repair practical.

They chose one private evening each week. No media coverage, club leadership, tutoring, band extras, or relationship posts without discussion. Emergencies could change the plan, but ordinary busyness could not quietly consume it.

"Thursday?" Julian asked.

Nia checked her schedule. "Thursday. Seven to nine."

"Protected."

"Protected."

Then Nia added her own apology.

"I have been acting like love means endless flexibility," she said. "If I move everything for you, I feel devoted. If you move everything for me, I feel chosen. That is not fair to either of us."

Julian considered her words. "I like that you show up."

"I like that you make moments feel important."

"Those are good things."

"Yes. Until they become the only way we know how to care."

He nodded, though the answer seemed more complicated than the calendar block they had just created.

They walked Cypress Walk together without taking a picture.

For the first time since homecoming, the relationship felt quiet enough to hear.

## Wednesday, October 22: Submit

The Heron Analytics regional submission was due at 8:00 p.m.

At 6:15, Andre found a mislabeled formation. At 6:42, Priya corrected a legend. At 7:03, Dr. Bennett removed a paragraph that claimed more transferability than the evidence supported. At 7:21, the export broke one of Simone's interval labels.

Nobody panicked gracefully.

Malik rebuilt the label. Simone checked the final narrative against the charts. Andre and Priya verified the source table. Dr. Bennett read the limitations aloud.

At 7:47, Simone placed the cursor over **Submit**.

"Last objections?"

Malik looked at the model. It did not promise the best fourth-down answer. It showed when evidence favored an option, when the range widened, and when a coach should treat the model as one input among several.

It was honest.

It was competitive.

It might lose.

"Submit," he said.

Simone clicked.

The confirmation page appeared.

For three seconds, the room remained silent. Then Andre cheered, Priya dropped her head onto the table, and Simone raised both hands as if surrendering to the deadline.

Malik took a picture of the team, not the model.

He sent it to his parents with the message: **Regional submission complete. Team effort.**

Celeste replied with six celebration symbols. Marcel wrote: **Proud of you. Call when you can. Repair update not good.**

Joy and worry occupied the same screen.

## Thursday, October 23: A Larger Map

Dr. Price's email arrived Thursday morning.

**DELTA NATIONAL LABORATORY STUDENT TOUR: APPLICATIONS OPEN**

The visit offered a limited number of seats for students interested in applied mathematics, computing, risk, energy, and environmental modeling. Applicants needed a short statement explaining what question they hoped to explore.

Nia found Malik outside calculus reading the message.

"Are you applying?" she asked.

"Yes."

"That was fast."

"The lab studies risk modeling."

"It also has scientific visualization and community systems."

They looked at each other.

"Accountability application session?" Nia asked.

"Saturday. Two hours. No disappearing into other commitments."

"You have become aggressive about constraints."

"Evidence supports them."

They agreed to apply separately and review each other's statements only after drafting their own.

Malik wanted to ask how things were with Julian. Nia wanted to ask what Marcel's repair update meant. Both sensed the other question and let it wait until they had more privacy.

Ahead of them, the Delta visit offered a wider world: supercomputers, scientists, models connected to consequences larger than a classroom or a football field.

Behind them, the house repairs had stalled, and one protected evening could not prove a relationship was healed.

The next opportunity did not erase the current problems.

It gave them a larger map on which to carry them.

---

# Week Thirteen: Beyond the Campus Map

The insurance company wanted proof that a drill press had existed before water destroyed it.

Malik read the request during his Friday break at North Star. Around him, students ate vending-machine dinners and argued quietly over derivatives. On his laptop, a claims portal listed six items as **NEEDS ADDITIONAL DOCUMENTATION**.

Drill press. Air compressor. Power saw. Custom tool cabinet. Two small appliances. School-record storage.

His father had owned the drill press for eleven years. It had repaired lawn equipment, shaped replacement parts, and helped Marcel earn money from jobs too small for larger shops to accept. It appeared in family photos only as a gray shape in the background.

Malik called home.

"They need a receipt," he said.

"From eleven years ago?" Marcel asked.

"Or a dated photo, bank record, warranty, repair invoice, anything showing model and ownership."

"It was in my shop. The water did not ask for paperwork before ruining it."

Malik closed his eyes.

"I know."

"Do you?"

The question was unfair. It also came from a man looking at the damaged equipment that had once made him independent.

Malik opened a new evidence folder.

## Friday, October 24: Evidence of a Life

During his twenty-minute break, Malik searched years of family messages.

He found a photograph from Micah's tenth birthday. In the background, Marcel stood beside the drill press cutting a replacement bracket before the party.
