Another image showed the tool cabinet during a garage cleanup. A bank statement contained a payment to a repair shop that had serviced the compressor.

Malik renamed each file with dates and item descriptions. He wrote short notes connecting the images to the inventory.

Ms. Alvarez passed the desk carrying returned calculators. "Eat."

"I have fourteen minutes."

"Which is enough time to eat and not enough time to solve insurance."

He opened the sandwich Nia had made him buy before his shift.

The claims process wanted a clean history for every object. Families did not live that way. They bought used tools in cash, repaired appliances instead of replacing them, inherited furniture, misplaced receipts, and assumed the things surrounding them would not someday need to prove themselves.

Malik submitted the new evidence.

The portal changed from **NEEDS ADDITIONAL DOCUMENTATION** to **UNDER REVIEW**.

It was not approval.

It was movement.

## Saturday, October 25: The Missing Constant

Dr. Brooks introduced antiderivatives by erasing part of a story.

She drew a graph of velocity over time, then asked the class to recover a possible position function.

Students reversed the derivative rules. Malik integrated the polynomial terms. Nia checked by differentiating the result.

"Are we finished?" Dr. Brooks asked.

"Plus C," Amara said.

Dr. Brooks wrote the constant of integration in a box.

The rate of change could reveal how position evolved, but not where the object began. Without an initial value, an entire family of histories fit the same derivative.

"Same rate, different starting point," Nia said.

"Exactly. If I give you one position at one time, you can select a particular history. Without it, do not pretend the rate tells you everything."

Dr. Brooks gave them an initial-value problem: a shuttle's velocity was known, along with its position at noon. Malik found the family of antiderivatives, used the noon position to determine the missing constant, and checked the result by differentiating. Nia drew two candidate position curves that shared the same rate but began miles apart.

"The derivative tells us how the story moves," she said. "The initial value tells us which story we are in."

For the final question, they computed accumulated change across an interval and added it to the starting position. The process felt almost investigative: recover what could not be observed directly from the rate that remained.

Malik thought about the insurance inventory. The current loss did not reveal the family's starting point: which objects were inherited, repaired, or essential to income. Numbers showing damage could not reconstruct every history unless someone supplied the missing constants.

After class, Nia reviewed Malik's Delta application statement one last time.

"You wrote about the storm," she said.

"The lab studies risk."

"You also wrote about your father's equipment."

"Risk is financial too."

Her own statement focused on visualizing evacuation choices so communities could question assumptions instead of merely receiving an official map.

Both had been selected.

The bus left before dawn Sunday.

## Sunday, October 26: Beyond the Gate

At 4:50 a.m., Hart Student Union looked like a gathering of ghosts carrying travel mugs.

Malik arrived with a backpack organized by category. Nia arrived wearing two different socks and blamed the hour.

"They are both blue," she said.

"One is indigo. One is navy."

"At five in the morning, all colors are rumors."

Dr. Price checked names against the travel list. The selected group boarded the bus for Delta National Laboratory, a federal research campus several hours from Bellwether.

Nia texted Julian: **Boarding now. I will call after orientation.**

He replied with a heart and a request for photos if security rules allowed them.

By the time the bus reached Delta's guarded entrance, phones had to be silenced and stored during security orientation. Students received visitor badges, data-handling rules, photography restrictions, and a direct warning that curiosity did not override consent or clearance.

Julian messaged twice during the long briefing.

Nia did not see either message.

Malik did not see his mother's update that the adjuster had delayed a decision.

Inside the laboratory, their ordinary campus concerns did not disappear. They waited beyond the gate while the students entered a world of controlled rooms, wall-sized displays, and questions too large for one spreadsheet.

## Monday, October 27: Water in Motion

Dr. Arturo Vega led the storm-surge demonstration.

On a visualization wall, a model coastline appeared beneath layers of wind, pressure, elevation, and water depth. The simulation advanced. Blue spread inland through channels, across low roads, and into neighborhoods that looked safe on a simple distance map.

Nia moved closer. "Can you separate the layers?"

Dr. Vega toggled rainfall, river flow, and coastal surge. "The image is not decoration. Each layer should answer a question. Too many at once can make uncertainty look like authority."

Malik asked how the model represented household-level loss.

"It does not, directly," Dr. Vega said. "We estimate physical exposure. Economists, public-health experts, emergency managers, and communities help interpret consequences. A water depth is not the same loss for every household."

He displayed two homes under the same projected depth. One had flood-resistant materials and savings. The other housed a family with limited transportation and no easy place to relocate.

"Same hazard," Malik said. "Different vulnerability."

"Exactly."

Nia began sketching a visual that paired route options with uncertainty and access needs. Malik filled a page with questions about repair delays, work interruption, and how models valued equipment used for income.

They approached the simulation from different directions and kept arriving at the same point: a forecast mattered only through the decisions people could make with it.

## Tuesday, October 28: Failure Is Information

Dr. Lena Washington began her presentation with photographs of models that had failed.

She was a Black applied mathematician who led a validation group at Delta. One slide showed a flood map that underestimated water movement through an industrial canal. Another showed a traffic model that performed well in simulation and badly during a real evacuation.

"Nobody invites you to a national laboratory to admire your perfect first draft," she said. "We need you to find where a model stops deserving trust."

Students laughed, then became quiet as she described validation: compare predictions with observations, test extreme cases, examine transferability, document bias, and revise or reject the model when evidence demanded it.

Malik thought of the missing-data chart and the calculus score he had hidden.

During questions, he asked, "How do you keep a failed model from feeling like a verdict on whether you belong in the work?"

Dr. Washington studied him for a moment.

"You separate accountability from identity," she said. "If your method failed, you must name it, learn from it, and protect people from the consequences. But a failed test is information about the work. It is not a complete measurement of the worker."

Nia wrote the answer down word for word.

Malik did not. He wanted to remember it without turning it into another line he could hide behind.

## Wednesday, October 29: Work That Matters

On the ride back to Bellwether, Nia and Malik sat across the aisle from each other, talking over the low engine noise while most of the bus slept.

"I used to think applied math meant getting the classroom answer into the real world," Nia said. "Now it feels like the real world changes what counts as an answer."

Malik looked at the photographs he had been allowed to take: a public lobby display, the group beneath the Delta sign, Nia pointing at a permitted visualization poster.

"My family had forecasts," he said. "They still lost things. I keep wondering whether a better model would have changed anything."

"Maybe not everything."

"That is the problem."

"Or maybe that is the honest reason to do it. Not to promise no loss. To give people more time, clearer choices, or one less surprise."

Malik looked out at the dark highway. "One less surprise is not nothing."

They talked about majors, summer research, and the terrifying possibility that the questions they cared about might become careers. The conversation felt intense without feeling romantic to either of them. It was the closeness of two young people recognizing that their private fears could become public work.

Nia posted three approved photos after the bus returned. One showed her and Malik laughing beneath the Delta visitor sign.

Julian saw that image before he heard her voice.

## Thursday, October 30: What He Was Asking

Julian met Nia outside Bellwether Media House.

"You did not call," he said.

"Security orientation ran long. Then we had sessions, and the hotel Wi-Fi was terrible. I texted when I could."

"You posted."

"After we got back."

"With Malik."

Nia's patience tightened. "There were six students in the group."

"The picture is you and Malik."

"So this is jealousy."

Julian looked away. "Partly."

The honesty should have slowed her. Instead, she was tired and already defending herself.

"Malik is my friend. I am not going to apologize for having a meaningful conversation with someone who understands the work."

"I did not ask you to apologize."

"You are acting like the photo proves something."

"I am acting like a big part of your life happened, and I learned about it through the same post as everybody else. Again."

Nia opened her mouth, then stopped.

Julian continued. "I do not understand the math the way he does. I wanted you to tell me what mattered. I wanted a way into the story."

"You could have asked without making it about Malik."

"I am asking now, badly."

There it was: jealousy, yes, but also exclusion. Nia heard only the first part clearly enough to answer.

"I cannot keep proving our relationship by making my friendships smaller," she said.

Julian's face closed. "And I cannot keep pretending I do not notice where you feel most understood."

They separated without discussing the laboratory at all.

The trip had expanded Nia's world. The argument made her wonder why bringing that world home felt so difficult.

---

# Week Fourteen: Riemann Sums and Missing Pieces

Nia wore a copper cape to teach Riemann sums.

The cape was not mathematically necessary. She had found it in a costume bin at North Star and decided an outreach night held on Halloween deserved commitment.

At her table, middle-school students received irregular paper masks shaped like heron wings. A square grid lay beneath each mask. Their challenge was to estimate the area using rectangles, then compare estimates made with different widths.

"The mask has curves," a seventh grader named Zoe said. "Rectangles do not fit."

"Exactly," Nia replied. "So we let them fit imperfectly on purpose."

She showed how left-endpoint rectangles missed some regions and covered others that were not part of the mask. Smaller rectangles followed the shape more closely.

"So we are wrong a lot of times," Zoe said, "but in smaller pieces?"

Nia laughed. "That is one way to describe numerical approximation. We make the error visible, then refine it."

Across the room, paper bats hung from the ceiling, volunteers dressed as famous scientists, and Malik helped students calculate candy-bag volume while wearing the smallest possible concession to costume: an indigo tie printed with tiny silver equations.

For two hours, the semester's tension loosened.

Nia remembered that mathematics could feel like an invitation.

## Friday, October 31: Smaller Rectangles

The first outreach session ended with masks taped across the wall in a bright flock.

Zoe's group compared estimates using four, eight, and sixteen rectangles. The numbers approached a stable range without becoming perfectly exact.

"Which estimate is the answer?" another student asked.

"The best one we can defend with this method," Nia said. "And we should say how we made it."

She pushed the idea one step farther. If they kept shrinking the rectangle width while increasing the number of rectangles, the sums could approach a definite integral: an exact mathematical object built from the limit of approximations.

"But our measurements are still imperfect," Zoe said.

"Right. The integral does not magically improve the data we started with. A precise method can still produce a questionable total if pieces are missing."

They tested that warning by removing two width measurements from the mask. One group filled the gaps using nearby values; another left the region uncounted. Their totals differed enough to matter.

Nia wrote both results on the board and labeled the assumptions. Estimating a total from incomplete data was not only about adding rectangles. It was about reporting which pieces had been observed, which had been inferred, and how those choices changed the answer.

She heard Malik's influence in the sentence.

He heard it too. "Reasonable."

"That is the nicest thing you have ever said about my teaching."

"It is not."

"Let me have the moment."

The two of them reset the table for Saturday's family session. Nia told Malik about Dr. Washington's description of model failure. Malik told her the insurance company had accepted two pieces of documentation and requested more for the compressor.

Neither solved the other's problem. The conversation still helped.

When Nia checked her phone, Julian had sent an apology for the laboratory argument and a note saying he would film Saturday's outreach session.

She replied: **Come see the cape before judging it.**

His response arrived immediately: **I am already judging the cape. Affectionately.**

For one evening, the joke felt like them again.

## Saturday, November 1: The Edge in the Joke

Julian arrived forty minutes late to the family outreach session.

Band rehearsal had run long, and campus media had needed equipment returned before he could collect the camera. By the time he entered North Star, Nia and Malik were demonstrating how the rectangle estimates changed with partition size.

Julian filmed from the doorway.

Zoe returned with her mother and pointed at them. "Those are the two math people."

Julian stepped closer. "They do come as a matched set."

His tone was light enough for the family to laugh.

Nia heard the edge.

Malik did too.

"We are accountability partners," Nia said.

"I know," Julian replied, still smiling behind the camera. "Very accountable."

The session moved on, but the joke stayed in the room. Nia became overly formal with Malik. Malik moved to another table. Julian filmed more footage than necessary and disliked himself for every extra minute.

After the families left, he lowered the camera.

"That came out wrong."

"It came out exactly how you meant it for about two seconds," Nia said. "Then you regretted it."

Julian rubbed one hand over the back of his neck. "Maybe."

"I am not doing this here."

She carried the mask supplies toward storage.

Malik watched her go, then looked at Julian. "Can we talk?"

## Sunday, November 2: Not a Competition

Malik met Julian outside the union Sunday afternoon.

He had no interest in winning an argument about Nia. Winning would be the wrong goal.

"I am not trying to interfere in your relationship," Malik said.

Julian leaned against the terrace rail. "I did not say you were."

"You implied that our friendship needs explaining."

"You two have a connection."

"Yes. We are friends. We study together. We care about similar work."

"And you have feelings for her."

Malik's silence answered too much.

Julian looked away toward Cypress Walk. "I am not imagining everything."

"No," Malik said carefully. "But Nia is with you. I have respected that. She should not have to make our friendship smaller to prove your relationship is real."

"Easy for you to say. You get to be the understanding friend while I am the jealous boyfriend."

"I am not especially understanding. Ask anyone."

Julian almost smiled.

Malik continued. "This is not a competition I am entering. If you are worried about what Nia needs, ask her. Do not make her defend me."

"And what do you need?"

Malik thought of the photo beneath the Delta sign, the bus conversation, and every private hope he had treated like patience.

"Better boundaries," he said.

It was not a complete answer. It was the answer he could responsibly give.

## Monday, November 3: The Drop

The rivalry game against Bayland State began with DJ dropping a pass.

The ball hit both hands on a short third-down route and fell to the turf. The crowd groaned. DJ returned to the sideline with every camera in the stadium apparently pointed at his failure.

His receivers coach met him near the bench. "What happened?"

"Looked upfield before I secured it."

"Fix your eyes. Next play."

No speech. No punishment. No promise of another target.

DJ spent two quarters blocking, running clear-out routes, and trying not to beg the quarterback with his body language.

In the fourth quarter, McCall-Hart trailed by four. On fourth-and-five, DJ ran a crossing route beneath the linebackers. The pass arrived low. He bent, caught it cleanly, and absorbed the tackle beyond the marker.

First down.

The drive stayed alive. Two plays later, McCall-Hart scored the winning touchdown.

DJ's conversion did not erase the drop. The drop did not erase the conversion. Both belonged to the same game and the same player.

In the locker room, he texted Malik: **I HAVE EXPERIENCED THE FULL EMOTIONAL RANGE OF RECEIVING.**

Malik replied: **Secure the message before looking upfield.**

DJ sent back twelve angry symbols and saved the text anyway.

## Tuesday, November 4: Advance

The Heron Analytics team received its regional decision at 10:03 Tuesday morning.

**Selected: Regional Semifinal Presentation**

Simone read the message aloud in the lab. Andre shouted. Priya covered her face. Dr. Bennett permitted exactly thirty seconds of noise before asking when the presentation file was due.

Malik opened the schedule.

Nine days.

His first instinct was not celebration. It was calculation.

Presentation revision, rehearsals, classes, North Star shifts, family calls, make-up work, travel preparation. Nine days became blocks, then hours, then a narrowing path between obligations.

Simone closed his laptop.

"We advanced," she said. "Experience the event before you convert it into a countdown."

Malik looked around the room. The model had survived review because the team showed uncertainty instead of hiding it. They had earned the chance to be questioned in person.

He allowed himself one full minute.

Then he reopened the schedule.

"Hopeless," Simone said.

"Prepared."

"Those words are cousins, not twins."

## Wednesday, November 5: Public Laughter

The basketball exhibition at Ruth Ellis Fieldhouse introduced the men's and women's teams before the season.

Nia attended with Julian and several of his media friends. She wanted an ordinary night: snacks, music, friendly arguments about which team had the better entrance.

Instead, one of Julian's friends mentioned the Delta photo.

"Your girl looked real happy at the lab," Marcus joked. "Math husband in the frame and everything."

Nia waited for Julian to stop it.

He laughed once. "Apparently accountability is a full-contact partnership."

The group laughed.

Nia did not.

"Can we not?" she said.

Julian noticed her expression. "It is a joke."

"It is our argument performed for your friends."

"Nobody knows our argument."

"They know enough to laugh at me."

Marcus began apologizing, but Julian tried to smooth the moment with another joke about mathematicians taking everything literally.

Nia stood.

"Where are you going?"

"Somewhere I do not have to become content before I can have a feeling."

She left before the women's team scrimmage began.

Julian remained in his seat, surrounded by friends who suddenly found the court very interesting.

## Thursday, November 6: Known in Private

Keisha found Nia sitting on the floor between their beds, still wearing the exhibition wristband.

"Do you need soft or useful?" Keisha asked.

"Soft first."

Keisha sat beside her without speaking.

After a while, Nia said, "Julian tells people he is proud of me. He posts me. He introduces me like I am brilliant. Everybody thinks he adores me."

"Does he?"

"I think so."

"That was not the whole question."

Nia pulled at the paper wristband. "Being loved in public is not helping me feel known in private."

She explained the joke, the friends, and how quickly Julian had protected the mood of the group instead of the dignity of their conversation.

"I also keep score," Nia admitted. "I count every late arrival and missed date. Then I act like I am above the performance because I am the one hurt by it."

Keisha leaned back against the bed. "Do you want the relationship, or do you want to prove it can work?"

Nia's eyes filled.

"I do not know anymore."

Outside, campus workers removed the last Halloween decorations. Ordinary November returned, carrying a withdrawal deadline, a semifinal, and choices that could no longer be disguised as scheduling problems.

---

# Week Fifteen: What Can Still Be Changed

During advising week, honesty spread across campus like a weather system.

Students who had spent months saying "good" and "busy" began using more precise language. A chemistry major admitted she was failing lab. A senior learned one missing prerequisite could delay graduation. Two roommates compared emergency-aid forms over lunch. Outside Eliza Moss Hall, an adviser explained to a tearful student that withdrawing from one course was not the same as withdrawing from a future.

The deadline sharpened every conversation: remain, withdraw, change strategy, accept the grade, ask for help.

Malik sat across from Dr. Brooks with his corrected first test, completed make-up quiz, and applications-exam feedback arranged in a careful stack.

"Your work is improving," she said. "Your schedule is not."

He had earned back some credit through corrections. He attended office hours. His explanations now showed the reasoning he once assumed answers could carry alone.

He was also working later, preparing for the analytics semifinal, and answering family calls whenever the insurance company produced a new request.

"Can I stay in the course?" Malik asked.

"Yes," Dr. Brooks said. "The question is whether you will continue making the changes that allow you to succeed in it."

Remaining was not a passive decision.

## Friday, November 7: Stay, Leave, Change

Nia attended her own advising appointment expecting praise and received a question instead.

"What are you protecting in your schedule?" her adviser asked.

Nia named calculus, outreach, sleep, and one weekly evening with Julian.

"And what are you protecting emotionally?"

That answer was harder.

Across campus, DJ met with an athletics academic adviser who reminded him that increased playing time did not lower assignment expectations. Keisha adjusted a programming-course deadline around travel for her first indoor-track preparation meet. Students were not choosing between being successful and unsuccessful. They were choosing which consequences they could responsibly carry.

At North Star that evening, Malik checked in a student who had just withdrawn from calculus.

"I feel like everybody is going to know," the student said while returning a calculator.

Malik looked around the busy lobby. "Most people are trying to survive their own decisions."

"Did you ever think about dropping?"

"Yes."

It was not entirely true, but he had thought about whether the course would drop him.

"I am staying," Malik added. "That means I have to change how I am doing it."

The student nodded. "I am leaving this one so I can change how I do the next one."

They handed over the calculator like a small ceremonial object.

## Saturday, November 8: More Hours

Marcel's orthopedic appointment ended with another delay.

The knee had improved, but not enough for him to return to repair work safely. He needed physical therapy, more healing time, and a follow-up evaluation.

"How long?" Malik asked over the phone.

"They do not know," Celeste said.

"What did the doctor estimate?"

"Weeks, not days."

Malik opened the family budget. Reduced income had begun to eat through the emergency balance. Temporary aid helped with immediate costs, but insurance had not resolved the equipment claim and repairs required payments before reimbursements.

He emailed Ms. Alvarez and requested two additional North Star shifts.

Then he told Dr. Bennett he could still complete his semifinal rehearsal section.

Dr. Bennett read the schedule Malik had submitted.

"No," he said.

"The shifts are evening hours."

"Your body does not distinguish paid hours from academic hours when deciding whether it needs sleep."

"My family needs the money."

"I am not telling you not to work. I am telling you the current analytics assignment must shrink if work increases."

Malik agreed to remove one data task.

Afterward, he accepted both additional shifts and kept a smaller task Dr. Bennett thought had been reassigned.

It was not a dramatic lie. It was a quiet failure to subtract.

## Sunday, November 9: Accumulated Loss

The Fundamental Theorem of Calculus connected rate and accumulation.

Dr. Brooks drew a rate function, shaded the area beneath it, and showed how a definite integral measured net change over an interval. Then she differentiated an accumulation function and returned to the original rate.

"These ideas are not strangers," she said. "Accumulation and instantaneous change describe the same process from different directions."

Then she introduced substitution through an accumulation problem whose rate contained a function nested inside its own derivative. Instead of expanding everything, the class renamed the inner expression, transformed the differential, and recognized a familiar antiderivative.

"Substitution is not a trick for making symbols disappear," Dr. Brooks said. "It identifies the structure governing the change."

Nia checked the result by differentiating. Malik evaluated the endpoints and interpreted the sign of the net change rather than reporting only its magnitude. A positive contribution in one part of the interval and a negative contribution in another both affected the final total.

Malik understood the proof.

He struggled with the example.

A small negative rate sustained over time produced a large total loss. On paper, the effect appeared clean. At home, losses had accumulated through missed workdays, replacement meals, transportation, medical copays, cleaning supplies, temporary storage, and damaged equipment. No single cost had destroyed the budget. The area beneath all of them had.

Nia saw him stop writing.

"Home?" she asked.

"Every day my father cannot work is another change in the total."

"Do you want to talk after class?"

He looked at the semifinal notes in his bag. "I have rehearsal."

Nia nodded. She did not ask him to cancel.

"Later, then."

Malik appreciated the offer and the absence of pressure. He also recognized how easily *later* became his hiding place.

## Monday, November 10: Three Slides Too Many

Malik's semifinal section contained fourteen slides.

He presented the source data, missingness audit, model structure, sensitivity analysis, uncertainty intervals, opponent adjustments, decision thresholds, edge cases, limitations, and three alternative visualizations.

At minute eight, he had not yet stated what decision the model supported.

Simone held up one hand. "Stop."

Malik stopped mid-sentence.

She walked to the display and deleted three slides from the sequence.

"Those explain the sensitivity design," he said.

"The appendix explains it."

"A judge may question the intervals."

"Then we answer the question. Right now your need to prove rigor is burying the decision."

"If I simplify it, the model may sound weaker."

"If nobody knows what the model is for, rigor becomes camouflage."

Andre looked down at his notes. Priya pretended to adjust the timer.

Malik stared at the missing slides.

Simone softened her voice. "Lead with the decision context. Show the range. Name the limitations. Let the judges ask for depth. You do not have to force every piece of evidence through the front door at once."

He thought of his schedule, his family calls, his unfinished task, and every fact he used to prove he was not falling apart.

"Again," Simone said.

Malik began from the first slide.

This time, the decision appeared before minute two.

## Tuesday, November 11: The Score Between Them

McCall-Hart marked Veterans Day with a morning observance near Founders' Bell. Students read names from the university's history, the band played a restrained arrangement, and Julian recorded oral histories with alumni who had served.

That evening belonged to Nia and Julian.

Their protected time had survived on the calendar. It did not survive contact with everything they had not said.

They ordered dinner from a small restaurant off campus and sat on the Jubilee Library terrace.

For ten minutes, the evening was gentle.

Then Julian asked why Nia had told Keisha about the basketball argument before speaking to him.

"Because I needed to understand what I felt," Nia said.

"But when I talk to Marcus, it becomes public performance."

"You joked about our conflict in front of him. I talked privately to my roommate."

"After you left me sitting there in front of everybody."

"You made me the joke."

They began counting.

Nia counted the late homecoming date, the missed five minutes after Battle of the Bands, the photograph, the Delta argument, and the exhibition.

Julian counted the missed showcase, unanswered lab messages, the photo with Malik, and every time Nia treated his media work as less serious than her academic commitments.

"I never said your work was less serious," she said.

"You do not have to say it. You explain your work to Malik. You tell me the headline."

"Because when I try, you turn the story into how it makes you look."

The protected evening gave them privacy. They used most of it to present evidence against each other.

By nine, the food was cold and neither felt heard.

## Wednesday, November 12: No Performance

Nia and Julian met beside the closed radio booth Wednesday afternoon.

No camera. No friends. No beautiful campus backdrop.

"We keep apologizing," Julian said, "and then using the apology as proof the next problem should not count."

Nia nodded. "We fix the event and keep the pattern."

"Do you want to break up?"

The directness hurt.

"I do not know," she said.

"Neither do I."

They agreed to several days apart. No relationship posts, no public explanation, no friends sent as messengers. They would attend their responsibilities and decide what they wanted when the argument was not still speaking for them.

Julian took her hand once before leaving.

Nia returned to the residence hall and told Keisha, "We are taking space."

Keisha opened her arms.

Nia stepped into the hug.

For once, nobody asked her to explain the decision immediately.

## Thursday, November 13: What Support Looks Like

Malik noticed Nia's silence during calculus.

She answered when called on and took complete notes, but none of her usual side comments appeared in the margins of his page. After class, she left with Keisha instead of waiting for him.

DJ knew enough from Imani to say only, "Relationship situation. Do not be weird."

"That instruction lacks operational detail."

"It means do not turn into a romance speech while she is hurting."

Malik had an analytics rehearsal in twenty minutes. His instinct pulled in two directions: abandon the rehearsal to prove he cared, or attend it and pretend he had not noticed.

He chose neither performance.

He sent Nia one message:

**I can tell today is hard. I have rehearsal until seven. What support do you want after: food, company, help with calculus, or space? No response needed right away.**

Then he silenced his phone and entered the lab.

At 6:42, during a scheduled break, he checked.

Nia had replied: **Company. No fixing. Maybe fries. Good luck at rehearsal.**

Malik wrote back: **Fries are within scope.**

He returned to the presentation.

Nia was hurting. Malik did not use the hurt as an opening. He kept the commitment he had already made and offered support on her terms.

Within days, the analytics team would learn whether honest uncertainty could survive a semifinal. Nia and Julian would learn whether affection could survive an honest decision.

For one evening, Malik focused on the next responsible thing.

---

# Week Sixteen: Two Honest Losses

The radio booth sounded different when it was off.

No music filled the pauses. No control-board lights blinked beneath Julian's hands. No red **ON AIR** sign gave either of them permission to perform.

Nia sat in the guest chair where she had corrected his domain-and-range joke in August. Julian sat at the console, turned away from the microphone.

Outside the glass, Bellwether Media House moved through a normal Friday. Students carried cameras down the hall. Somebody laughed near the editing rooms. A printer made the same grinding noise every thirty seconds.

Inside, Nia and Julian faced the question they had postponed for several days.

"I missed you this week," Julian said.

"I missed you too."

The answer did not solve anything.

Friday was the final day to withdraw from fall courses, and campus had spent the morning discussing which difficult things could still be left. Nia had not expected the question to follow her into the booth.

## Friday, November 14: Compatible

Julian placed both hands flat on the desk.

"I keep thinking about the good parts," he said. "The first rehearsal. Copper Cup. The game. You making fun of my math on air."

"Your math deserved it."

He smiled. So did she.

Then the smile hurt.

Nia remembered his first kiss, the way he asked permission. She remembered him giving verified storm information instead of chasing attention. She remembered the copper tape around his wrist, quiet dinners, and the care he took with stories when he was not trying to make their relationship into one.

"We do love each other," she said.

Julian looked down. "I think so."

"That sounds uncertain."

"Not about caring. About what word makes this better instead of harder."

Nia understood.

They needed different things from closeness. Nia wanted private conversation deep enough to make her feel known. Julian experienced connection through shared moments, visible celebration, and being welcomed into the stories that mattered. Under pressure, she withheld conflicts until she believed she could solve them. Under pressure, he turned private tension into public ease.

Neither need was shameful.

Together, the pattern exhausted them.

"Affection has not made us compatible," Nia said.

Julian's eyes shone. "No."

The word was quiet.

It still changed the room.

## Saturday, November 15: No Villain

They met once more Saturday beneath the arcade outside Media House.

Julian brought the scarf Nia had left in the radio booth. Nia brought a book he had loaned her about documentary storytelling.

The exchange looked too ordinary for the end of a first love.

"I am sorry about the jokes," Julian said. "Especially at the exhibition. I made you carry my jealousy in front of other people."

"I am sorry I kept deciding what you could handle instead of telling you the truth," Nia replied. "And for making every conflict a test of whether you understood me without explanation."

"I wanted to understand."

"I know."

"I also wanted you to need me in the part of your life where Malik already understood the language."

Nia could have argued about Malik. There was no reason now.

"That was not fair to you," she said. "Or to us."

Julian gave her the scarf. Their fingers touched.

"So this is it?"

Nia wanted him to have betrayed her. She wanted one terrible act she could point to whenever she missed him. Instead she had memories of two people who had tried, apologized, repeated patterns, and reached an honest limit.

"Yes," she said.

They hugged for a long time.

When they separated, Julian wiped his face and laughed once at himself. "I had a better exit planned."

"Of course you did."

"It had lighting."

Nia laughed through tears.

They walked in opposite directions without anger strong enough to carry either one home.

## Sunday, November 16: The Space Between

Test three asked students to find the area between two curves.

Nia sketched both functions, found their intersections, determined which curve lay above the other, and built the integral of top minus bottom.

Every difficult step created the same impulse: text Julian.

When she found the second intersection after an algebra mistake, she wanted to send him a victory message. When the substitution simplified cleanly, she wanted to tell him calculus had briefly decided to behave. When another student dropped a calculator, she wanted to share the joke she knew he would make.

Her phone remained zipped inside her bag.

The area problem changed where the curves crossed. One expression stayed on top over the first interval; the order reversed over the second. Nia split the integral instead of allowing signed area to cancel the total region she was asked to measure.

She checked the limits, evaluated each part, and added the positive areas.

Grief did not behave so neatly. Good memories and painful ones did not cancel into zero. Missing Julian did not prove the breakup was wrong. Knowing the breakup was right did not make the missing smaller.

Nia finished the test with four minutes left.

Outside, she texted Keisha: **Done. Please tell me there is food.**

Keisha replied: **Food, silence, and one legally binding ban on checking his page.**

Nia smiled for the first time that day.

## Monday, November 17: Transferability

The Heron Analytics regional semifinal took place in a conference center two hours from Bellwether.

McCall-Hart presented third.

Simone opened with the decision context. Priya explained the data. Andre described missingness and tagging. Malik presented the uncertainty intervals, showing when the model favored a fourth-down attempt and when the evidence left multiple defensible options.

The timing held. The visualizations worked. Nobody hid the limitations.

During questions, a judge from another university leaned toward the microphone.

"Your model incorporates opponent-specific tendencies and McCall-Hart possession values," she said. "What evidence supports transferring these decision thresholds to a team in another conference with different personnel and game pace?"

Malik knew the honest answer: not enough.

He also knew the answer he wanted to give.

"The framework is general," he began. "Another program could replace the inputs with its own data and estimate team-specific thresholds."

"Have you validated that process on an external team?"

"Not yet."

"Then how do you know the structure transfers rather than only the interface?"

Malik paused.

The room waited.

"We do not know," he said. "We would need external validation across programs and probably a revised model for differences in pace, roster strength, and data quality."

The answer was honest.

It was also incomplete because the work itself was incomplete.

After the session, Malik replayed the question until every possible better response arrived too late.

## Tuesday, November 18: Does Not Advance

The finalist announcement appeared on a screen in the conference lobby.

Bayland Institute advanced. So did two other universities.

McCall-Hart did not.

For several seconds, nobody on the team spoke. Around them, another group shouted and embraced.

Malik stared at the list.

"I lost it on transferability," he said.

Simone turned toward him. "We did not have transferability evidence."

"I could have explained the adaptation process better."

"That would not create validation we did not perform."

"The judge asked me."

"Because you presented that section. The model belonged to all of us."

Dr. Bennett gathered the team for a brief debrief and told them not to diagnose the loss in the lobby. They would review scores and comments when available.

On the bus home, Malik opened his presentation notes and rewrote the answer.

Then he rewrote it again.

Every version sounded better than the one he had given. None supplied the missing evidence.

He closed the file only when Simone reached across the aisle and lowered his laptop screen with one finger.

"You are trying to turn grief into editing," she said.

"It was a competition."

"It was also work you cared about. You are allowed to be disappointed before becoming educational."

## Wednesday, November 19: What Failed

The official feedback praised McCall-Hart's uncertainty communication and decision framing. It questioned external validation, sensitivity to data quality, and whether the model would support real-time use under limited staff capacity.

Andre called the scoring inconsistent with another team's presentation.

Malik wanted to agree.

Simone did not.

"We are not calling the result unfair because we dislike it," she said. "If there was a procedural error, document it. If not, document the weaknesses."

She created a postmortem file with four headings: **What Worked**, **What Failed**, **What We Do Not Know**, and **What We Would Test Next**.

Malik read the headings. "Can this wait?"

"The emotions can take time. The evidence should not. We will forget details and rewrite history to protect ourselves."

The team listed what worked: honest intervals, strong visual hierarchy, clear decision context, disciplined timing.

Then the failures: no external validation, limited evidence across conferences, incomplete real-time workflow testing, and an interface easier to understand than to operationalize.

"We can discuss next year after this is complete," Simone said.

The loss remained a loss.

It also became legible.

## Thursday, November 20: Opposite Ends

Malik found Nia on a couch at North Star after closing.

She sat at one end with her shoes tucked beneath her. He sat at the other, leaving the middle cushion empty.

"We broke up," Nia said.

"We did not advance," Malik replied.

Neither offered congratulations on honesty or speeches about better things ahead.

Nia looked toward the dark tutoring rooms. "I keep wanting to text him when something happens. Then I remember the person I would tell is the person I am grieving."

Malik nodded. "I keep rewriting one answer as if the right sentence would change the evidence we did not collect."

"Do you want to try again next year?"

"I do not know. Do you want to be friends with Julian?"

"I do not know."

They sat with two futures that had existed a week earlier and no longer did.

Malik did not move closer. Nia did not turn his loss into perspective about hers. They shared vending-machine pretzels and the kind of silence that did not demand improvement.

When Ms. Alvarez told them the building was closing, they stood.

Outside, River Classic banners had begun appearing along Cypress Walk.

The campus was already preparing to celebrate something else.

---

# Week Seventeen: The Catch and the Quiet After

The spare radio-booth key weighed less than an ounce.

Nia carried it all Friday morning as if it were made of iron.

Julian had given it to her in September after she began helping with occasional math segments. The key did not open the building, only the booth, and she had rarely used it without him. Still, it had represented access to his world: the microphones, music beds, handwritten schedules, and small red light that transformed ordinary speech into broadcast.

His final fall performance with the Marching Herons would come during River Classic weekend. After that, band activity would slow for exams and the holiday break.

Nia found him near Media House after rehearsal.

"I should give this back," she said.

Julian looked at the key in her palm. "You could keep it through finals."

"I do not think I should."

He nodded.

Their fingers touched during the handoff. Neither made a joke.

"Good luck at the Classic," Nia said.

"You too. I mean, you are not playing."

"I understood."

"Right."

They hugged briefly. Julian walked toward rehearsal, the key disappearing into his pocket.

Nia remained composed until she reached the second-floor bathroom in Jubilee Library. Then she locked herself in a stall and cried hard enough to miss ten minutes of calculus review.

## Friday, November 21: The Returned Key

By afternoon, Nia had washed her face and joined a cumulative review session.

Dr. Brooks asked students to explain how definite integrals appeared beyond geometric area. Groups considered accumulated distance from velocity, total cost from a marginal-cost rate, water volume from changing flow, and population change from a growth rate.

"Units tell the story," Nia said when her group reached the water-flow problem. "Rate in gallons per minute, integrated over minutes, gives gallons."

Her voice sounded normal.

Amara did not ask why her eyes looked swollen. She handed Nia the marker for the next explanation.

Nia drew the accumulation graph and communicated the solution in three layers: the equation, the units, and one sentence about meaning. She did not fill every silence. She did not perform extra brightness.

Afterward, Keisha met her outside with two drinks.

"Key returned?"

"Yes."

"How bad?"

"Quiet."

Keisha handed her the sweeter drink. "Those are sometimes the worst ones."

Nia nodded and let herself be known without producing a better ending.

## Saturday, November 22: The File Stays Open

Malik returned to the analytics postmortem Saturday morning.

His first instinct had been to bury the semifinal folder beneath a new directory named **SPRING DEVELOPMENT**. Instead, he opened Simone's four-part document.

He added evidence from the judge feedback and linked each limitation to a possible test. External validation required data from other teams. Operational testing required coaches or analysts completing time-limited scenarios. Transferability needed more than replacing inputs; the team had to examine whether model relationships remained stable across styles of play.

Then Malik wrote the part that felt worst:

**Our interface communicated uncertainty more effectively than our study established generalizability. The presentation invited users to trust a process we had validated only in one institutional context.**

Simone read the sentence over his shoulder.

"Keep it," she said.

"It sounds like we built the wrong project."

"We built a useful project with a smaller evidence boundary than we wanted."

"The judges may have thought it was not useful."

"Do not invent additional rejection. We have enough real rejection."

Malik added a clean diagram showing where evidence ended and proposed testing began. He uploaded the postmortem to his portfolio beside the polished semifinal slides.

The failure would not become a secret file he never opened again.

It would remain visible without becoming the only thing the portfolio said about him.

## Sunday, November 23: The River Classic Catch

River Classic tied McCall-Hart and Bayland State at twenty-four with less than two minutes remaining.

The rivalry had filled Bellwether Civic Stadium in two colors and several generations of argument. Bands traded songs. Alumni shouted across sections. Every tackle seemed personal to someone whose graduation year began with nineteen.

McCall-Hart faced third-and-long near midfield.

DJ lined up wide. His defender played tight coverage toward the sideline, daring him to win the boundary.

At the snap, DJ released inside, forced the defender to turn, then broke back outside. The pass sailed high and farther toward the sideline than planned.

DJ jumped.

He caught the ball with both hands, twisted in the air, and brought one foot down inches inside the white line. His second foot dragged across the turf before the defender drove him out.

For one second, nobody knew.

The official raised both arms to stop the clock, then signaled complete.

First down.

The McCall-Hart side detonated.

Malik grabbed the shoulders of a stranger. Nia screamed beside Keisha. Imani nearly climbed over the row in front of her. Julian's alto section launched into the fight song before the stadium had finished replaying the catch.

The gain placed McCall-Hart in range for the winning score. Three plays later, the Herons crossed the goal line.

Final: McCall-Hart 31, Bayland State 24.

DJ's catch did not score the winning touchdown. It made the winning touchdown possible.

He lay on the turf after the final whistle, looking up at the stadium lights while teammates piled onto him.

## Monday, November 24: Call Home First

By Monday morning, DJ had appeared on three campus accounts, one regional sports show, and a highlight reel titled **CATCH OF THE CLASSIC?** with a question mark designed to create arguments.

He completed two interviews before breakfast.

Reporters asked about the catch, the route, the sideline awareness, and whether he expected more playing time. DJ thanked the quarterback, the receivers coach, and the teammates whose blocks kept the play alive.

Then he stepped away and called his mother.

"They said this could affect my scholarship review," he told her.

Her voice changed. "How?"

DJ's current aid package required careful family budgeting. A larger football scholarship would mean fewer loans and less money his mother sent each month.

"No promises," he said. "Coach said the staff will review roles after the season."

"Then we do not spend imaginary money."

"I know."

"But I am proud of you right now. No review needed."

DJ went quiet.

Attention was fun. The possibility of easing pressure at home mattered more.

After the call, he returned to the residence hall and found Malik editing the analytics postmortem.

"You have a national celebrity in the room," DJ said.

"Regional, possibly."

"Hater."

Malik looked up. "Good catch."

DJ smiled. From Malik, those two words carried a complete scouting report of affection.

## Tuesday, November 25: The Last Fall Song

The Marching Herons performed a farewell set Tuesday evening for students leaving early for Thanksgiving.

Nia considered staying away.

Then she decided avoiding every place Julian mattered would turn the breakup into a new schedule controlling her life.

She stood with Keisha near the back of the crowd. Julian played with the same focus that had first drawn her toward him. The final song slowed into a warm arrangement of the university hymn. Alumni joined softly. Copper lights reflected from the instruments.

Afterward, Nia waited until the band had finished packing.

"You sounded beautiful," she told Julian.

"Thank you. I saw you."

"I figured. Small campus, large cape reputation."

He smiled. "I am glad you came."

"Me too."

They stood close enough to remember what closeness had meant.

"Have a good Thanksgiving," Julian said.

"You too."

Nia walked away before respect became an excuse to reopen the decision. Closure did not feel clean. It felt like choosing not to turn pain into another invitation.

## Wednesday, November 26: Stripped Walls

Malik returned to Gulfport for Thanksgiving recess.

The damaged lower walls had been stripped to the studs. Dehumidifiers hummed where the couch once sat. Repair estimates covered the dining table, each one higher than the family had hoped and lower than the worst number Malik feared.

Marcel walked through the room slowly, his injured leg stiff.

"Physical therapy says bend it more," he said. "The knee says mind my business."

Malik watched him grip a doorframe while stepping over an exposed section of floor.

"Sit down."

"I have been sitting for two months."

"You have not. That is part of the problem."

Marcel gave him a look, then accepted the folding chair.

They reviewed estimates together. Insurance would cover part of the structural work but remained uncertain about several tools. Family members had contributed labor. A church fund had helped with temporary materials. The remaining gap was smaller than before and still beyond easy reach.

"Your job money helps," Celeste told Malik quietly. "But school is still your job."

"I can do both."

She looked at him.

Malik corrected himself. "I am working on doing both."

It was less impressive and more true.

## Thursday, November 27: Separate Kitchens

On Thanksgiving evening, Nia called Malik from her mother's kitchen in Huntsville.

Behind her, relatives argued about card-game rules and whether macaroni and cheese counted as a vegetable. Behind Malik, Micah searched for foil while Celeste insisted leftovers required a system.

"How is home?" Nia asked.

"Stripped walls. More estimates. My father is walking better than he admits and worse than he claims."

"That sounds like him."

"You met him for zero minutes."

"I understand the genre."

Malik laughed.

He asked about Huntsville. Nia described her aunt's tutoring program, her mother's interrogation about sleep, and the cousin who had asked whether college had made her famous.

Neither mentioned the North Star couch directly. Neither used holiday distance to confess feelings that had not become responsible simply because Nia was single.

"I am glad you called," Malik said.

"Me too."

"You okay?"

Nia looked toward the family noise. "Not all the time. More than last week."

"Same."

They stayed on the phone while washing dishes in separate states, talking about finals, food, and nothing that demanded a decision.

The call ended warm, unfinished, and exactly where it needed to end.

---

# Week Eighteen: The Work Before the Answer

McCall-Hart welcomed students back from Thanksgiving with couple traditions Nia had never noticed before.

People took matching photos beneath the first holiday lights. Two students shared headphones on Cypress Walk. Copper Cup advertised a two-drink study special called **The Perfect Pair**, which felt less like marketing and more like targeted harassment.

Nia returned to the residence hall Friday afternoon, unpacked, and discovered the scarf Julian had returned during their breakup folded between two sweaters.

She sat on the edge of her bed holding it.

Keisha looked up from her laptop. "What do you need?"

The old Nia would have said she was fine. A slightly newer Nia might have created an evening plan involving three friends, a review session, and service work until sadness lost her address.

"Can you stay?" she asked. "Just for tonight. I do not want the first quiet evening back by myself."

Keisha closed the laptop.

"Yes."

The answer contained no advice, which was one reason Nia trusted it.

## Friday, November 28: Ask, Then Receive

They ordered food, watched a ridiculous baking competition, and kept their phones on the other side of the room.

Halfway through the second episode, Nia began crying because one contestant dropped a cake.

"This is not about cake," Keisha said.

"The cake worked hard."

"The cake is structurally unsound sugar."

Nia laughed and cried harder.

She told Keisha about seeing Julian's performance, returning the key, and how respectful closure had somehow made the good memories more available.

"If he had been awful," Nia said, "I could be angry every time I missed him."

"You would still miss who you hoped he was."

"Why are you wise during my suffering?"

"Efficiency."

At ten, Nia thanked her.

"You do not have to thank me for living here," Keisha said.

"I asked you to change your evening."

"And I said yes because I wanted to. See how consent makes the schedule less haunted?"

Nia threw a pillow at her. This time Keisha did not catch it. The pillow struck her laptop bag, and both of them laughed until the room felt inhabitable again.

## Saturday, November 29: Grounds for Appeal

Malik met Dr. Bennett with the semifinal rubric, score report, and a draft appeal.

"I think the transferability question affected the judges too much," Malik said.

"That is not grounds for appeal."

"Another team also lacked external validation."

"Did the panel violate a published scoring rule?"

"Not that I can prove."

"Was there a mathematical error in the feedback?"

"The feedback did not include enough detail to determine that."

"Was your score recorded incorrectly? Did a conflict of interest go undisclosed? Was your team denied presentation time promised in the rules?"

"No."

Dr. Bennett slid the appeal draft back across the desk.

"Then identify the procedural error before you ask me to support this. Disliking how the judges weighted a real weakness is not evidence that the process failed."

Malik looked at the score report. "So we do nothing?"

"You document. You ask for clarification if the process allows it. You improve the work. An appeal is not a device for converting disappointment into another round."

Malik left angry enough to search every rule.

For two hours, he compared the timeline, scoring categories, judge assignments, and written feedback.

He found ambiguity.

He found no violation.

## Sunday, November 30: Withdraw

Malik withdrew the draft appeal Sunday morning.

The email was short: **After reviewing the published process and available records, I do not have evidence of a procedural error. I will not submit an appeal.**

Then he returned to his portfolio.

He added the semifinal result, judge feedback, and technical postmortem. His analysis distinguished performance error from model limitation. He could have framed his unanswered question as a speaking failure. Instead, he wrote that the question exposed an evidence boundary the team had communicated but not fully investigated.

He described what a stronger project would require: multi-team data, out-of-sample validation, operational testing, and a clearer account of which relationships could reasonably transfer.

Simone reviewed the entry.

"This makes us look unfinished," she said.

"We were unfinished."

"Correct answer."

She suggested one edit: add what the team had contributed successfully, not as consolation but as evidence. Malik included uncertainty communication, missing-data transparency, and decision-focused design.

The portfolio no longer read like a victory page or a confession.

It read like work.

## Monday, December 1: Share the Board

Nia led the final Calculus I review circle at North Star.

She divided the board into limits, derivatives, applications, and integrals. Normally she would have prepared every explanation herself. This time, she assigned Amara one-sided limits, Malik linear approximation, another student related rates, and two upperclass tutors integral applications.

"What are you teaching?" Amara asked.

"Connections between units," Nia said. "And snacks. I am coordinating snacks."

The circle began well. Then a student asked about optimization, and Nia lost the thread halfway through explaining endpoint checks. For a terrible second, her mind supplied Julian's joke about her turning every idea into a story.

She stopped.

"Amara, can you finish why the critical point is not automatically the maximum?"

Amara stepped forward and completed the explanation.

Nia drank water, looked at her notes, and returned for the next section.

She did not apologize for needing help. She did not reclaim the board with double energy. The review became stronger because several voices carried it.

Afterward, a student told Nia, "I like how everybody explains it differently."

Nia looked at the erased board. "Me too."

## Tuesday, December 2: Freshman of the Week

DJ was named conference freshman of the week after the River Classic.

The announcement appeared at nine. By nine fifteen, his family group chat had produced forty-three messages and one aunt had misspelled "conference" in three unique ways.

At ten, coaches congratulated him and reminded the team that the regular-season finale still required preparation. At noon, a student reporter requested another interview.

At two, DJ sat in Jubilee Library staring at a statistics paper due by midnight.

The assignment asked him to compare two measures of player performance and discuss how sample size affected the conclusion. He had chosen catch percentage and yards per route run, then discovered that celebrating online did not generate a literature review.

Malik found him surrounded by articles.

"Freshman of the week," Malik said.

"Freshman with a paper due. The title did not grant immunity."

"Tragic."

DJ showed him the comparison. Malik asked questions instead of rewriting it: Did catch percentage account for target difficulty? Could limited snaps inflate yards per route? What did each metric reward?

"So the answer is both numbers have agendas," DJ said.

"The numbers do not. The definitions do."

DJ typed that sentence into his notes, then added quotation marks and Malik's name.

"Do not cite me."

"Too late, Professor Data Man."

He submitted at 11:47 p.m.

Public success had not completed his private assignments. It had made them harder to schedule.

## Wednesday, December 3: The Group Outing

The basketball home opener gave everyone an excuse to leave their review notes for two hours.

Malik and Nia could have gone together. Neither suggested it.

Instead, they joined DJ, Keisha, Imani, Amara, and three analytics teammates at Ruth Ellis Fieldhouse. The group occupied most of a row and disagreed immediately about which concession line moved faster.

McCall-Hart's women opened with aggressive defense and a quick transition game. The men's team followed in the second half of the doubleheader. DJ analyzed spacing as if secretly auditioning for another sport. Keisha informed him that knowing statistics did not qualify him to coach from row twelve.

Nia cheered without checking whether Julian was covering the game. Malik sat beside Simone for part of the second half and listened to her explain why one lineup's apparent efficiency came from a favorable three-minute matchup.

No one used the evening to escape into romance.

When Nia became quiet during the band's halftime feature, Keisha squeezed her hand. When Malik checked a family message, Simone asked whether he needed to step out. Care moved through the group without becoming a spotlight.

McCall-Hart won both games.

For one night, uncomplicated celebration was allowed.

## Thursday, December 4: The Review Map

Malik and Nia finished their Calculus I review map at a wide table in Eliza Moss Hall.

They began with August: function values versus limits, one-sided behavior, and the first graph that had unsettled Malik. From there they linked continuity, derivatives as limits, rules, applications, antiderivatives, Riemann sums, definite integrals, the Fundamental Theorem, and net change.

Beside each topic, they wrote three things:

**What we thought. What changed. What still needs work.**

Malik wrote that he once believed a correct answer could speak for itself. Now he knew reasoning had to reveal assumptions, boundaries, and meaning. He still needed to slow down under time pressure.

Nia wrote that she once believed explaining quickly proved understanding. Now she knew listening could improve both the mathematics and the group. She still needed to check algebra when emotion scattered her attention.

For their final project, they used the map to build a short guide showing how one concept supported the next. The guide did not present their semester as a straight line. It showed loops, revisions, and ideas they had to encounter more than once.

"This is messy," Malik said.

"Accurately messy."

"The arrows cross."

"Learning does that."

He let the arrows remain.

Finals would produce grades soon. Football still had a conference title to chase. Neither result would answer every question the semester had raised.

For now, the work before the answer was complete.

---

# Week Nineteen: Everything That Counts

On the last day of Calculus I, Dr. Brooks placed two papers on Malik's desk.

The first was his corrected test from September. The original score, sixty-four, remained visible in red at the top. Beneath it, the correction credit and comments documented what he had repaired.

The second paper was recent work on the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus. Malik had built the accumulation function, connected its derivative to the original rate, interpreted the units, and stated the conditions supporting the argument.

Dr. Brooks tapped both pages.

"Do not compare only the numbers," she said. "Compare the reasoning."

Malik did.

The corrected first test showed understanding added after consequence. The recent work showed understanding made visible the first time. It was not flawless. He had lost points for skipping one endpoint interpretation. But the explanation no longer hid behind the answer.

"You improved," Dr. Brooks said. "That does not mean the difficult beginning was secretly good for you. It means you responded to it."

Malik looked at the sixty-four one last time.

The grade still belonged to him. So did everything he had done after it.

## Friday, December 5: Last Class

Fall classes ended with no dramatic speech.

Dr. Brooks reviewed final-exam expectations, reminded students that justification mattered, and asked them to write one concept they understood differently now.

Nia wrote: **A complete explanation leaves room for other people to enter it.**

Malik wrote: **An answer is not complete if its assumptions remain private.**

Amara wrote longer than either of them and covered her response with one hand when Nia tried to peek.

After class, students lingered in Eliza Moss Hall. Some exchanged study times. Others photographed the board before it was erased. The room where Malik and Nia had first argued over limits now held months of accumulated evidence: stress, repair, missed sleep, better questions, and friendships that had survived correction.

"Championship tomorrow," DJ announced when Malik returned to the residence hall. His game bag was packed with enough precision to suggest Malik's influence or panic.

"Finals Monday," Malik replied.

"Let joy have one full sentence."

Malik looked at him. "Championship tomorrow."

"Thank you."

## Saturday, December 6: The Catch Before the Score

The Southern Collegiate Heritage Conference championship stayed close into the fourth quarter.

McCall-Hart trailed by three when the offense faced third-and-eight. DJ lined up in the slot. The defense disguised pressure, then dropped a linebacker beneath his route.

DJ adjusted without abandoning the pattern. He settled in the narrow space behind the linebacker and in front of the safety. The quarterback delivered the ball high.

DJ caught it, absorbed a hit, and held on.

Nine yards. First down.

The catch did not appear on the final scoring graphic. Four plays later, another receiver caught the touchdown. The defense protected the lead, and McCall-Hart won the conference championship by four.

DJ sprinted onto the field with everyone else. Indigo confetti caught in his hair. Imani reached the front rail and screamed his name. Malik, Nia, Keisha, Simone, Amara, and half the analytics team celebrated from the stands.

During the trophy ceremony, cameras focused on captains and senior stars. DJ stood behind them, smiling so hard his face hurt.

He had made the River Classic catch. He had made the third-down catch that kept the championship drive alive. He had not scored the final touchdown.

He did not need to.

In the locker room, the receivers coach found him.

"Good feel against the zone," he said. "And you secured the ball."

DJ nodded solemnly. "I have experienced growth."

The coach stared at him.

"Yes, sir," DJ corrected.

## Sunday, December 7: Leave Before the Song

Campus celebrated the championship through Sunday.

Nia joined Keisha, DJ, Imani, and friends at Hart Student Union. She wore an indigo championship shirt over a long-sleeved top and let herself enjoy the uncomplicated part: DJ's grin, alumni dancing, the trophy reflecting Copper Cup lights.

Julian arrived with band members before their gathering upstairs.

He and Nia saw each other across the terrace. He raised one hand. She returned the gesture.

For a moment, she imagined staying. The band gathering would have music she knew, people who still thought of them together, and the familiar possibility of standing beside Julian until the breakup blurred.

Keisha noticed her watching.

"Want to go?"

Nia nodded. "Before I turn missing him into a reason."

She congratulated DJ again, hugged Imani, and left with Keisha before the band moved upstairs.

Walking away did not feel powerful. It felt sad and responsible.

Imani caught them at the bottom of the union steps.

"You leaving because you want to," she asked Nia, "or because you think being healed means never sharing a room with him?"

Nia looked back through the glass. Julian was laughing with two band members, unaware of the conversation.

"I can share a room with him," she said. "I did at the farewell performance. Tonight everybody is celebrating, people are taking pictures, and I know exactly how easy it would be to stand beside him until the old version feels available."

"So this is strategy, not fear."

"Tonight, yes. Ask me again next month."

Imani nodded. "Good answer. Healing is not attending every event to prove you can survive it."

Keisha linked her arm through Nia's. They walked toward the residence hall and bought convenience-store ice cream on the way. The night became smaller than the championship celebration and more suited to what Nia could honestly carry.

Behind them, the first song began.

Nia did not turn around.

## Monday, December 8: One Argument

Final examinations began Monday.

The Calculus I final required more than separate techniques. One long problem displayed a graph of a function, asked students to analyze its derivative, and then interpret accumulated signed area over an interval.

Students had to connect increasing and decreasing behavior, critical points, concavity evidence, net change, and units in one argument.

During the review briefing, Dr. Brooks had warned them: "A graph, a derivative, and an integral are not three unrelated chapters. They are three ways of asking what changes, how it changes, and what accumulates."

The exam schedule divided sections across several days. Malik's appointment was Tuesday morning. Nia's was Wednesday.

On Monday night, they reviewed separately.

Malik used the map they had built but stopped at ten thirty. Nia redrew regions until the integration bounds felt visible. Neither called the other for reassurance.

At 9:48, Malik texted: **Sleep is part of test preparation. Evidence remains annoying.**

Nia replied: **Agreed. Phone going across room. Good luck tomorrow.**

They honored the message.

## Tuesday, December 9: Leave One Blank

Malik entered the final with a rule: no answer without the reasoning that made it defensible.

He moved more slowly than he had on the first test. He stated one-sided behavior. He checked derivative signs. He wrote what accumulated area meant in context instead of leaving a number alone.

With twenty-two minutes left, he reached a proof-style question he did not fully know how to finish.

The old panic returned. One unfinished part threatened to turn the entire exam into evidence of failure.

Malik read the prompt again. He wrote the conditions he knew, established the first implication, and marked the step he could not justify instead of inventing one.

Then he used the remaining time to check every complete argument.

When the exam ended, the proof remained unfinished.

So did one era of Malik's thinking.

He had not rushed the rest of the work to attack one blank. He had not made confidence perform where understanding ended.

Outside, Dr. Brooks collected his paper.

"How was it?" she asked.

Malik considered the automatic answer.

"Mostly strong," he said. "One part incomplete."

"That sounds like information."

"I am trying to treat it that way."

## Wednesday, December 10: Redraw the Region

Nia froze on the integration setup.

Two curves enclosed a region but exchanged order after their intersection. She knew the process. Find the intersection, identify top and bottom, split the integral, evaluate.

Her mind supplied nothing.

For several minutes, she stared at the equations as if they belonged to a course she had never taken.

Then she stopped trying to remember the finished setup.

She redrew the region.

The first sketch was wrong. She marked test points and corrected it. The intersection became visible. So did the change in which curve lay above the other.

Nia built two integrals.

She checked the bounds and continued.

No glance toward Malik. No imagined explanation from Julian. No need to become the fastest voice in the room. She used the remaining time and the tools she had practiced.

When the exam ended, Nia walked outside and leaned against the limestone wall.

Keisha waited with two snacks.

"How bad?"

"I froze. Then I drew. Then I recovered."

"So which snack?"

"Both. Recovery is hungry work."

## Thursday, December 11: The Only Promises

After the final, Malik and Nia met beneath Founders' Bell.

The semester had begun with them standing near the same place and agreeing to become accountability partners. Now students rolled suitcases down Cypress Walk while residence halls prepared to close.

"How did you do?" Nia asked.

"Mostly strong. One incomplete proof."

"I froze on area between curves. Recovered after redrawing."

Neither inflated the answer. Neither softened it to protect the other.

"What now?" Nia asked.

Malik thought of Gulfport, stripped walls, insurance documents, and a living room that would become his bedroom. Nia thought of Huntsville, family questions, and the first holiday season without Julian in her phone.

"Get home safely," Malik said.

"Tell the truth when grades post."

"Agreed."

No promise to call every day. No confession timed to the end of calculus. No claim that surviving one semester had made them ready for every next step.

They hugged, wished each other safe travel, and walked away carrying everything that counted, including the things no grade would measure.

---

# Week Twenty: After the Grades

Fall commencement made finishing look simple.

Names were called. Graduates crossed a stage inside Ruth Ellis Fieldhouse. Families screamed from the stands, flowers crowded the lobby, and every program reduced years of work to a name, degree, and line of honors.

Malik sat with Nia, Keisha, and DJ near the back.

They watched a mathematics graduate hug Dr. Brooks after crossing. They saw a father lift a toddler above his head when the child's mother received her degree. One older graduate stopped midway across the stage, closed her eyes, and pressed a hand to her heart before continuing.

"I wonder what her semester was like," Nia said.

"Probably more than the program says," Malik replied.

Commencement displayed the answer. It concealed most of the work before it.

The first-years understood that now.

They also understood they were still near the beginning.

## Friday, December 12: Invisible Work

After the ceremony, the group moved through the fieldhouse lobby while families took photographs beneath indigo banners.

DJ carried his travel bag for the Heritage Bowl. Residence halls closed Saturday, so the football team had moved directly into bowl preparation.

"Imagine our graduation," he said. "They will need additional applause time for me."

Keisha looked at him. "You have completed one semester."

"A decorated semester."

"One paper almost took you out last week."

"The public does not need every detail."

Malik laughed, then watched another family surround a graduate.

His own parents had talked about his future as if the degree were a bridge to stability. They did not see every correction form, overnight shift, or moment he considered whether the path still fit. Today reminded him that invisible difficulty did not make completion less real.

Nia noticed him watching.

"Four years," she said.

"Approximately."

"Do not add uncertainty to graduation."

"Evidence requires it."

She smiled. The joke felt like an old piece of their friendship returning without the pressure to become anything else.

## Saturday, December 13: The Block

The Heritage Bowl came down to one decisive play.

McCall-Hart led by three late in the fourth quarter and needed a first down to protect the clock. The offense called a run toward the edge. DJ lined up wide, far from where television viewers expected the action to matter.

His assignment was to block the cornerback.

At the snap, DJ released as if beginning a route, forced the defender backward, then engaged cleanly. The running back cut outside and passed behind him. DJ kept his hands inside, moved his feet, and avoided holding.

The runner gained eleven yards.

First down.

McCall-Hart drained the remaining clock and won the Heritage Bowl.

The highlight replay focused on the runner. The announcer praised the offensive line. DJ appeared at the edge of the frame for less than two seconds.

In the film room later, the coach paused on those two seconds.

"Cole seals the corner," he said. "No edge, no first down."

DJ nodded.

The ball had gone elsewhere. His work still shaped the result.

He texted Malik: **I WON A BOWL GAME WITHOUT RECEIVING A PASS. EXPLAIN THE STATISTICAL INJUSTICE.**

Malik replied: **Your sample contains one excellent block. Report the correct variable.**

DJ saved that message too.

## Sunday, December 14: More Than Imaginary Money

The head coach asked DJ to stay after Sunday's team meeting.

DJ sat across from him expecting a discussion of spring training.

"Your role changed this fall," the coach said. "Special teams, offensive snaps, River Classic, championship. Your academic reports are solid. We are increasing your athletic scholarship for spring."

The new amount would not make college free. It would reduce the gap enough to change his family's monthly decisions.

DJ thanked the coach, left the office, and called Malik before the athletics department announced anything.

"It increased," DJ said.

Malik sat up on Aunt Renee's couch in Gulfport. "How much?"

DJ told him.

"That covers most of the remaining housing gap."

"I know."

For once, DJ's voice held no joke.

"You earned it," Malik said.

"The team earned the season."

"Your scholarship is not a group assignment."

DJ laughed, then became quiet again. "My mom can breathe a little."

That mattered more than the public post that appeared an hour later.

## Monday, December 15: B

Malik opened his final grade from the living room in Gulfport.

Calculus I: **B**.

In August, he had expected an A. He had arrived with a budget, a roadmap, and the belief that preparation could protect him from outcomes that belonged to less disciplined people.

The letter on screen first looked like distance: from his expectation, from the image his family held, from the transcript he had planned.

Then he opened the grade details.

Sixty-four on the first test. Improved applications exam. Strong corrections. Completed make-up work. A final exam that confirmed recovery without perfection.

The B did not mean the storm was good for him. It did not reward exhaustion. It did not transform the poor first test into an inspirational requirement.
