It measured a semester in which he had nearly hidden himself out of the course and then learned to ask for structure.

Celeste looked over his shoulder. "Is that bad?"

"No."

"You made the face you make when a number insults you."

Malik looked again. "It is not the grade I expected."

"Is it the grade you earned?"

"Yes."

"Then greet it properly."

Malik laughed. "Hello, B."

"See? Manners."

Later, Malik opened his spring registration audit. Calculus II required more than the letter grade that unlocked enrollment. His final feedback identified concrete prerequisites: reliable algebraic manipulation, confident substitution, interpretation of definite integrals, and the ability to explain why a method applied.

He created a two-week review plan, then deleted half of it. Winter break was not a hidden semester.

The revised plan contained three short sessions: algebra patterns that had slowed him, integral setups he still confused under pressure, and one explanation exercise using words rather than compressed notation. He also blocked North Star work hours only after confirming his spring class schedule.

For the first time, transcript review did not become a judgment of who Malik was. It became evidence about what the next course would ask him to carry.

## Tuesday, December 16: A-Minus

Nia's A-minus arrived in Huntsville while her mother was reorganizing holiday decorations and asking questions from another room.

"What did you get?"

"A-minus."

Denise Reynolds appeared in the doorway. "That is excellent."

"It is."

Nia waited for the grade to erase the exhaustion.

It did not.

The A-minus reflected strong understanding, improved listening, and a final exam recovery after freezing. It did not change the missed showcase, the overcommitted review, the breakup, or the nights she confused being needed with being valued.

"You are doing the face too," Denise said.

"What face?"

"The one where good news has to defend itself in court."

Nia put down her phone. "I am proud. I am also tired. And sad."

"Those feelings are not fighting for one chair."

Nia hugged her mother.

She celebrated the grade that evening with family. She did not post it as proof the semester had gone beautifully.

The next morning, she reviewed her own spring prerequisites. Calculus II would move quickly into integration techniques and applications. Her A-minus showed conceptual strength, but the exam history showed that emotional overload made routine algebra less dependable. She wrote one goal beneath the transcript: **Practice the fragile skills before they become emergency skills.**

Then she closed the portal. Reflection had produced a next step. It did not need to consume the holiday.

## Wednesday, December 17: Easy Without a Deadline

An alumni actuarial workshop brought Malik back to Bellwether for a day.

The session covered exam pathways, internships, professional communication, and how early-career analysts should discuss model limitations with people who wanted certainty.

Simone attended for the finance-and-data panel.

Afterward, she and Malik found a quiet table at Porter Center.

"B in calculus," he told her.

"Congratulations."

"It feels complicated."

"Most earned things do."

They debriefed the analytics loss without slides or a countdown. Simone admitted she had known transferability was weak but hoped clear uncertainty communication would carry more weight. Malik admitted he had blamed his answer because personal failure felt easier to fix than a limited study design.

"You cannot rehearse your way into evidence," Simone said.

"I tried."

"Aggressively."

Their conversation moved from analytics to spring classes, career goals, and the terrible workshop coffee. Without a deadline, Simone's directness felt less like pressure and more like ease. Malik's dry humor appeared often enough to surprise both of them.

When they finally checked the time, ninety minutes had passed.

"We are better at talking when nobody needs a deliverable," Simone said.

"Preliminary result."

"We may need replication."

They smiled, leaving the possibility unnamed.

## Thursday, December 18: The Living Room

Malik returned to Gulfport after dark.

His old bedroom had new lower drywall but still smelled of removed wallboard, sealant, and damp wood. The floor remained unfinished. Boxes occupied the bed.

"You can sleep at Aunt Renee's," Celeste offered.

"I want to be here."

They set up an air mattress in the living room beside the dehumidifier. Micah brought three blankets and claimed one was "structural support."

Malik lay awake beneath the ceiling fan, hearing his parents speak quietly in the kitchen about repair costs.

He had earned a B. DJ had earned more scholarship support. Nia had earned an A-minus. The Herons had won two championships.

None of those answers rebuilt the room.

Still, they mattered.

The semester had ended without the competition victory Malik wanted. It had also ended with work he could defend, people he trusted, and a transcript that showed recovery rather than collapse.

He turned onto his side as the dehumidifier hummed.

Spring would begin from here, not from the version of home he remembered.

---

# Week Twenty-One: Christmas Without the Old Version

Nia found Julian's playlist in a box labeled **DESK / IMPORTANT / DO NOT LOSE**, which guaranteed it had been packed beneath three less important boxes.

The playlist was not digital. Julian had made it by hand after their first official month together: twelve songs written on a copper card, each with one sentence explaining why he chose it. Beside track four, he had written, **This sounds like you explaining an idea faster than the rest of us can think.**

Nia sat on her childhood bedroom floor and played the songs in order.

The list held their good parts without including the arguments that followed. It remembered Copper Cup, band rehearsals, campus rain, the Heritage Kickoff, and one late-night radio segment when Nia laughed so hard she snorted on air and Julian promised to delete the recording.

He had not deleted it.

She smiled at the memory.

Then she cried.

Missing those moments did not mean she should call him. Loving what had been good did not make the relationship compatible after all.

Nia let the playlist finish.

## Friday, December 19: The Good Parts

When the final song ended, Nia placed the card back in the box instead of throwing it away or leaving it on top.

Her mother found her downstairs making tea.

"Julian?" Denise asked.

"Playlist."

"That sounds like Julian."

Nia looked into the mug. "He was thoughtful. People act like breakups reveal the relationship was fake. Ours was not fake."

"No."

"It was also not working."

"Both facts get to stay."

Nia had spent weeks trying to sort the relationship into a clean result: good or bad, right or wrong, worth it or mistake. The playlist resisted the categories. It had been loving. The public jokes had been hurtful. The first kiss mattered. The ending was still necessary.

She carried her tea to the living room and helped untangle holiday lights with her cousins.

The good memories came with her. They did not drag her backward.

## Saturday, December 20: Chosen

Denise asked the harder question Saturday morning.

They were driving to Nia's aunt's tutoring program, where families were collecting holiday learning kits and donated books.

"Do you miss Julian," Denise said, "or do you miss being chosen?"

Nia looked out the window. "That feels rude before ten."

"We can reschedule truth for noon."

"No."

Nia thought about Julian asking her to be his girlfriend beneath Founders' Bell. The label had made her feel visible and wanted. She had liked belonging to an *us* that other people recognized.

"I miss him," she said. "His jokes when they were not defensive. How serious he was about band. The way he listened when he forgot to perform listening."

"And being chosen?"

"Yes. I miss that too. I cannot separate them yet."

Denise nodded. "You do not have to separate them today. Just do not call one feeling by the other's name."

At the tutoring program, Nia helped children build paper bridges and estimate how much weight each design could hold. She did not volunteer to redesign the entire event. She stayed at her assigned table.

Her mother noticed and said nothing.

## Sunday, December 21: Dependence

In Gulfport, Malik helped his father complete physical-therapy exercises.

Marcel sat in a sturdy chair while Malik counted knee bends and checked the printed range-of-motion guide.

"Ten," Malik said.

"That was twelve."

"That was ten."

"College has damaged your counting."

"Again."

Marcel bent the knee. Pain crossed his face.

"Stop if it is sharp," Malik said.

"It is not sharp."

"You made the sharp face."

"I have one face."

They completed the set. When Marcel tried to stand, he refused Malik's hand, failed on the first attempt, and became angry at the chair.

Malik waited instead of grabbing him.

"Want help?" he asked.

His father stared at the floor. "Yes."

Malik offered his forearm. Marcel stood.

The humiliation in his father's expression unsettled him more than the weakness. Marcel had built his identity around providing, repairing, and being the person others called. Dependence made him feel as if his usefulness had been removed along with the damaged walls.

"You are still my father when I help you stand," Malik said.

Marcel looked at him. "I know that in my head."

"The rest takes time?"

"Apparently."

It was the closest they came to discussing fear.

## Monday, December 22: Which Rooms Wait

The contractor called Monday with another delay.

A material shipment would not arrive before January. One subcontractor had overbooked holiday work. The estimate for the back rooms had increased after hidden moisture damage appeared behind the remaining wall.

Repairs would continue into spring.

The family gathered around the dining table with floor plans and costs. They could complete the kitchen and one bedroom first, then delay Malik's room and the utility area. Or they could spread work across all rooms and leave everything unfinished longer.

"Finish Mom and Dad's room," Malik said. "I am at school most of the time."

Celeste objected. Marcel objected louder.

"This is not about whose room matters," Malik said. "It is about which space gets used every day."

He used a rough accumulation estimate to compare temporary housing and storage costs under each schedule. Small weekly expenses, extended across months, changed which delay was actually cheaper.

Micah watched Malik shade weekly cost bars on a hand-drawn timeline.

"Is that another integral?" he asked.

"A rough preview. If the cost rate changes over time, total cost is what accumulates under the rate. Right now we only have weekly estimates, so we are adding pieces."

"Like the flood-water math?"

"Related idea. Different thing we wish we did not need to measure."

They compared two schedules. Finishing every room slowly looked fair, but it extended storage and temporary-living costs. Completing essential rooms first created an earlier drop in the weekly expense rate. The area represented by those later weeks became smaller.

Malik explained the result to his parents without pretending the estimates were exact. Material delays could change. New damage could appear. The calculation clarified the tradeoff; it did not make the decision for them.

The calculation offered no painless choice.

They prioritized the kitchen, primary bedroom, and safe utility access. Malik's room would wait.

He agreed because the decision made sense.

He grieved because the room was his.

## Tuesday, December 23: The Pie Model

Micah decided to bake a pie from an online recipe and treated measuring cups as nonbinding suggestions.

The crust emerged from the oven shaped like a continent nobody recognized. Filling leaned heavily toward one side.

Malik photographed it.

He started to text Nia, **How are you doing? Are you lonely?**

Then he stopped.

The question was not wrong, but he recognized the habit beneath it. Care could become surveillance. Checking on Nia's heartbreak every day could turn him into her primary emotional witness and make his reliability feel like a claim on what happened next.

He sent the photograph instead.

**Micah has invented non-Euclidean pie. Volume may require Calculus II.**

Nia replied with a photograph of a paper bridge collapsing beneath canned beans.

**Structural engineers unavailable. Recommend whipped cream stabilization.**

They exchanged three more jokes and no emotional inventory.

Malik put down the phone and helped Micah reshape the next crust.

Nia returned to wrapping gifts with her cousins.

Connection did not require constant evaluation.

## Wednesday, December 24: Phone in the Other Room

The Reynolds family held its main Christmas dinner on Christmas Eve.

Nia's phone vibrated through appetizers: club messages, grade celebrations, holiday posts, and a notification that Julian had added a new radio recording.

She carried the phone to her bedroom and left it on the dresser.

For the first time all fall, she sat through dinner without being reachable.

Her younger cousins performed a chaotic holiday song. An uncle told the same college story everyone had heard. Denise brought out a dessert that cracked down the center and blamed the oven with great confidence.

Nia was present for all of it.

She still wondered whether Julian had messaged. She still thought about Malik's pie and Keisha's family gathering in Mobile. Being present did not require every other connection to disappear from her mind.

It meant she stopped leaving the table to maintain them.

After dinner, she checked the phone.

There was no message from Julian.

The absence hurt. It also let the boundary remain intact.

## Thursday, December 25: Spring Goals

On Christmas night, Malik and Nia separately wrote spring goals.

Malik used a plain notebook page. His first list began with grades, actuarial preparation, work hours, and financial support for home. He crossed it out and started again.

**People:** call home on planned days when possible; ask before assuming what help is needed; make room for friendships that are not projects.

**Sleep:** protect six and a half hours as a minimum, seven as the goal; reduce something if the schedule makes that impossible.

**Uncertainty:** state what is unknown before building a plan; do not treat incomplete control as personal failure.

Nia wrote in copper ink.

**People:** ask what others need; say what I need before resentment; let friends contribute without directing every contribution.

**Sleep:** stop treating midnight as free real estate.

**Uncertainty:** an open opportunity is not an emergency; a difficult feeling is not a decision deadline.

Neither wrote the other's name.

That omission was not distance. It was space.

The new year would begin with Malik more willing to move forward and Nia still learning that grief could coexist with curiosity about what came next.

Neither knew yet how much she would mind when forward movement stopped meaning her.

---

# Week Twenty-Two: The Year Turns Quietly

Malik finished the analytics postmortem the day after Christmas.

The final page listed the work a future team would need: external data, operational testing, a transferability study, and a version of the interface that remained useful when staff had seconds rather than minutes. He added source links, archived the code, and gave Simone final editorial access.

Then he sent her a message.

**Thank you for refusing to let us call the result unfair before we understood it. Also for cutting the slides. They needed to die.**

Simone replied: **I have waited six weeks for you to admit that. Call me. I need to hear the sincerity.**

Malik called from the Baptiste porch.

The conversation began with deleted slides. It moved to the alumni workshop, spring schedules, DJ's scholarship, Simone's finance internship search, and the strange relief of having a break long enough to discover which people they wanted to talk to when no deliverable required it.

When Malik checked the time, sixty-eight minutes had passed.

"We did it again," he said.

"Replication," Simone replied. "Our finding may be robust."

Malik smiled into the cold Gulfport evening.

## Friday, December 26: The Thank-You

Simone asked about the house without turning the call into an interview.

Malik told her which rooms had been delayed and how his father handled physical therapy like a personal feud. She described her family's holiday debate over whether her international-internship plans counted as "going too far away on purpose."

"Do you want to go that far?" Malik asked.

"If the right opportunity is there, yes."

She did not soften the answer.

He liked that about her.

Before hanging up, Simone said, "We should get coffee when we are back. Not a postmortem. Not a team meeting."

Malik leaned against the porch rail. "Personal coffee?"

"You are an analyst. Interpret the available evidence."

"I would like that."

The invitation created a nervousness distinct from his old hope around Nia. It was not built on waiting for an opening. Simone had asked. Malik had answered.

Inside, Celeste called him to help move a cabinet.

Malik ended the call and carried the new possibility into the house without trying to define it further.

## Saturday, December 27: The Call for Proposals

In Huntsville, Nia received Dr. Price's research announcement.

The Founders' Research Forum was accepting early proposals for a spring flood-resilience track. Strong projects could advance to the Southeastern Undergraduate Mathematics Symposium and compete for a national summer fellowship.

Nia read the message three times.

The Delta laboratory visit returned to her in pieces: water moving through low roads, uncertainty layers on the visualization wall, Dr. Washington saying failure was information about work rather than a complete measurement of the worker.

She opened a new document titled **Bellwether Flood Evacuation Model**.

Her first idea was too large. It included road capacity, vehicle access, shelter choice, warning trust, mobility, traffic flow, and dynamic flood depth. The project would have required an entire laboratory, two government agencies, and possibly a minor deity.

Nia reduced it to a question: **How might missing road-capacity and household-access information change a recommended evacuation route?**

For readiness practice, she graphed a simplified rate of traffic accumulation, recovered antiderivatives, and repaired the algebra she had marked as fragile. The math was review. The application made it feel pointed toward spring.

She texted Keisha: **Research proposal. Flood evacuation. I need a real programmer and somebody who tells me when the question is impossible. Interested?**

Keisha replied: **Define “need.”**

Nia recognized the test.

## Sunday, December 28: Coauthor

Keisha joined the video call from Mobile wearing track gear and holding a bowl of cereal.

"What do you want me to do?" she asked.

Nia shared the draft. "Build the route model, clean data, help decide what the system can actually compute--"

"Stop."

Nia stopped.

"You just listed code, data, and model design as if they are support services around your idea."

"I did not mean it that way."

"Meaning is not the project structure."

Keisha set down the bowl. "I will join if I am a collaborator. That means I help define the question, choose methods, reject bad data, and own the computational work. My name goes on the application from the beginning. I am not emergency technical help after you promise something the code cannot do."

Nia felt the reflex to defend herself. Then she remembered the spring goal written in copper ink: let friends contribute without directing every contribution.

"Coauthor," Nia said. "Shared method decisions. You can veto computational claims we cannot support."

"And track meets remain on my calendar."

"Agreed."

"Then send me the data description. Not the whole giant dream."

Nia smiled. "The giant dream has excellent visual language."

"I am sure it does. Send the data."

The partnership began with a boundary, which made it stronger than a favor.

## Monday, December 29: Delete Reminder

Malik's private calendar contained a reminder he had created in October.

**Tell Nia after finals.**

No details. No explanation of what he intended to tell her. The reminder assumed finals would create a perfect moment, Nia's relationship would resolve somehow, Malik's family crisis would calm, and his patient feelings would become a claim worth presenting.

He stared at it.

Nia did not owe him a moment because he had waited. He did not owe his past self indefinite loyalty to a possibility that had never become a mutual decision.

Malik deleted the reminder.

The action did not erase his feelings. It changed what he planned to do with them.

For several minutes, he let himself grieve the scene that would never happen: finding Nia after finals, explaining that he had cared through every other relationship and crisis, hearing that his loyalty had finally become the right answer. The imagined scene had asked nothing of Nia except to recognize a role Malik had written for her.

He could admit the fantasy had comforted him without treating it as a contract she had broken.

Malik opened their message history, not to search for evidence, but to see the friendship as it actually existed: jokes, study plans, disagreements, storm updates, and honest support. That relationship was real. The promised romantic ending had been private.

Deleting the reminder protected the friendship from becoming an invoice.

He opened his spring calendar and added Simone's coffee without marking it "networking" or "analytics."

Then he completed a registration check. Calculus II, actuarial prerequisites, North Star hours, and one open evening remained visible. He practiced antiderivative patterns for thirty minutes, corrected two algebra errors, and stopped when the timer ended.

Moving forward looked less dramatic than confession.

It looked like making room for the future to contain evidence he did not already control.

## Tuesday, December 30: Warm and Final

Julian messaged Nia before New Year's Eve.

**Hope Huntsville has been good to you. Thank you for the good parts of this year. Wishing you a spring that feels like yours.**

Nia read the message slowly.

No question. No reference to missing her. No disguised invitation to restart the relationship at midnight.

She replied: **Thank you for the good parts too. I hope spring gives you stories worth telling and some you keep for yourself. Happy New Year, Julian.**

His response was a simple heart.

The exchange felt warm, sad, and finished.

Nia placed the phone down and returned to the proposal call with Keisha. They repaired an algebraic relationship in the simplified flow model and compared two graph interpretations. Keisha rejected a variable Nia could not define from available sources.

"The map looked better with it," Nia complained.

"We are not applying for Prettiest Unsupported Arrow."

"That award has prestige."

"No funding."

They removed the arrow.

## Wednesday, December 31: Background Laughter

At midnight, Nia called Malik.

Her family had counted down in the living room. Fireworks sounded several streets away. Nia expected Malik to answer from the Baptiste porch or the temporary living-room bed.

Instead, several voices filled the connection.

"Happy New Year," Malik said.

In the background, DJ shouted something about championship energy. Andre argued with a video filter. Then Simone laughed, clear and close enough to be unmistakable.

Nia's chest tightened.

"Group call?" she asked.

"Analytics team and DJ. Simone started it after somebody posted the wrong time zone."

"Sounds fun."

Her voice became brighter than the moment required.

Malik paused. "You okay?"

"Yes. Just loud here. Happy New Year."

"Happy New Year, Nia."

She ended the call before she could ask why Simone was there, whether they had talked over break, or why any answer mattered.

The fireworks continued.

Nia told herself the sharp feeling was surprise.

## Thursday, January 1: Open the Application

New Year's Day began quietly.

Nia woke late and saw no missed call from Malik. That was reasonable. They had spoken. Nothing had gone wrong.

The sharp feeling remained.

She considered calling Keisha and turning it into a full analysis. She considered calling Malik back with a casual question. She considered checking Simone's social media, which would have produced data and no wisdom.

Instead, Nia opened the research application.

The form asked for the question, team roles, available data, proposed methods, anticipated limitations, and how the work would involve affected communities.

She entered both names: **Nia Reynolds, mathematical modeling and communication. Keisha Morgan, computation and data-method co-lead.**

For the limitations section, Nia wrote that missing road-capacity values might prevent a reliable route recommendation. She did not promise the model would solve what the data could not support.

The work steadied her without erasing the feeling.

Spring would return with two new partnerships: Malik and Simone exploring something personal, Nia and Keisha building something competitive.

The emotional current had reversed before Nia was ready to name it.

---

# Week Twenty-Three: The First Return

DJ had placed the Heritage Bowl trophy beside an unfinished economics assignment.

Malik discovered both when he returned to the residence hall Friday afternoon. The trophy occupied the center of their shared shelf beneath a battery-powered spotlight DJ had apparently purchased for this purpose. Beside it, a laptop displayed half a paragraph about labor markets.

"One of these is evidence of completed work," Malik said.

DJ emerged from beneath his bed holding a missing shoe. "One is inspiration for future work."

"Your paper is due Monday."

"The trophy believes in me."

The room had been reorganized. DJ's expanded football schedule claimed a new section of wall. Malik's desk remained clear except for mail, his spring books, and a note from Ms. Alvarez confirming North Star shifts.

His old room in Gulfport remained unfinished. This one had become more recognizably home.

Malik unpacked his calculus materials and placed the analytics postmortem in a drawer rather than on top of the new semester.

Then his phone lit up with Simone's message.

**Coffee Sunday? Personal, in case the analyst remains confused.**

Malik replied: **Interpretation confirmed. Yes.**

## Friday, January 2: Reorganized

DJ showed Malik the scholarship update in his student account.

"Official," he said. "My mother checked twice and then made me check while she watched."

"Reasonable audit procedure."

"She also told me the money does not make me too important to complete economics."

"Strong methodology."

They worked side by side for an hour. Malik reviewed algebra and antiderivatives for Calculus II. DJ finished the missing paragraph, then argued that football had given him practical experience with labor specialization.

"You catch passes," Malik said.

"Specialized labor."

"You also block."

"Cross-functional expertise."

The familiar nonsense settled Malik into campus life more effectively than the unpacked boxes.

Before bed, he looked at Simone's coffee on his calendar. He felt hopeful, nervous, and free of the old obligation to compare every possibility with Nia.

That freedom was not complete.

It was real enough to begin.

## Saturday, January 3: The Skeptical Mentor

Research matching took place in Eliza Moss Hall.

Nia and Keisha pitched their evacuation-model idea to Dr. Price and a room of faculty, graduate mentors, and student teams. Nia explained the public question. Keisha explained the computational limits. Their application named missing road capacity as a central problem rather than an inconvenience to hide.

Dr. Price assigned them Alexis Grant, a second-year applied mathematics master's student.

Alexis read the one-page proposal without smiling.

"What decision will this model support?" she asked.

"Route recommendation under flood risk," Nia said.

"For whom?"

"Households and emergency planners."

"Those are different users with different authority. Which one?"

Nia glanced at Keisha.

"Emergency planners first," Keisha said. "Household communication would require another interface and trust assumptions we do not have."

Alexis nodded slightly. "What is your smallest defensible question?"

Nia described how missing road capacity might change which route appeared feasible.

"Better. Do not tell me you are saving Bellwether. Tell me what comparison you can complete by the campus forum."

The skepticism irritated Nia.

It also prevented the giant dream from swallowing the project before it began.

Alexis scheduled their first data audit for Wednesday.

## Sunday, January 4: Personal Coffee

Residence halls officially reopened Sunday, filling campus with rolling suitcases and reunion noise.

Simone met Malik at Copper Cup.

She wore a rust-colored coat over a cream sweater and had selected a table away from the foot traffic.

"No laptops," she said when he arrived.

"I did not bring one."

"Good. I was prepared to confiscate it."

Their coffee began with holiday stories. Simone described her younger brother attempting to build a stock portfolio using only companies whose commercials he liked. Malik described the non-Euclidean pie and showed photographic evidence.

"That crust needs structural analysis," Simone said.

"Nia recommended whipped cream stabilization."

The name entered naturally. Simone did not react. Malik did not turn the conversation toward Nia.

After an hour, Simone rested her elbows on the table.

"I asked you here because I like talking to you," she said. "Not because I want to rebuild analytics."

"I like talking to you too."

"Are you available to find out what that means?"

Malik thought of the deleted reminder, the friendship he still valued, and the distinction between being uncertain and being unavailable.

"Yes," he said.

This yes belonged to the person in front of him.

## Monday, January 5: Too Bright

Malik told Nia about the coffee during their first accountability check-in.

"Simone and I met yesterday," he said. "It was personal. We are going out again."

He did not ask whether Nia approved. He told her because their friendship had room for significant news.

Nia's smile arrived too quickly.

"That is great! Simone is amazing. Smart, direct, accomplished, very good at telling you when you are being ridiculous. Honestly, ideal."

Malik stared at her.

"What?"

"That was a lot of approval."

"I am happy for you."

"Thank you."

The exchange should have ended there. Instead, Nia asked where they went, how long they talked, and whether Simone had called it a date.

"Coffee," Malik said. "Personal coffee. We are having dinner Thursday."

"Dinner. Nice. Great."

Her brightness confused him. It confused her more.

That night, Nia told herself she was protective. Malik had been through a hard fall. Simone was a second-year with large ambitions. Nia only wanted clarity for her friend.

The explanation had too many words.

## Tuesday, January 6: What Can an Integral Measure?

Dr. Victor Delgado opened Calculus II with one question.

"What can an integral measure besides area?"

Hands rose.

"Volume," one student said.

"That is accumulated area. Try again."

"Distance."

"From what? Under which conditions?"

"Work."

"Define it."

Five memorized answers failed under follow-up questions.

Dr. Delgado was precise without being cold. He did not reject the topics; he rejected labels offered without relationships.

Nia described total change accumulated from a rate. Malik explained that integrating velocity gave displacement, while integrating speed gave distance traveled. Amara added units and conditions.

"Better," Dr. Delgado said. "Calculus II is not a catalog of integration tricks. Every method must answer why this structure represents this quantity."

He assigned an accumulated-area review using water inflow and outflow. Nia saw immediate connections to flood modeling. Malik saw repair costs and risk over time.

The rate graph crossed the horizontal axis twice. Positive regions represented water entering a reservoir; negative regions represented water leaving. A student added the absolute values and reported total movement.

Dr. Delgado shook his head. "The question asks for net change. Direction matters."

Nia split the integral at the sign changes, evaluated each signed region, and combined them. Malik checked that rate units multiplied by time produced volume. Amara explained why total water moved would require absolute accumulation instead.

"Same graph," Dr. Delgado said. "Different quantity. Your integral does not know what you meant to ask."

Nia wrote the sentence beside her research notes. A route model could accumulate travel time, vehicle count, or exposure, but the team had to decide which quantity matched the decision. Computation would not rescue a vague objective.

The course began by demanding meaning before technique.

Neither student could accuse that demand of being unfamiliar.

## Wednesday, January 7: The Model Cannot Run

Spring classes began Wednesday, along with add-drop and the research team's first data audit.

Keisha loaded the road network. Nia prepared flood-zone overlays. Alexis watched without helping them avoid the problem.

Road-capacity values were missing for nearly thirty percent of the network.

Not evenly missing. Smaller neighborhood roads had the least documentation, while major roads had the most. Filling every gap with an average would make poorly documented streets resemble well-measured roads.

"The proposed model cannot run as written," Keisha said.

Nia looked at Alexis. "Could we estimate from road width?"

"Do you have width?"

"For some roads."

"Then you have another missing-data pattern."

The team tested a simplified route set. Even there, capacity assumptions changed the recommended path.

Nia felt the proposal collapsing.

Keisha did not. "We asked how missing capacity changes recommendations. The missing data is not blocking the question. It *is* the question."

Alexis finally smiled. "Now decide what experiment can demonstrate that honestly."

They stayed late, replacing a promise of one best route with a comparison of how route choices shifted under plausible capacity scenarios.

## Thursday, January 8: Coffee Becomes Dinner

Malik met Simone after his North Star shift.

They began at Copper Cup again, but the cafe closed before they ran out of conversation. Simone suggested dinner at a small place off Cypress Walk.

Malik checked his schedule. He had left the evening open on purpose.

"Yes," he said.

Across campus, Nia and Keisha worked with Alexis to define the smallest research question their data could survive. Nia's phone showed no new message from Malik. That was normal. He was not required to report a date in progress.

At dinner, Simone asked Malik what he hoped spring would contain.

"Better calculus. Stable work hours. Progress on the house."

"Those are outcomes. What do you want to experience?"

Malik had no prepared answer.

"I do not know yet," he said.

"Good. Something we cannot turn into a deliverable."

They shared dessert and talked until the server began resetting nearby tables.

At Eliza Moss Hall, Nia erased the phrase **optimal evacuation route** and replaced it with **route sensitivity under missing capacity data**.

Two commitments took shape that night: one relationship and one research question, each becoming more honest as it became narrower.

---

# Week Twenty-Four: Volumes and Openings

Dr. Delgado refused to let anyone integrate.

He drew a region between two curves and announced that it would be rotated around a vertical line. Students reached for formulas.

"No computation," he said. "Choose a method first."

The room became uneasy.

Washers would slice perpendicular to the axis of rotation. Shells would slice parallel. Both could produce the same volume, but one setup required solving each curve for the other variable and splitting the region. The other used one clean integral.

"Shells," Malik said.

"Why?"

"Vertical slices match the current equations. Rotating them around a vertical axis creates shells. We avoid rewriting and splitting."

Dr. Delgado looked at Nia. "Would washers be wrong?"

"No. Just less efficient here. If the axis or equations changed, the choice could change."

"Exactly. Technique is a decision, not a reflex."

Only after the class defended its choice did he allow the first integral.

Nia drew a shell beside the region. Malik labeled radius, height, and thickness. The volume came last.

## Friday, January 9: Choose Before Computing

The class compared washer and shell methods across several solids.

Dr. Delgado required a short justification before every setup. "Because I remember the formula" earned no credit. Students had to name the slice, axis, radius or radii, and reason the method reduced complexity.

Nia liked the visual logic. She could imagine each rectangle turning through space. Malik liked that method choice could be defended by structure rather than preference.

Amara found a case where both methods required two integrals.

"Which is better?" she asked.

"Define better," Dr. Delgado replied.

Fewer algebraic steps? Easier bounds? Lower risk of sign error? More meaningful units? The answer depended on what they were trying to protect.

After class, Malik left to meet Simone for lunch. He did not announce it.

Nia noticed anyway.

She returned to her research notes and drew flood zones as regions. If a region rotated, she could visualize volume. If water depth varied across the actual city, the elegant solid became an approximation with geography hidden inside it.

Her project demanded a method choice too.

## Saturday, January 10: Elegant and Impossible

Nia presented the team with a polished three-dimensional sketch.

The flood zone became a rotated region. Height represented water depth. Concentric layers suggested growing exposure. The image was elegant enough for a poster.

Keisha placed the road network over it.

The recommended paths crossed water, ignored a rail line, and sent traffic toward a bridge with incomplete capacity data.

"Your picture is beautiful," Keisha said.

"I hear the word *but*."

"But the roads did not agree to become radial coordinates."

Alexis asked what the visual was supposed to establish.

"Accumulated exposure," Nia said.

"Then do not let the audience confuse exposure volume with an evacuation network."

They separated the layers. One visual estimated accumulated flood exposure. Another displayed the real road graph. A third showed where uncertain road capacities changed feasible routes.

To test the distinction, Nia used washers to estimate the volume represented by one simplified cross-section. Outer radius described the projected flood boundary; inner radius excluded a raised region. Keisha verified the numerical setup, then pointed to three roads inside the same geometric band.

One road crossed a low underpass. One ended at a locked service gate. The third appeared connected on the map but lacked a verified capacity value.

"The volume estimate can be mathematically sound," Keisha said, "and still tell us nothing about whether a bus can reach a shelter."

Alexis made Nia write that limitation directly beneath the image. The picture could describe exposure. It could not stand in for transportation access.

The result was less elegant and more truthful.

Nia saved the original image in a folder labeled **ATTRACTIVE WRONG IDEAS**.

Keisha approved. "That folder will be full by March."

"From both of us."

"Correct answer."

## Sunday, January 11: After the Error

The Heritage Tip-Off began with the women's basketball game at Ruth Ellis Fieldhouse.

Nia sat with Keisha and Imani. The McCall-Hart women led early, then lost the lead after two turnovers. A first-year guard made the second mistake in front of the student section and immediately looked toward the bench.

"That walk back is the longest distance in sports," Imani said.

As a softball shortstop, she knew what a visible error felt like: the ball rolling beneath a glove, the crowd reacting before the play finished, the next batter arriving while shame still occupied the last pitch.

The guard returned in the fourth quarter. She did not take a miraculous game-winning shot. She defended well, made the simple pass, and helped stabilize the offense during a winning run.

"People think confidence means forgetting the error," Imani said. "Usually it means making the next choice while remembering it."

Nia thought about the attractive wrong image, her first relationship, and the too-bright congratulations she had given Malik.

"How do you know when to trust yourself again?" she asked.

"You do not wait for trust to become a feeling. You prepare, then make the next defensible choice."

McCall-Hart won by seven.

Nia left the fieldhouse with no clear answer about Malik, only a better rule for what not to do next.

## Monday, January 12: Leave on Time

The men's Heritage Tip-Off game ran late.

Overtime began at 7:28. Malik's dinner with Simone was scheduled for eight. The student section begged everyone to stay. DJ argued that leaving overtime violated campus law.

At 7:34, Malik stood.

"The game is tied," DJ said.

"I can see that."

"History is occurring."

"Simone is waiting at eight."

"Tell her basketball requested an extension."

Malik put on his coat. "I told her I would be there."

Nia watched from two seats away as he left before the final score.

In fall, Malik had rushed a calculus test for analytics. Nia had tried to squeeze a review and Julian's showcase into one evening. Now Malik chose the prior commitment and accepted missing the campus moment.

He arrived at the restaurant at 7:56.

Simone glanced at the game updates on his phone. "You left overtime?"

"Yes."

"I am impressed and slightly concerned about your school spirit."

"DJ has enough for both of us."

McCall-Hart won while they ordered dinner.

Malik was disappointed to miss it.

He did not make Simone pay for the choice.

## Tuesday, January 13: Outside Achievement

Spring add-drop ended Tuesday, locking schedules into something more serious.

At dinner, Simone returned to the question Malik had not answered Thursday.

"What do you want outside achievement?"

Malik considered giving a polished response. He had none.

"Stability," he said. "A home where one storm does not undo everything. Work that pays enough for my parents to stop treating every bill like a threat."

"Those are still outcomes."

"I know."

He looked at his hands. "I want to be able to enjoy something while it is happening. I want people to tell me bad news without worrying I will turn it into a plan before they finish. I want to rest without feeling like someone else is becoming more prepared than me."

Simone's expression softened.

"That is unfinished," Malik said.

"Good. I asked what you want, not for a mission statement."

He asked her the same question.

Simone wanted work that placed her in consequential rooms, travel, financial independence, and relationships that did not require her to make ambition smaller to appear loyal.

Neither promise fit perfectly with the other.

They liked the honesty enough to keep learning.

## Wednesday, January 14: The Smile

Malik returned to the residence hall courtyard smiling.

Nia was crossing toward North Star with research maps under one arm. She saw him before he saw her.

The smile was not the public grin from a team announcement. It was loose, private, and attached to whatever message he had just read from Simone.

Nia stopped walking.

All fall, she had assumed Malik's feelings would remain where she left them: patient, quiet, available if she ever looked directly. She had never asked him to wait. She had also benefited from believing he would.

Malik noticed her. "Hey."

"Hey. Good date?"

"Yes."

No apology. No attempt to make the answer easier for her.

"Good," Nia said, less brightly this time.

He asked about the research maps. Nia explained the road-network problem. Malik listened, asked one useful question, and continued toward his room.

She watched him go.

The sharp feeling from New Year's Eve finally acquired a more honest name.

Loss of possibility.

And perhaps desire.

## Thursday, January 15: No Plan to Interfere

Nia told Keisha after track conditioning.

"I may have feelings for Malik."

Keisha drank half a bottle of water before responding. "May?"

"Fine. I have feelings for Malik."

"And Malik is dating Simone."

"They have been on two dates."

Keisha looked at her.

"I know how that sounded," Nia said. "I am not trying to interfere."

"Good, because I am not helping you build a strategy."

"I did not ask."

"Your face opened a planning meeting."

Nia sat beside her on the bench. "What am I supposed to do?"

"Feel it. Respect their relationship. Keep being honest with yourself. Do not turn friendship into a campaign."

"That sounds deeply inactive."

"It is active restraint. You should recognize the concept by now."

Nia groaned.

Keisha continued, "Also, our proposal is due soon. My code does not care about your romantic reversal."

"The code has poor emotional intelligence."

"The code has boundaries."

Nia opened the research folder.

The spring relationship had begun. So had her feelings. Only one of those facts gave her permission to act.

---

# Week Twenty-Five: Work Worth Doing

By nine Friday morning, the gym at Bellwether Community Center had become a machine made of folding tables, canned beans, clipboards, and people shouting helpful instructions in opposite directions.

Nia stood near the entrance holding a map that said the line should move clockwise.

The line had other plans.

Families entered through the east doors, checked in beside the bleachers, crossed to the produce tables, doubled back for dry goods, and then collided with volunteers bringing boxes from the loading dock. On paper, the path was a clean loop. In reality, one grandmother had stopped beside the oranges to reorganize three bags, two toddlers were conducting an unauthorized sprint relay, and a pallet of bottled water had narrowed the safest aisle to one person at a time.

Keisha stepped around a rolling cart. "Your map is being defeated by juice boxes."

"My map is a preliminary model."

"Your preliminary model is still losing."

Nia looked down at the evacuation network she and Keisha had brought for comparison. Roads, capacities, and shortest paths covered the page. It assumed movement happened along available edges. It did not account for people waiting, carrying supplies, helping relatives, or reaching a narrow point at the same time.

She circled the crowded aisle.

"Congestion," she said.

"Here? Absolutely."

"In our model. We treat a route like it stays equally usable no matter how many people choose it."

Keisha stopped teasing. "That is not a small problem."

"No," Nia said, watching the line bend around the water. "It is finally a useful one."

## Friday, January 16: The Map Meets the Room

The service project was part of McCall-Hart's Martin Luther King Jr. weekend, which meant students arrived in matching cream volunteer shirts and spent the first fifteen minutes taking pictures before anyone trusted them near inventory.

Malik and Simone were assigned to packing. Malik counted items with quiet precision. Simone redesigned the station after observing that everyone reached across everyone else for rice.

"You moved my beans," Malik said.

"I improved your beans."

"They were alphabetized by category."

"They are canned goods, not scholarship applications."

Within ten minutes, their table filled boxes faster than either neighboring station. Malik staged empty cartons. Simone called quantities. When supplies ran low, she announced it before the line stopped. They did not compete for control because each noticed what the other was already doing well.

Across the gym, Nia and Keisha timed how long families spent at each station. They asked permission before recording anything and used station numbers rather than names. A route that looked shortest was often slowest because it crossed the loading path. An older volunteer explained that the center normally placed water near the exit, but a delivery delay had forced them to improvise.

"So today's bottleneck is not permanent," Nia said.

"None of them are," the volunteer replied. "That is why a pretty plan can embarrass you."

Nia wrote the sentence in her notebook.

At cleanup, she showed Keisha a new question: how sensitive were recommended evacuation routes to missing road-capacity information and changing congestion?

Keisha read it twice. "Now we have something testable."

"Not just admirable?"

"Admirable does not compile."

Nia laughed. The project felt less like a promise to save everybody and more like work they could honestly do.

## Saturday, January 17: Clear Terms

The second service shift ended with flattened boxes stacked shoulder-high and Malik discovering rice inside one shoe.

Simone sat beside him on the community-center steps while volunteers waited for the campus shuttle. The winter sun was bright without being warm.

"We make a good station," Malik said.

"An irresistible declaration."

"I had a more complete thought."

"I was hoping."

He smiled, then became serious. "I like what we are doing. I do not want to keep calling it two dinners and coffee as if naming it would damage it."

Simone rested her forearms on her knees. "What name are you proposing?"

Malik had rehearsed several sentences. All of them sounded like contract language now.

"I want to date you exclusively," he said. "If you want that too."

Simone did not rescue him from the silence. She considered the offer with the same respect she gave important data.

"I do," she said. "Exclusively, but not invisibly. I am applying for internships, including one overseas. I am not shrinking that plan to make a relationship feel safer."

"I would not ask you to."

"You might try to solve the distance before it exists."

Malik conceded the point with one lifted hand.

"And you," Simone continued, "do not get to present calm when you are actually upset and then expect me to discover the difference."

"That seems specific."

"I pay attention."

"Then yes. Clear terms."

"You make dating sound like a well-designed study."

"Replication is important."

She laughed and leaned against his shoulder. When the shuttle arrived, they boarded as boyfriend and girlfriend, though neither said the titles aloud until DJ asked why Malik looked as if his credit score had improved.

## Sunday, January 18: The Long Way Home

Nia heard the news over brunch.

Malik said it simply. Simone reached for the hot sauce. Keisha studied her waffle with the concentration of a person refusing to become an emergency-response team.

"That is good," Nia said. "Really. Congratulations."

She meant it.

That was the inconvenient part.

Simone deserved someone who listened to her ambition without treating it as a threat. Malik deserved a relationship he had chosen rather than a waiting room built around Nia's uncertainty. Wanting those things for them did not prevent something inside Nia from folding in on itself.

After brunch, Malik and Simone walked toward the Porter Center. Nia said she needed to stop by the library.

She did not.

Instead, she took the longest reasonable route to North Star Hall. She crossed the arts quad, circled Ruth Ellis Fieldhouse, and followed the campus edge where bare trees scratched at the blue sky. Her phone offered shortcuts she ignored.

The walk was not punishment. It was privacy.

She did not want to perform being unaffected for friends who knew her too well. She also did not want pain to turn into evidence that Malik had done something wrong.

Near the residence hall, she found Julian loading saxophone cases into a van.

"You know North Star is the other direction," he said.

"I am pursuing a scenic theory of navigation."

"Your theory looks cold."

He offered no questions. She offered no explanation. They lifted the final case together, exchanged a small goodbye, and continued with separate days.

In her room, Nia opened the research map. A longer route was not always a mistake. Sometimes the shortest available path cost more than distance could measure.

## Monday, January 19: What the Work Requires

The university was closed for Martin Luther King Jr. Day, but Dr. Delgado had posted one optional problem to prepare for Tuesday.

A cart carrying relief supplies moved along a route while the force required to push it changed with distance. Students had to calculate the work.

Nia nearly skipped it. Then she pictured the community center: bottled water narrowing an aisle, full bags becoming heavier, volunteers slowing at turns.

The integral did not simply multiply one force by one distance. It accumulated changing effort across many tiny intervals.

She covered her desk with notes.

If evacuation difficulty changed along a road, then route length alone was not enough. A mile with reliable capacity, level access, and low congestion was different from a mile that narrowed near a flooded underpass. They could define a cost that changed by segment, then test how recommendations shifted when uncertain capacities changed.

Keisha returned from an early conditioning session and found Nia talking to a diagram.

"Should I give you privacy?"

"The diagram and I are developing a weighted route cost."

"That sounds serious."

Nia explained distance, expected load, access constraints, and uncertainty. Keisha listened, then opened her laptop.

"We can run scenarios instead of pretending we know one exact capacity," she said. "But we have to state which weights are assumptions."

"Agreed."

"And we are not calling it the Reynolds model."

"I was thinking Morgan-Reynolds."

"Alphabetical order has briefly saved you."

They worked until dinner, building a method that admitted what it did not know. The mathematics did not make the project noble. It made the work accountable.

## Tuesday, January 20: A Question, Not a Halo

Dr. Alana Price read their symposium draft in complete silence.

Nia had written about flooding, unequal access, vulnerable neighborhoods, and the urgent need for better evacuation planning. The introduction glowed with purpose. The method occupied four uncertain lines near the bottom.

Dr. Price placed the pages on her desk.

"You care," she said.

"Yes."

"That is not a research method."

Nia felt the sentence hit every paragraph she had polished.

Dr. Price tapped the title. "Symposium reviewers are not judging whether flooding is sad or whether service is good. They are judging whether you asked a precise question, used a defensible method, and understood your limits. Noble intentions can still produce unreliable claims."

Keisha turned the draft sideways and began crossing out entire sentences.

Nia stared at her. "Those took hours."

"They are wearing a halo where the method should be."

Dr. Price almost smiled.

Together, they narrowed the application. They would not claim to identify the best evacuation plan for Bellwether. They would test how missing or uncertain road-capacity data changed route recommendations in a defined sample network. They would compare scenarios, report instability, and name assumptions.

"Better," Dr. Price said. "Now who made which decisions?"

Nia began, "I developed--"

Keisha's chair made one quiet sound against the floor.

Nia stopped.

The application named Nia first. It described Keisha as technical support.

The project had survived its first inflated claim. Its next problem was sitting beside her.

## Wednesday, January 21: Put Her Name on the Decision

Keisha did not argue in Dr. Price's office. That would have made the correction too easy.

Back at North Star, she changed for track practice while Nia stood beside the desk holding the application.

"I wrote that badly," Nia said.

"You wrote what you believed."

"No. I know you are a coauthor."

"Then describe my authorship."

Keisha tied one shoe. "Who chose the data structure? Who built the scenario runner? Who flagged that one missing capacity should not become zero by accident? Who decided we need uncertainty ranges instead of one dramatic result?"

"You did."

"That is not support. Those are research decisions."

Nia wanted to explain that she had been rushing, that application language was awkward, that she had not meant to diminish anyone. Each explanation placed her intention at the center again.

"You are right," she said. "I will rewrite it. You decide whether the new version is accurate."

While Keisha practiced, Nia rebuilt the contribution statement. She named their shared question development. She credited Keisha's data-cleaning rules, code architecture, scenario design, and decision to preserve uncertainty. She described her own mathematical formulation, literature synthesis, and interpretation plan. She replaced every vague *we* with an actual responsibility where the distinction mattered.

Keisha returned after dark, damp with sweat and unimpressed by Nia's hopeful expression.

She read the new version.

"Move scenario design above implementation," she said. "The decision came before the code."

Nia moved it.

Keisha read again, then typed her name into the coauthor field herself.

"I am in," she said.

Nia did not hug her without asking. She did, however, perform a seated dance so embarrassing that Keisha threatened to withdraw on aesthetic grounds.

## Thursday, January 22: A Clean Exit

Malik found the symposium draft open on a shared-lab monitor Thursday evening.

Nia was at the whiteboard, trying to write a route-cost expression without making every variable look equally certain. Malik read the title, then the research question.

"This is sharper," he said.

She turned. "Sharper than the version you saw last week?"

"That version wanted to solve evacuation. This one asks what your data can support."

He pointed to the scenario table. "You are measuring how often the recommended route changes?"

"And where it changes. Keisha thinks instability near one bridge could matter more than an average over the whole network."

"She is right."

"I know. I credited her this time."

Malik looked at her, understanding more than the sentence directly said. "Good."

For several minutes, they talked about the model. It was familiar and easy. Nia felt the old rhythm return: question, answer, correction, dry joke. She wanted to ask whether becoming official felt different. She wanted to tell him that she had taken the long route Sunday. She wanted, most dangerously, to see whether the quiet between them could still become something else.

Malik checked the time.

"I am meeting Simone," he said. "Your question is strong. Submit it."

He packed his notebook and left before Nia could turn intellectual closeness into emotional trespassing.

The door clicked shut.

Nia faced the whiteboard again. Respecting a boundary did not require pretending the feeling vanished. It required deciding what the feeling was allowed to do.

She completed the equation, sent the draft to Keisha, and waited for her coauthor's approval before touching the submit button.

---

# Week Twenty-Six: Choosing a Method

Dr. Delgado wrote three integrals across the board.

At first glance, they looked like siblings: each contained a product, each included an exponential or trigonometric expression, and each appeared designed to ruin somebody's morning.

"Which formula are we using?" a student asked.

"That," Dr. Delgado said, "is the question that causes the trouble."

One integral yielded to substitution. One required integration by parts. The third could be manipulated either way, but one choice produced a longer problem than the one they started with.

Nia raised her hand. "Can we have a decision tree?"

"You may build one after you understand what the branches mean."

A groan moved across the room.

Dr. Delgado did not sympathize. "Methods are not recipes you match by appearance. Ask what changes when you differentiate one part. Ask what simplifies. Ask what new difficulty your choice creates."

Malik stared at the third integral. "So choosing a valid method is not enough."

"Correct. You are responsible for where the method takes you."

Nia wrote the sentence beside the symposium deadline in her notebook.

## Friday, January 23: The Recipe Revolt

By the end of class, the board looked like a family argument conducted in symbols.

Amara had chosen the wrong factor for (u) in integration by parts and produced an integral more complicated than the original. Nia used substitution where no matching differential existed. Malik reached a correct answer, but only after two pages of algebra that Dr. Delgado reduced to six lines.

"I object to the phrase *only after*," Malik said.

"Your objection has been recorded and denied."

Dr. Delgado made them annotate each attempt: what they noticed, why they selected a method, and the first sign that the choice was helping or failing.

Nia hated the exercise until she saw the pattern. Her worst decisions were not random. She noticed one familiar feature, committed too early, and then defended the method because changing course felt like admitting defeat.

After class, she met Keisha in the lab for the research data audit.

"Today I learned we are allowed to abandon a method," Nia said.

"I have been telling you that since August."

"Dr. Delgado used notation."

"I will add notation to future complaints."

They divided the evacuation records. Nia checked route assumptions and units. Keisha tested identifiers, missing values, and duplicate rows. Their application had been submitted, but Dr. Price required a full audit before they presented a single result.

For two hours, everything looked encouraging.

Then Keisha said, "Do not celebrate that graph."

## Saturday, January 24: The Beautiful Duplicate

Nia's first graph was excellent news.

Under most simulated capacity changes, the recommended routes remained stable. The result suggested their network could tolerate uncertain data better than expected. The bars were clean. The colors were accessible. The title almost wrote itself.

Keisha turned her laptop around.

"These records have different row numbers but the same location, time stamp, and route code."

"Could two reports be identical?"

"Four hundred and twelve times?"

Nia's excitement began backing slowly toward the exit.

The duplicated records were not evenly distributed. One well-documented route appeared hundreds of times more often than several uncertain routes. Their graph did not prove broad stability. It proved that repeated copies of one stable case could dominate an average.

"Maybe the duplicates represent confirmed observations," Nia said.

Keisha gave her a look.

"I heard it while I was saying it."

They traced the error to merged files from two municipal exports. One dataset had already included the other's records. Nothing malicious had happened. The mistake was ordinary, quiet, and powerful enough to manufacture good news.

Nia stared at the graph. "The application deadline passed."

"Our question is still valid."

"Our preliminary result is not."

"Then we do not use it."

Nia saved the audit notes, removed the graph from the slide deck, and renamed the image **DO_NOT_RESURRECT.png**.

"Very technical," Keisha said.

"I want future me to feel warned."

The corrected data produced a messier picture: several route recommendations changed dramatically when capacity estimates moved. It was less comforting and far more relevant.

## Sunday, January 25: Delete in Daylight

Dr. Price convened the team Sunday afternoon. Alexis joined by video from the graduate lab, her face appearing above a stack of route maps.

Nia projected the beautiful graph first.

"This result is invalid," she said.

She explained the duplicate records, the biased average, and the corrected output. Then she deleted the graph from the active presentation while everyone watched.

There was no applause. Good research habits rarely received movie music.

Alexis asked why the duplicates had survived their first cleaning step.

Keisha explained that the files used distinct row identifiers, so exact-row detection failed. She proposed a composite check using location, time, and route attributes, followed by manual review of ambiguous matches.

"And what changes in the claim?" Dr. Price asked.

Nia took a breath. "We cannot say recommendations are generally stable. We can identify which parts of this sample network are sensitive to plausible capacity changes. The instability may be the finding."

"May be," Dr. Price repeated. "Keep that word until the evidence earns stronger language."

After the meeting, Nia expected to feel humiliated. Instead, she felt disappointed and strangely lighter. The false graph no longer required protection.

Keisha bumped her shoulder. "You deleted it before anyone had to wrestle the mouse away."

"Growth."

"Do not become unbearable about it."

They began the corrected run. Progress did not look like recovering the result Nia wanted. It looked like trusting the question enough to survive an inconvenient answer.

## Monday, January 26: Her Room, Her Work

The Porter Center career panel was called **Data in High-Stakes Financial Decisions**, a title that guaranteed half the audience came for insight and the other half for free sandwiches.

Simone sat onstage beside an alumna from a regional bank and a professor who studied consumer credit. She spoke about model outputs that appeared objective while encoding historical decisions about who received loans, which neighborhoods attracted investment, and whose missing data was treated as risk.

Malik sat in the third row.

He had helped Simone practice Sunday night, but none of her answers belonged to him. When questions opened, he waited while several students asked about salaries and software certifications.

Then he raised his hand.

"When a model influences a financial decision," he asked, "what should an analyst document so someone outside the technical team can challenge its assumptions?"

Simone's expression brightened, not because he had rescued her, but because the question gave her room.

She discussed model cards, data lineage, uncertainty, monitoring, and the need to record who had authority to override an automated recommendation. The alumna expanded on regulatory review. The conversation became the strongest part of the panel.

Afterward, Malik waited while other students approached Simone. He did not hover behind her chair or tell anyone they were dating. When she finally joined him, she carried three internship contacts and one untouched sandwich.

"Good question," she said.

"Good answer."

"You knew I had one."

"That was the point."

She kissed his cheek in the crowded hallway, then handed him the sandwich because she knew he had skipped lunch.

Supporting her had not required becoming visible in her success. It required paying attention to what she wanted the room to understand.

## Tuesday, January 27: A Seat Without a Sequel

Ruth Ellis Fieldhouse was almost full when Nia arrived for the men's basketball game.

Keisha was at track study hall. Imani was helping with softball recruits. The only open seat Nia could see was beside Julian, who had a camera bag under one knee and a paper tray of fries balanced on the other.

"This seat comes with media obligations," he said.

"I refuse to be interviewed during free throws."

"Reasonable boundary."

She sat.

The conversation began with the game and moved to the Marching Herons' spring travel schedule. Julian was helping produce short documentaries about student musicians balancing rehearsal, classes, and road trips. One trumpet player had recorded a statistics lecture from a hotel stairwell because it was the only quiet place in the building.

"You would like her," Julian said. "She labels every practice recording by date and emotional damage."

Nia laughed. "That is excellent metadata."

They did not discuss why their relationship ended. They did not test whether old tenderness remained. They talked about work they cared about, shared fries, and complained when an official missed an obvious traveling call.

Across the fieldhouse, Malik saw them.

His first reaction arrived before his better judgment: a tightness in his chest, a story about Nia and Julian finding their way back to each other, an old desire to know whether he had lost something.

Then Simone texted him a photograph of four internship application tabs and the caption: **ROMANCE IS TEMPORARILY UNDER REVIEW.**

Malik smiled, but the jealousy did not disappear.

He decided not to feed it with imagination.

## Wednesday, January 28: Ask the Actual Person

Malik found Nia outside Dr. Delgado's classroom.

"Can I ask you something directly?"

Nia closed her notebook. The formality in his voice made her brace herself. "Yes."

"Are you and Julian considering getting back together?"

The question was so clean that she had no place to hide inside it.

"No. We happened to sit together. We talked about his band project."

Malik nodded once.

Nia studied him. "Why are you asking?"

"Because I saw you, felt jealous, and did not want to invent an explanation."

Her heart made one disloyal leap at the word *jealous*.

Malik continued before she could build a future out of it. "The feeling is mine to understand. I am not asking you to change anything."

That sentence returned the room to its proper dimensions.

"Julian and I had closure," Nia said. "Not the fake kind where people say they are fine and then communicate through playlists. We mattered to each other. We are still over."

"Thank you."

"Are you going to tell Simone you asked?"

"Yes."

Nia believed him.

Dr. Delgado opened the classroom door and announced that anyone conducting emotional conferences in the hallway should conduct them elsewhere or begin evaluating an integral.

"We choose the integral," Nia said quickly.

"Speak for yourself," Malik replied, but he followed her inside.

## Thursday, January 29: Wanting the Wrong Proof

Nia told Keisha about the conversation while they checked the corrected dataset.

"He was jealous," Nia said.

"That is a feeling, not a proposal."

"I know."

"Your voice has created a parade for something you know."

Nia lowered her voice. "He cared."

"He asked a direct question, accepted your answer, and plans to be honest with his girlfriend. Those are good actions. Do not convert them into secret permission."

The correction stung because it was accurate.

Nia had told Malik the conversation with Julian was closure. It was true. She had also wanted Malik's jealousy to prove that his relationship with Simone had not changed what existed between them.

That was not a demand she had the right to make.

Across campus, Malik told Simone exactly what had happened.

She listened without pretending the admission was comfortable.

"Do you want to be with Nia?" she asked.

"I chose to be with you," he said.

"That is an action. I asked about the feeling."

Malik looked down. "Some of it is unresolved. I do not want to act on it."

Simone appreciated the truth and disliked needing it. Both reactions were allowed.

"Then do not make me manage what you refuse to examine," she said.

"I will not."

Neither called the conversation a victory. They stayed, asked harder questions, and made plans for Friday.

In the lab, Nia reviewed the corrected route results. She highlighted uncertainty instead of hiding it. A method was not good because it reached the answer she wanted. A relationship was not meaningful because it supplied the proof she wanted either.

Both required a more honest choice.

---

# Week Twenty-Seven: Identity in the Integral

Nia volunteered for the board before Dr. Delgado finished asking.

The integral contained an odd power of sine and an even power of cosine. She knew the pattern. Save one sine factor, convert the remaining sine squared using an identity, and substitute.

She wrote (1+\cos^2 x) where (1-\cos^2 x) belonged.

The error sat in the center of the board, large and confident.

Nia continued for three more lines before Amara raised one careful hand.

"Should that be minus?"

Nia stopped.

Every instinct told her to repair the line quickly enough that the class might forget who had made it. Instead, she stepped aside.

"Yes," she said. "I used the wrong identity. Will you finish from there?"

Amara approached the board with visible surprise. She corrected the sign, completed the substitution, and reached the answer Nia had been charging toward.

Dr. Delgado asked Nia what had gone wrong.

"I recognized the problem type and stopped checking the actual relationship."

"And what repaired it?"

Nia looked at Amara. "Letting someone else see what I could not."

## Friday, January 30: Finish the Solution

At lunch, Nia replayed the board mistake as if embarrassment improved with repetition.

Keisha stabbed a piece of broccoli. "You made a sign error, not a declaration of war."

"I began with tremendous confidence."

"That is your natural habitat."

"Amara had to finish my solution."

"And the university remained open."

Nia knew the embarrassment was not really about trigonometry. She liked being the person who moved first, connected ideas, and made the room less afraid. Stepping aside felt dangerously close to becoming unnecessary.

Later, she sent Amara a photograph of the corrected work with a thank-you. Amara replied that she usually knew the answer but talked herself out of raising her hand.

**Then we both practiced something,** Nia wrote.

In the research lab, the corrected data run finished. Keisha projected a network map covered in route changes. A few uncertain roads caused recommendations to swing across entire neighborhoods.

Nia nearly began assigning tasks. She caught herself.

"What do you think we should examine first?" she asked.

Keisha zoomed toward the bridge corridor. "Not the largest average change. This cluster. It affects fewer routes, but those routes have no close alternative."

Nia saw it after Keisha pointed it out.

Being a co-lead did not mean finishing every solution. Sometimes it meant recognizing whose view made the answer possible.

## Saturday, January 31: Advancement With Notes

The campus research office sent its decision at 10:07 Saturday morning.

Nia was brushing her teeth when her phone began vibrating across the sink. She grabbed it with one wet hand and read the first line.

Their proposal had advanced to the campus presentation round.

Her scream sent Keisha running from the bedroom holding one track shoe.

"We advanced!"

Keisha screamed too. They jumped in a circle, nearly collided with the shower curtain, and called Dr. Price before remembering it was Saturday.

Then they read the reviewer notes.

The question was timely. The sensitivity approach was promising. The division of technical contributions was clear.

The assumptions about human decisions were vague.

Why would residents choose the modeled route? Did access to a vehicle change the recommendation? What happened when families traveled together, waited for relatives, or avoided roads they considered unsafe? The model treated route choice as if every person responded to the same cost in the same way.

Nia sat on the closed toilet lid, celebration temporarily adjourned.

"They are right," she said.

"Probably," Keisha replied. "But we should not invent human behavior because reviewers asked for it."

They called Alexis, who told them not to fix social uncertainty with decorative numbers.

"Separate what your model decides from what a person decides," she said. "Then ask people who understand the difference."

Advancement did not mean approval of everything they had done. It meant the work was strong enough to receive more demanding questions.

Nia printed the reviewer notes and taped them above her desk.

## Sunday, February 1: Not Everybody's Weekend

Nia scheduled a six-hour Sunday rehearsal without consulting anyone.

She created an agenda, assigned sections, and brought enough snacks to make the assumption look generous. Keisha arrived at noon in track warmups.

"I have to leave at two," she said.

"The practice presentation is at four."

"My meet check-in is at three."

Nia blinked. "Today?"

"It has been on the calendar for a month."

"Can you join remotely afterward?"

Keisha stared at her. "After I race?"

Nia heard herself and disliked the sound.

"I am sorry. I assumed we would work through the weekend."

"You assumed *my* weekend belonged to the project."

Keisha packed her laptop. She had already completed the data-audit slides and left notes for the sections she would miss.

"We advanced because of both of us," she said. "That does not make you the owner of my time."

After she left, the room felt too large for Nia's agenda.

Nia shortened the rehearsal. She moved the code demonstration to Monday and sent Keisha a revised schedule rather than an apology disguised as another request.

Then she attended the meet.

She did not bring presentation notes.

## Monday, February 2: A Life Beside the Project

Keisha crouched in the starting blocks beneath white fieldhouse lights.

Nia sat with Imani high in the bleachers. She knew Keisha trained, of course. She had seen the alarm go off before sunrise and watched damp practice clothes colonize their bathroom. But knowing an activity existed was not the same as understanding that somebody had built a life inside it.

The starter raised the pistol.

Keisha exploded forward.

For less than a minute, there was no code, no symposium, no Nia. There was the curve of the indoor track, the tight rhythm of spikes, and Keisha hunting the stagger ahead of her. She crossed the line second in her heat and bent with both hands on her knees.

The board updated.

Personal best.

Nia shouted so loudly that Imani moved one seat away for hearing preservation.

Afterward, Keisha accepted Nia's congratulations but not an instant repair.

"I should have asked before scheduling," Nia said. "And I should know your meet calendar because you matter, not because I need to work around it."

Keisha considered that.

"Better apology."

"I practiced."

"Do not over-celebrate basic respect."

They took a picture beneath the results board. Back on campus, Nia rebuilt the project calendar with shared availability, separate task owners, and explicit approval before major meetings moved.

Keisha's goals did not orbit the research project. The project was fortunate to travel beside them.

## Tuesday, February 3: The Planned Road Trip

Malik sent Simone a road-trip itinerary with departure time, fuel stop, arena parking, dinner options, backup dinner options, and a weather contingency.

She replied with a single question.

**Are we attending basketball or evacuating a nation?**

They were driving two hours to an away game with a student-alumni group. Malik wanted to leave at 2:15. Simone wanted to stop at a bookstore she had found near the highway and decide about dinner after the game.

"After the game, every restaurant near the arena will be crowded," Malik said.

"Then we wait or go somewhere else."

"That is avoidable uncertainty."

"It is dinner, Malik."
