The disagreement sharpened in the Porter Center lobby. To Malik, planning protected limited money and time. To Simone, planning every hour made a short trip feel like a supervised field exercise.

"You said you wanted to enjoy things while they happen," she reminded him.

"Enjoyment does not require logistical failure."

"Spontaneity is not failure."

They stood in silence as the group loaded the bus.

Finally, Malik folded the itinerary and tore off the lower half.

"We keep the departure and tickets," he said. "You choose one unscheduled stop."

"One?"

"I am negotiating against my nature."

Simone accepted.

They visited the bookstore, missed Malik's planned dinner reservation, and ate excellent tacos from a truck outside the arena. McCall-Hart lost by four, but the trip did not.

## Wednesday, February 4: Compatible Is Not Identical

On the ride home, Simone admitted that the bookstore had almost made them late.

Malik admitted the tacos were better than his reservation.

"Please document that conclusion," she said. "I may need it in future disputes."

"The sample size is one."

"A historic one."

They laughed quietly while most of the bus slept.

The disagreement had not revealed a fatal incompatibility. It had revealed two reasonable needs that could become unreasonable when treated as universal rules. Malik wanted predictability because surprises had often arrived carrying bills, damage, or bad news. Simone wanted room because ambition already placed enough of her life on schedules built by institutions and deadlines.

"I do not want every trip to be unplanned," she said.

"I do not want every minute assigned."

"That sounded painful."

"It was."

They agreed that future trips would have fixed anchors and open space. Neither described the compromise as one person winning.

Back at McCall-Hart after midnight, Malik walked Simone to her residence hall. She had an internship application due the next evening. He had an integration quiz in the morning.

They did not turn tiredness into a test of affection. They said goodnight and went to do separate work.

Compatibility, Malik was learning, was not sameness. It was the ability to remain curious when difference interrupted the plan.

## Thursday, February 5: Shared Ownership

Nia opened Thursday's team meeting by projecting a new schedule.

Every task had an owner. Every meeting had confirmed availability. Keisha's track meets were marked as commitments, not inconveniences. Nia's mathematical analysis, Keisha's software work, and their joint interpretation were separate colors with equal space.

"You gave me veto authority over code-demo changes," Keisha said.

"Because you own the code demo."

"I accept this constitutional reform."

They turned to the reviewer comments about human decisions. Neither had the training to make a documentary, conduct public interviews, and redesign the model alone. Nia knew who could help with one part without becoming absorbed into all of it.

She met Julian at the campus radio studio.

"We need advice on public visualization," she said. "Only that. We are not asking you to speak for residents or make the research look more certain than it is."

Julian leaned back. "What do you want people to understand?"

"Where route recommendations become unstable and what assumptions cause the change."

"Then stop using three nearly identical shades of blue. Also, animation might show the route changing better than five frozen maps."

He sketched a sequence: one road capacity shifted, the recommended path moved, and a caption named the assumption responsible. He suggested testing the visual with viewers who had not spent six weeks staring at the network.

"That is exactly the scope we need," Nia said.

"Good. I have band travel and two films. I cannot become your media department."

"I know."

And this time, she did.

Nia returned to the lab with Julian's notes. Keisha approved the visualization test and added it to the shared plan.

Outside, February rain silvered the campus walkways. A research conference, a Calculus II test, and the opening of spring sports waited in the next week. Their calendar was crowded, their model was unfinished, and no one person owned the whole answer.

For the first time, that made Nia feel more prepared rather than less.

---

# Week Twenty-Eight: The Room Beyond the Classroom

Dr. Delgado placed the Calculus II test face down on every desk.

"Before you turn it over," he said, "understand the first instruction."

Nia had never enjoyed a sentence that began that way.

"For each integral, identify the method you would use and justify the choice before computing. A correct answer with an unsupported method may lose credit. A correct setup with incomplete computation may earn substantial credit."

Someone near the windows whispered, "Substantial is not a number."

"Neither is panic," Dr. Delgado replied. "Yet many of you assign it great value."

He started the clock.

Nia turned over the paper. The first problem used substitution. The second required integration by parts. The third was a rational expression waiting to be decomposed into partial fractions. The fourth asked for a numerical approximation and an error comparison.

Her mind wanted to sprint. The instructions demanded that it choose a direction first.

## Friday, February 6: Commit Before Comfort

Malik wrote his method choices in the margin before touching a single antiderivative.

Nia wrote one, began computing, doubted herself, and nearly erased everything. She stopped with the eraser pressed against the paper.

Dr. Delgado had not asked them to predict whether the method would feel comfortable. He had asked them to justify why its structure fit.

She left the line and continued.

The numerical-integration problem described changing water depth along a roadway. Exact measurements existed only at fixed intervals. Students could use left endpoints, right endpoints, trapezoids, or Simpson's rule, but they had to explain what information the approximation preserved and what it might miss.

Nia drew the road segments. The problem felt uncomfortably close to her research. One clean number could hide rapid changes between measurements. A method that performed well on a smooth curve might misrepresent a sudden dip beneath an underpass.

She chose Simpson's rule, wrote the conditions under which she trusted it, and named the risk.

Across the aisle, Malik caught himself beginning integration by parts with a choice that made the remaining integral worse. He crossed out the setup and restarted. The clock continued without sympathy.

When Dr. Delgado called time, the room released one collective breath.

"I would like to file a complaint against method selection," Nia said in the hallway.

"On what grounds?" Malik asked.

"It forces me to reveal my decisions before I know whether they work."

"That may be the point."

"I withdraw my request for your opinion."

## Saturday, February 7: The Honest Blank Space

The final partial-fractions problem had survived Nia's test.

She had factored the denominator, written the correct decomposition, and solved two of the constants. Then time ended. Half the page remained white.

At breakfast, students compared answers with the energy of people investigating a crime they had all witnessed differently. One group insisted the last coefficient was negative. Another said the entire denominator had been copied wrong.

Nia refused to check.

"You are showing maturity," Keisha said.

"I am showing fear with excellent posture."

Dr. Delgado returned the tests electronically that afternoon. Nia's score was strong. The incomplete problem lost points, but her setup and explanation earned most of the credit.

His comment read: **Correct structure. Incomplete is preferable to unsupported invention.**

Nia stared at the blank portion of her scanned page.

In the fall, she had filled uncertain spaces with words because an empty answer felt like public evidence that she did not belong. This time, she had shown exactly what she knew and where time had stopped her.

Malik received a slightly higher score and a note warning him that his restart had cost too much time.

"We have opposite problems," Nia said.

"You commit before checking. I check until commitment becomes expensive."

"Together, we form one adequately timed student."

They laughed, then separated for the weekend. Nia and Keisha had conference bags to pack. Malik had already promised Saturday to Simone.

## Sunday, February 8: Imperfect Work, Serious Questions

The Southeastern Mathematics Futures Conference occupied three floors of a downtown convention hotel and smelled like coffee, carpet cleaner, and printer ink.

Nia expected polished graduate research presented by people who had never made sign errors in public. Instead, the first poster she visited belonged to a first-year student studying bus arrival predictions with incomplete location data.

The student's model failed during rush hour.

She showed the failure on the poster.

"Why not remove that plot?" an audience member asked.

"Because that is when riders most need the estimate," the student replied. "If I only report the quiet hours, I am answering an easier question."

At another session, two undergraduates modeled food-pantry demand and admitted that their training data covered only one semester. A judge asked whether the model should guide purchasing. They said not yet, then described the validation they would need.

Nobody laughed at the unfinished work. The questions became more serious because the presenters were honest about it.

Nia filled pages with notes: define the decision, show the failure, name who is missing, do not confuse a visual with evidence, explain why uncertainty matters to a real person.

Keisha attended a session on reproducible computing. She returned carrying a sample data dictionary and the expression of someone who had discovered twelve new ways their project could be inadequate.

"Having fun?" Nia asked.

"Extremely. We need versioned datasets."

"That sounded less like fun and more like a threat."

They stayed until the final student poster closed, surrounded by imperfect work taken seriously.

For the first time, Nia could imagine their project in the room without needing it to pretend it was finished.

## Monday, February 9: Mathematics in the Decision Room

The national-laboratory panel began with a storm map projected across a ballroom screen.

A Delta National Laboratory scientist explained how teams combined weather observations, infrastructure data, computation, social science, and emergency-management knowledge. The mathematical model did not command officials to evacuate. It clarified scenarios, uncertainty, and possible consequences so people could make decisions under pressure.

"Communication is not the decoration added after the mathematics," she said. "If a result cannot be interpreted, challenged, or connected to a decision, the work is unfinished."

Nia wrote the sentence in capital letters.

Another panelist described a technically accurate flood forecast that local officials misunderstood because the uncertainty range appeared as a faint shaded band. Residents saw one bold line and treated it as a guaranteed boundary.

Julian's advice about color suddenly felt less cosmetic. Keisha's insistence on data lineage felt less like documentation and more like trust.

During questions, Nia asked how early-career researchers could contribute without claiming expertise they did not have.

"Join a team where your limits are visible," the scientist said. "Learn to ask what decision your work affects and who carries the cost when it is wrong."

Afterward, Nia stood beneath the conference banner while people streamed toward lunch.

She did not merely want to visit rooms like this. She wanted to become capable of contributing inside them.

The fellowship connected to the spring symposium could place an undergraduate with a national research team. Until that moment, Nia had treated it as a bright possibility attached to good work.

Now it had shape.

She wanted it.

## Tuesday, February 10: Both Parts of the Story

Imani's first softball game began with a routine ground ball rolling beneath her glove.

The error put a runner on base. Two batters later, the runner scored.

Imani returned to the dugout furious, but she did not throw her glove or search the stands for sympathy. She reviewed the play with the infield coach, adjusted her starting depth, and took the field again.

In the seventh inning, McCall-Hart trailed by one with two runners aboard. Imani drove a pitch into the right-center gap. Both runners scored. She reached second standing, shouting toward the dugout as her teammates spilled against the rail.

The Herons won.

A student reporter met her near the field afterward.

"How does it feel to erase that early error with the game-winning double?"

Imani wiped dirt from one forearm. "It did not erase it. The error happened, and the run counted. The double happened too. I adjusted and helped us win. Both are part of the game."

Nia, listening behind the fence, felt the answer settle beside her test and research notes.

Public stories loved replacement: one heroic moment canceling one bad moment, one award proving every earlier struggle worthwhile. But an achievement did not travel backward through time. It added to the story without deleting what came before.

Imani joined the group and pointed at Nia's conference badge. "How was the math convention?"

"Conference."

"Did anybody hit a double?"

"A first-year student defended a failed bus model."

Imani nodded solemnly. "So yes."

## Wednesday, February 11: Say How Much You Want It

Nia and Keisha spread conference notes across their room.

They had collected ideas about validation, reproducible code, audience testing, uncertainty, and multidisciplinary teams. Every page suggested more work. The symposium fellowship application now looked less like a reward at the end of a good presentation and more like a narrow doorway surrounded by excellent people.

"I want it," Nia said.

Keisha continued typing. "We established that in December."

"No. I said I wanted the experience. I said presenting would be valuable either way. Those things are true, but they were also protective."

Keisha looked up.

"I want the fellowship badly enough that losing it will hurt," Nia said. "I want to work with people making infrastructure decisions. I want access to the lab, the mentoring, all of it."

The confession made her feel ambitious and childish at once.

Keisha did not tell her awards were unimportant. "Good."

"Good?"

"Now we can build for the actual standard instead of acting like wanting less makes us noble."

She pointed to their project plan. "Wanting the fellowship means we test harder. We document what fails. We do not overclaim because a dramatic result feels competitive. And if we lose, we call it a loss instead of pretending the experience was secretly all we wanted."

Nia exhaled. "You make emotional honesty sound like project management."

"Everything improves with version control."

They created a new milestone for the campus pitch. Honest ambition did not guarantee the outcome. It clarified what the work required.

## Thursday, February 12: A Choice Without a Scoreboard

Malik had missed the conference.

Months earlier, that fact would have become a private competition in his mind. Nia would have gone somewhere important; he would have felt left behind; every enthusiastic detail would have sounded like evidence that she was moving into a future without him.

This time, he had chosen a planned day with Simone.

They visited a museum exhibit on Black business history, ate lunch without checking the time, and worked on separate applications in a quiet cafe. Malik did not spend the day refreshing conference posts to measure what he was missing.

Thursday evening, Nia showed him her notes at North Star.

"This panelist said communication is part of the mathematics," she said, turning pages quickly. "And one presenter kept the failed rush-hour plot because that was the exact situation the project was supposed to serve."

Malik read the notes with real interest.

"You should use this question," he said, pointing to a line about decision consequences.

Nia waited for him to explain why he had not attended, or for herself to imply that serious students would have gone.

Neither did.

"How was your day with Simone?" she asked.

"Good. She found an exhibit on a mutual-aid bank and spent twenty minutes correcting the wall text."

"That sounds like her."

Malik returned the notebook. "I am glad you went."

"I am glad you had your day."

The choices did not need a scoreboard.

Still, when Nia later passed a campus store overflowing with red hearts, she felt the reversed shape of spring. Malik had chosen someone else without failing her. She wanted a future he did not have to witness for it to be real.

Both truths followed her into Valentine's weekend.

---

# Week Twenty-Nine: Valentine's Data

By Friday morning, McCall-Hart had been attacked by paper hearts.

They hung from residence-hall doors, bookstore windows, dining-hall napkin holders, and one deeply confused bronze statue near Cypress Walk. The campus store sold stuffed herons holding signs that read **YOU MAKE MY HEART TAKE FLIGHT**, which Nia considered an institutional failure.

"If anybody gives me that bird," she told Keisha, "I will release it into the wild."

"It is polyester."

"Then it will learn resilience."

Nia joked through breakfast, class, and three different student-organization rose tables. Humor kept every display at a safe distance. It worked until Julian's campus-radio show played a dedication from a student celebrating two years with her girlfriend.

The message was simple, affectionate, and public.

Nia stopped walking beneath the radio speaker outside the student center.

## Friday, February 13: The Dedication

The student on the radio thanked her girlfriend for learning her late-night study order, attending dance showcases even when she was tired, and making ordinary Tuesdays feel chosen.

There was no proposal, marching band, or drone carrying roses. Still, everybody crossing the plaza heard it.

Nia remembered how badly she had once wanted visible romance. With Julian, public celebration had first felt like proof that love was real. Later, the pictures had become a place to hide what they were not saying in private.

Now Malik was dating Simone quietly. He had left overtime to keep a dinner promise. He attended her panel without taking the room. Their relationship did not need Nia to observe it, which somehow made it harder to dismiss.

Julian's voice returned after the dedication and introduced the next song without adding commentary.

Nia continued toward class.

She did not want Julian back. She did not want Malik to perform affection for the campus. What hurt was remembering that she had once stood at the center of someone's public choice while Malik waited nearby, and she had not understood the cost of treating his feelings as a possibility she could revisit later.

At the calculus review, Dr. Delgado returned their test corrections. Every student had to explain one wrong method and the evidence that should have changed the decision.

Nia selected her rushed partial-fractions work.

Recognizing a pattern was not the same as understanding what it required. Valentine's week, apparently, had assigned the same lesson twice.

## Saturday, February 14: Questions for Later

Malik gave Simone a notebook.

It was small, copper-colored, and completely free of glitter. On the first page, he had written:

**Questions we have not had time to answer yet.**

Below it were six prompts.

What place do you want to visit without turning it into a career opportunity?

What does support feel like when you are disappointed?

Which part of your future are you afraid to say aloud because it may change someone else's plan?

Simone read the final question twice.

"This is either thoughtful or an extremely sophisticated homework assignment," she said.

"No due date."

"Extra credit?"

"No points."

"Now I know you are serious."

They spent Valentine's afternoon at a used bookstore and dinner at a small restaurant away from campus crowds. Simone had brought Malik a set of archival sleeves for the storm-damaged family photographs he kept sorting, plus a card that said she admired how he was learning to care without immediately controlling the outcome.

Neither gift was expensive. Both revealed attention.

At dinner, Simone wrote one question of her own in the notebook:

**What opportunity would you want me to take even if it made our relationship harder?**

Malik looked at it, then at her.

"You do not have to answer tonight," she said.

He appreciated the permission.

He also understood that the question had not appeared by accident.

## Sunday, February 15: The Missing Warning

Nia began the campus research pitch with confidence.

She introduced Bellwether's flood-route problem, showed how uncertain road capacities could change a recommended path, and used Julian's animation to reveal each shift. Keisha demonstrated the scenario engine. The audience followed. Dr. Price nodded once from the back row.

Then a visiting reviewer raised her hand.

"Your model recommends a route after an official warning. Why do you assume every driver receives the warning at the same time?"

Nia moved to the next slide. "Our current focus is network capacity rather than message distribution."

"But the route recommendation depends on when people leave."

"We account for changing congestion."

"After equal notification. Do residents without reliable mobile service receive the same alert? Do they trust it? What if a message is only in English? What if one neighborhood hears through family before the official system?"

The questions opened a hole beneath the presentation.

Nia knew the reviewer was right. She also knew everyone in the room could see that she had not prepared for it.

"Those factors are outside the current model," she said, too quickly. "Every model requires scope."

"Certainly," the reviewer replied. "But a boundary should not hide an assumption that changes the decision."

Nia thanked her and called for the next question.

The pitch ended on time.

For Nia, it did not end at all.

## Monday, February 16: The Answer Nia Would Not Give

During the feedback session, Nia defended the scope again.

They could not model everything. Communication data was difficult to obtain. Their proposal concerned route sensitivity. Adding trust, language, device access, and social networks could make the project impossible to finish before the symposium.

Each statement was true.

Together, they sounded like a wall.

The reviewer turned to Keisha. "How does the current software represent the warning?"

Keisha answered without looking at Nia. "It treats notification as simultaneous and universal. That is not evidence-based. It was a simplifying assumption we did not make visible enough."

Nia felt betrayed before she had time to feel ashamed of feeling betrayed.

"Would you remove the project from consideration?" the reviewer asked.

"No," Keisha said. "I would test how delayed or missing information changes departure timing, then state what we still cannot represent."

That was the answer the team needed.

Nia heard only that Keisha had given it in public.

After the session, she packed the laptop too aggressively.

"You could have discussed that with me first," she said.

"The question happened during the pitch."

"You made it sound like we ignored the problem."

"We did ignore the problem."

Keisha's voice stayed level. "I am your coauthor, not your agreement generator."

She left for track practice.

Nia remained beside the empty presentation screen, furious at criticism, furious at Keisha, and most furious because the honest answer had not belonged to her.

## Tuesday, February 17: The Alert That Arrived Late

The baseball opener began beneath a sky that could not make up its mind.

Sunlight crossed the infield while dark clouds gathered beyond left field. Nia sat with DJ and Imani, still carrying the argument with Keisha like a stone in her coat.

During the fourth inning, DJ's phone buzzed with a campus weather alert warning of lightning nearby. Nia's phone remained silent. Imani had received a text from her softball coach five minutes earlier. Students in the upper rows began leaving, but the public-address system said nothing.

"Did you get an alert?" DJ asked.

"No."

The umpire stopped play several minutes later. Only then did the stadium announcement instruct everyone to move indoors.

Rain arrived while people crowded the concourse.

Nia looked around. Some students had left before the delay. Some learned from friends. Others waited until the official announcement. One older visitor asked what the phone message had said because she did not use the campus app.

Same storm. Different information. Different departure times. Different congestion.

DJ tilted his screen toward Nia. "Your research face is happening."

"The warning did not reach us equally."

"My phone is on the football-team system too. That may be why mine came first."

Imani added, "Coaches get alerts through athletics. Students get the campus app. Visitors get whatever somebody tells them."

The reviewer had not asked the team to model everything. She had exposed one assumption that controlled the entire sequence.

Nia texted Keisha: **You were right. I was defensive. I want to explain without asking you to forgive me immediately.**

Keisha replied after practice: **Tomorrow. Bring the alert times.**

## Wednesday, February 18: A Role With Edges

Julian had already heard about the weather-delay confusion.

Campus radio received the official notice after athletics but before the stadium announcement. Several volunteers had reposted the alert on personal accounts, each using different wording.

"We can test message designs," he told Nia and Keisha. "Not emergency behavior. Just whether people understand what an alert asks them to do, how urgent it feels, and what information they think is missing."

Nia looked at Keisha before answering.

Keisha nodded. "If participation is voluntary and we do not collect personal emergency histories."

Julian suggested recruiting campus-media volunteers who could evaluate sample messages and explain how wording, audio, captions, and timing affected understanding.

"I will need a defined role," he said. "I do not want to become the person responsible for every public-facing decision because I know where the microphones are."

They wrote the role together.

Julian would advise on message design, coordinate a small comprehension test after Dr. Price approved it, document revisions, and manage the presentation animation. He would not conduct community interviews, interpret evacuation behavior, or alter the mathematical model without the co-leads.

Nia added authorship and credit terms.

"This is impressively specific," Julian said.

"Keisha has constitutionalized the project."

"Because your monarchy failed," Keisha replied.

Nia accepted the joke because she had earned it.

They sent the written role to Dr. Price. The project had gained another collaborator without pretending collaboration meant unlimited access to his time.

## Thursday, February 19: Criticism Is Not Disloyalty

Nia apologized in the research lab.

She did not begin with intention.

"When you answered the reviewer honestly, I treated it like you had embarrassed me instead of protected the project," she said. "I wanted loyalty to mean agreeing with my defense. That was unfair to you as a coauthor."

Keisha closed the data window but did not soften the moment for her.

"You also made me absorb the criticism you would not accept," she said.

"Yes."

"And if that happens at the symposium, I will answer honestly again."

"You should."

Keisha waited long enough to see whether Nia would add a condition.

Nia did not.

They rebuilt the proposal around information flow. The model would no longer begin with every driver receiving one warning at the same instant. It would test several notification patterns: immediate official alerts, delayed delivery, partial reach, and staggered word-of-mouth spread. They would not claim the patterns represented every neighborhood. They would show how communication failure changed departure timing and route congestion.

The new design made the project harder. It also connected the road network to the people trying to use it.

Keisha restored Nia's access to the presentation controls, an action she described as "conditional democratic reform."

Nia laughed, then became serious. "Thank you for not protecting my confidence at the expense of the work."

"That is not what confidence is for."

On the whiteboard, Nia drew three alert sequences spreading through the same network at different rates.

The next mathematical unit would ask whether an infinite sequence approached a stable limit. Their project now had a more urgent question: after repeated updates, did useful information reach everyone soon enough to matter?

---

# Week Thirty: The First Infinite Question

Dr. Delgado began the sequences unit without writing an infinity symbol.

Instead, he described a warning system that updated its estimated arrival time every minute. The first message predicted water in forty minutes. The next predicted thirty-four. Then thirty-one. Then twenty-nine and a half.

"If the updates continue," he asked, "are they approaching a stable warning time, or are we only hoping they are?"

Students proposed answers immediately.

"Twenty-eight minutes."

"It depends on the pattern."

"The storm does not care about our sequence."

"An excellent observation," Dr. Delgado said. "The mathematics describes the updates. It does not guarantee the world obeys the model."

He wrote the terms on the board and asked whether the sequence was monotonic, whether it was bounded, and what evidence would support a limit.

Nia thought about messages moving through the evacuation network. One alert was a snapshot. Repeated updates could approach clarity, amplify confusion, or never reach some people at all.

## Friday, February 20: Getting Closer Is Not Arriving

The class built sequences from formulas, graphs, and recursive rules.

One sequence increased forever but never passed a fixed bound. Another bounced above and below a value while moving closer to it. A third looked stable for ten terms and then wandered away.

"Ten calm terms do not prove convergence," Dr. Delgado said. "A convincing pattern is not the same as a result."

Malik liked the definitions. Monotonicity described direction. Boundedness described restraint. Together, under the right conditions, they could guarantee convergence.

Nia liked the movement. A sequence carried its own history: each term related to an index, sometimes to the term before it, always to the question of what happened next.

During group work, she modeled the delay between an alert entering the system and reaching a growing share of recipients. Malik noticed that the percentage approached one hundred only because her recurrence assumed every unreached person had the same chance of receiving the next update.

"That is probably false," he said.

"Definitely false. People without service may remain unreachable across every term."

"Then your upper bound may be below one hundred percent."

The idea was painful and useful. A warning system could converge and still converge to an unacceptable limit.

Nia circled the sentence.

Getting closer did not answer the ethical question. They also had to ask what they were getting closer to.

## Saturday, February 21: The Summer Door

Delta National Laboratory's virtual briefing filled a lecture room in the Porter Center.

The opening slide showed storm-surge simulations layered over roads, power systems, hospitals, and population data. Students from mathematics, computer science, engineering, public health, communications, and social science attended.

A Delta scientist described the summer partnership with McCall-Hart. Teams would examine how storm conditions and infrastructure failures changed evacuation options. No single major could own the problem.

"A fast model that ignores human decisions is incomplete," she said. "A perfect message that arrives after the road floods is incomplete. A community study disconnected from physical constraints is incomplete. We need teams capable of disagreeing across disciplines."

Nia looked at Keisha. Keisha was already taking notes.

The scientists emphasized data governance, reproducibility, uncertainty, and public accountability. Students would not receive unrestricted access to real emergency records. They would begin with carefully managed datasets and document every transformation.

Then Dr. Price explained the path forward. Spring symposium work could inform summer placements, but attendance at the briefing was not an invitation. Strong students might still be rejected. Selected teams would need faculty sponsorship and clearly defined roles.

Nia felt ambition rise without mistaking it for a promise.

During questions, Keisha asked about computation limits. Julian asked how public-facing media would be reviewed. Nia asked whether iterative route updates could be tested against changing warning reach.

The scientist said yes, if the team stated what drove each update and who remained outside the model.

The summer door had opened a few inches. Nobody had been invited through it yet.

## Sunday, February 22: A Map That Can Change Its Mind

Their original project produced one recommended evacuation map for each scenario.

Nia no longer trusted the stillness.

Storm forecasts changed. Road capacities changed. Warnings reached people at different times. A route that was sensible at noon could become dangerous at 12:20, and a static map could not show why the recommendation moved.

She proposed an iterative model.

At each time step, the system would update estimated road capacity, warning reach, and departure volume. It would recalculate route costs and record whether recommendations stabilized. Keisha immediately identified the danger of making the software appear more certain simply because it updated more often.

"Frequent wrong answers are not live intelligence," she said.

"Put that on the poster."

They defined update rules, confidence labels, and stopping conditions. If new data stopped changing the route, the model could report temporary stability. If recommendations continued to oscillate, it should display instability rather than selecting the most recent path as truth.

Julian tested a rough animation. Roads shifted from copper to indigo as conditions changed. A side panel showed which assumption caused each update.

"It looks alive," Nia said.

"Do not fall in love with movement," Julian warned. "Animation can make weak evidence look authoritative."

She added the warning to their design notes.

The new project did not offer one perfect map. It showed a map capable of changing its mind and explaining why.

## Monday, February 23: The City in the Notebook

Simone met Malik in the Porter Center courtyard carrying the copper notebook he had given her.

She opened to the question about opportunities that might make their relationship harder.

"I applied for a summer internship in London," she said.

Malik's first thought was distance. His second was cost. His third was the time difference, which he calculated before he could stop himself.

Simone continued. "It is a financial-data program with placements across banking and public policy. I have wanted it since October. I submitted the final application last week."

Last week.

Malik felt the delay, but he also heard the rest: she had wanted it for months and had been afraid the truth would become a relationship negotiation before the application was even real.

"You should have told me," he said.

"Yes. I should have."

She did not blame his planning for her silence. "I waited because I wanted the application to belong to me before it became about us. That was not fair."

"How badly do you want it?"

Simone met his eyes. "Very badly. If I receive it, I am going."

No softening. No promise that love would automatically solve geography.

Malik took a slow breath. "Then I hope you get it."

He meant that.

The future still rearranged itself inside him.

## Tuesday, February 24: Solving Before Asking

By Tuesday morning, Malik had researched international phone plans, compared flight prices, mapped the five-hour time difference, and built a summer schedule around work, classes, and video calls.

He had not asked Simone whether she wanted a long-distance relationship.

The spreadsheet looked excellent.

That was the problem.

DJ found him staring at it in North Star Learning Commons.

"Is this a budget?"

"A preliminary feasibility analysis."

"For romance?"

Malik closed the laptop.

"Did she ask for one?" DJ said.

"No."

"Then you are doing emotional group work alone."

The phrase was ridiculous enough to be useful.

Malik deleted no information, but he moved the file out of sight. That evening, he told Simone he had started planning before asking what she wanted.

"I do not know yet," she said. "I do not even have the offer. I know I care about you. I also know I do not want my interview to become a referendum on whether we survive the summer."

"It will not."

"And if I get it, we need to decide together whether long distance makes sense. Not assume staying together is the only respectful answer."

Malik wanted to promise they could make it work. Instead, he asked what interview help she wanted.

"Practice questions," Simone said. "No optimization model."

"That boundary feels targeted."

"It is supported by data."

## Wednesday, February 25: Nobody Attends Everything

McCall-Hart's baseball and softball schedules collided with a track meet, a Calculus II problem set, and DJ's offseason film review.

The friend group attempted to support everybody and nearly created a transportation crisis.

Imani wanted someone at the softball doubleheader. Keisha raced across town. Baseball hosted a conference opponent at the same time. DJ had promised to help Malik review sequences before reporting to football conditioning.

"We cannot divide one person into partial fractions," Nia said.

"That is not what partial fractions means," Malik replied.

"It expresses the emotional truth."

They built a coverage plan without pretending attendance measured friendship. Nia and Julian went to Keisha's early heat, then Julian left for baseball media work. Simone attended Imani's first softball game. DJ brought study notes to the second game and reviewed between innings. Malik stayed in the learning commons, where he hosted a shared call for anyone who needed sequence help.

Imani sent score updates. Keisha sent a picture of her relay team. Julian uploaded one baseball clip. Nobody demanded immediate replies.

The day was less cinematic than all of them arriving together beneath one banner. It was also more sustainable.

That evening, Keisha joined Malik's study call from the team van. DJ logged in from a quiet fieldhouse corridor. Imani watched the recording after treatment.

Support did not always mean witnessing the event. Sometimes it meant making sure the person could return to the rest of life afterward.

## Thursday, February 26: Ambition Has a Direction

Nia found Malik and Simone in a Porter Center practice room.

Simone stood at the front answering an interview question about model risk. Malik sat at the table with a list of follow-ups. He did not correct her mid-answer. When she finished, he asked where she could make her role in a team project more specific.

"I designed the monitoring thresholds," Simone said. "The answer makes it sound like I only interpreted results."

"Then claim the decision."

Nia paused outside the partly open door.

The scene resembled evenings when Malik had helped her prepare for calculus or listened to her research ideas. But the care moving through this room belonged to the relationship he had chosen. More importantly, it served Simone's future even though that future might carry her across an ocean.

Simone saw Nia and waved her in.

"We need a hostile interviewer," she said.

"I can be constructively alarming."

Nia asked why the London program should select Simone over candidates with more experience.

Simone answered with clarity, then improved the answer when Nia pressed her.

For half an hour, the three of them worked without turning the room into a romantic contest. When the session ended, Malik gave Simone the marked question list to keep.

Walking back to North Star, Nia understood something she had only partly learned from Julian.

Loving someone's ambition did not mean cheering because their success kept them close. It meant wanting the work to open in its true direction, even when that direction moved away from you.

The thought hurt.

It also made Malik's support look less like loss and more like love practiced honestly.

---

# Week Thirty-One: What Converges

Nia drew a warning network across the whiteboard.

The first alert reached half the households. If each reached household informed one unreached neighbor, the next round reached half of whoever remained. Then half of the new remainder. Then half again.

"Geometric series," Malik said.

"Exactly. One-half plus one-fourth plus one-eighth, continuing toward one."

On paper, the warning reached almost everyone as the number of rounds increased. The total approached one hundred percent with satisfying obedience.

Keisha turned her laptop around.

"The real access survey does not behave like your board."

Households with stable internet access received alerts at much higher rates. Households relying on prepaid mobile service, shared devices, or intermittent connections did not simply join the next round at the same probability. Some remained outside every update.

Nia erased the final arrow pointing toward one hundred percent.

"So the series converges," she said, "but to the wrong promise."

## Friday, February 27: The Missing Remainder

Dr. Delgado introduced geometric series by dropping a rubber ball.

Each bounce reached a fixed fraction of the previous height. Students calculated the total vertical distance the ball traveled if the pattern continued forever. The answer was finite, though the motion was described with infinitely many pieces.

"Infinite activity does not require an infinite total," he said. "But you must justify the ratio and its range."

Nia adapted the structure to repeated warning reach. The first model assumed every unreached household had the same chance of receiving the next message. It was elegant, computable, and unsupported.

Keisha separated the population into access groups. Julian's message test suggested that receiving an alert did not guarantee understanding it. A warning filled with unfamiliar abbreviations could arrive technically and fail practically.

"We need at least three states," Keisha said. "Not reached, reached but unclear, reached and understood."

"And trust," Nia added. "Somebody can understand a message and still not act on it."

Their clean series became several interacting sequences with uncertain transition rates.

Alexis reviewed the revision and asked where the rates came from.

"Campus volunteers and published communication studies," Nia said.

"Then do not call them community behavior. Campus volunteers are not a substitute for Bellwether neighborhoods."

Nia added a limitation in bold.

The model could demonstrate sensitivity. It could not claim to predict every household. The remainder was not merely a mathematical quantity. It represented people the system might repeatedly fail to reach.

## Saturday, February 28: What People Actually Trust

Dr. Price approved a small set of recorded community interviews under strict limits.

Julian explained the project and consent form before turning on any equipment. Participants chose whether their names, voices, or only anonymous notes could be used. They could stop at any time. The team asked about how people received severe-weather information, what made a warning credible, and what details they needed before changing plans.

The answers disrupted nearly every convenient assumption.

One resident trusted local radio more than phone alerts because storms often interrupted service. Another waited for a sister who worked at the hospital to confirm official messages. A parent said an instruction to "evacuate immediately" was useless without information about transportation and where children could be collected. An older man kept a weather radio but distrusted maps that did not show street names he recognized.

"If City Hall tells you to leave, would you?" Nia asked one participant.

"Depends who at City Hall is talking and whether my neighbor got the same message," she replied.

Trust was not a switch. It had history.

Julian recorded only what each person allowed. Keisha kept consent status separate from research notes. Nia resisted translating every answer into a parameter before the speaker finished.

Afterward, Julian packed the microphone.

"Your model keeps asking when information arrives," he said. "They keep answering who it arrives from."

Nia looked at the interview cards. "We treated the source like a label."

"To them, the source is part of the message."

The project had gathered richer evidence and become less certain. That was not a contradiction. It was what listening had done.

## Sunday, March 1: Winning With the Unknowns Visible

The campus semifinal took place in a lecture hall with a timer large enough to threaten the entire front row.

Nia opened with the original problem: uncertain road capacities changed route recommendations. Keisha demonstrated the iterative model. Julian's animation showed alerts arriving at different times and routes shifting as congestion grew.

Then Nia displayed a slide titled **WHAT WE DO NOT YET KNOW**.

They did not know reliable trust rates for Bellwether neighborhoods. Their interviews were too small to estimate community behavior. They could not represent every transportation constraint. They had not validated the update model against a real emergency.

One judge asked whether those gaps made the project premature.

"They make any broad prediction premature," Nia said. "But the sensitivity analysis is useful now because it shows which assumptions can reverse a recommendation. We are not presenting one evacuation answer. We are identifying where an answer becomes unstable and what evidence would be needed to improve it."

Keisha explained how the software preserved scenario histories instead of overwriting inconvenient runs. Julian described message testing without claiming to represent all residents.

The judges advanced them to the regional presentation stage.

For several seconds, Nia forgot every lesson about dignified academic conduct and bounced beside Keisha in the hallway.

"We won by listing everything wrong with us," she said.

"We advanced because we knew what the model could support."

"That is the mature wording. Mine has more rhythm."

The celebration was real. So were the unknowns. Neither canceled the other.

## Monday, March 2: One Final, Two Endings

The rivalry basketball doubleheader filled Ruth Ellis Fieldhouse in indigo and copper.

The women's game came first. Imani led chants from the student section while Keisha, Nia, Julian, Malik, Simone, and DJ crowded one row. McCall-Hart's women defended relentlessly, forcing a late turnover and scoring in transition to secure the win.

The fieldhouse shook.

Thirty minutes later, the men's rivalry finale began with the same crowd expecting the same ending.

It did not arrive.

McCall-Hart trailed by one with six seconds left. Their guard drove, kicked the ball to the corner, and a senior took an open shot at the buzzer.

The ball struck the rim and bounced away.

For one confused moment, cheers from the visiting section collided with the silence around them. The senior stood beneath the basket staring at the floor. His teammates reached him before the cameras did.

DJ exhaled. "Same building. Completely different walk home."

The women's team crossed the court carrying their rivalry trophy. The men's team disappeared into the tunnel.

Campus social media immediately combined the results into one graphic: **HERON BASKETBALL SPLITS RIVALRY FINALE.**

Nia disliked the word *splits*. It made triumph and disappointment sound like equal halves of one tidy object.

The people living each result did not experience a split. They experienced a win and a loss, each complete.

## Tuesday, March 3: Let Both Results Be Full

The next day's celebration honored the women's team in the student center.

Some students complained that the event should be quieter because the men's team had lost. Others spoke about the men's missed shot as if the women's trophy repaired it.

Imani rejected both ideas.

"The women do not have to shrink their win to protect anybody," she said. "And their win does not mean the men should stop hurting."

She helped hang a banner for the women's team, then delivered handwritten notes to two men's seniors whose final home game had ended at the rim.

Nia thought about convergent series. Mathematics could combine infinitely many terms into one total. Human experience resisted that kind of compression. A shared crowd could hold two results without averaging them into emotional neutrality.

At lunch, Simone congratulated Nia on the regional advancement.

"You should be proud," she said.

"I am. I am also aware that our trust model is currently held together by caveats."

"Those can both be true."

Nia noticed how naturally Simone made room for more than one truth. She understood why Malik valued her.

The realization no longer felt like a challenge to win. It felt like evidence Nia had to respect.

## Wednesday, March 4: The Interview Becomes Real

Simone received the London interview invitation between classes.

She read the email once, walked directly to Malik, and handed him her phone.

"Next Wednesday," she said. "Video interview. Two case questions and a behavioral panel."

Malik hugged her before speaking. "Congratulations."

She held him tightly, then stepped back. Excitement made her eyes brighter. Fear sharpened everything else.

"I need to tell you something before we start planning," she said. "I cannot promise what I will decide about us this summer before I even have an offer."

Malik felt the old spreadsheet opening inside his mind.

"I am not asking for a promise tonight."

"Not tonight. But you keep talking as if distance is a problem with a solution, and I do not know whether I want our first months together to become a maintenance project across time zones."

"So you are already deciding against it?"

"No. I am refusing to decide from fear before I know what the actual choice is."

Malik wanted certainty because uncertainty felt passive. Simone wanted room because premature certainty felt like surrendering ownership of her future.

They scheduled interview practice for the weekend.

The calendar entry was easy.

Everything beneath it was not.

## Thursday, March 5: Pleasant Semester or Shared Plan

Malik asked the question during a walk along Cypress Walk.

"Are we planning a relationship," he said, "or preserving a pleasant semester until your internship begins?"

Simone stopped beneath the bare branches.

"That is not a neutral question."

"I know."

"It makes every good thing we have done sound temporary unless I promise the future you want."

"I do not know what future you want."

"Neither do I. That is what I keep telling you."

Malik looked away. "I do not want to invest in something you have already decided to leave."

"And I do not want affection to become evidence that I owe you continuation."

They stood close enough to touch and did not.

Simone opened the copper notebook to the unanswered question about opportunities. "We have evidence that we care about each other. We have evidence that we work through disagreement. We do not have evidence about distance, London, or what either of us will need by summer."

"So we wait?"

"We keep being honest. We stop pretending uncertainty means one of us is secretly disloyal. And we gather more evidence."

Malik almost laughed. "You turned our relationship into a research design."

"You started it."

They continued walking, hands linked but not relaxed.

Some series converged. Some diverged. At that moment, Malik and Simone had only terms, not a theorem.

---

# Week Thirty-Two: Tests of Convergence

Dr. Delgado wrote four infinite series on the board and forbade anyone from calculating.

"Choose a convergence test," he said. "Defend the choice. Then you may touch the algebra."

Students stared at the series as if they had been asked to identify four strangers from childhood photographs.

One resembled a geometric series but contained an extra factor. One had factorials that suggested the ratio test. One alternated signs. One looked suitable for comparison with a familiar benchmark.

"Can multiple tests work?" Nia asked.

"Yes."

"Then how do we know which one you want?"

"I want the one you can justify. Mathematics is not mind reading."

Malik examined the factorial series. "The ratio test exposes the structure directly."

"Good. Do not use three weaker arguments because you are afraid to commit to one strong one."

The advice landed beyond the board.

## Friday, March 6: Name the Test

Nia chose comparison for a positive-term series.

She bounded it above by a convergent benchmark, then paused. An upper bound by something convergent could establish convergence, but only if the inequalities pointed the correct way. She checked each term rather than trusting the shape.

Across the room, Malik applied the ratio test. The limit fell below one. For once, the conclusion arrived cleanly.

Amara selected the alternating-series test and established conditional convergence. Dr. Delgado asked whether absolute convergence had also been shown.

"No," Amara said. "That would require another test."

"Exactly. A result may be true under one standard and unresolved under another."

After class, Nia and Keisha prepared for the symposium decision. Their project had passed the campus semifinal, but regional reviewers could still reject it. Nia refreshed the portal twice.

"Choose a test," Keisha said.

"For what?"

"Whether the email has arrived. Test one: look at the inbox. Test two through seventeen are still looking at the inbox."

Nia placed her phone face down.

The project would either advance or it would not. Repeated observation could not influence the result.

At 4:38 p.m., the portal changed.

Neither saw it until Saturday morning.

## Saturday, March 7: Two Invitations in One Email

Keisha opened the acceptance while standing in line for oatmeal.

She made a sound so sharp that three people checked the fire alarm.

Nia ran across the dining hall. "What happened?"

Keisha held up the phone.

Their project had been accepted to the Southeastern Undergraduate Mathematics Symposium.

Below that sentence was another.

The team had also been selected for a final-round interview for the national summer fellowship.

Nia read the paragraph until the words stopped moving. Then she grabbed Keisha's hands and jumped. Keisha joined for exactly two jumps before remembering she was carrying oatmeal.

They called Julian, Dr. Price, Alexis, Nia's mother, and Keisha's track teammate who answered half asleep and screamed anyway. Nia typed a message to Malik, erased an overly emotional version, and sent the simple news.

He replied immediately: **You earned the room. Congratulations to all three of you.**

All three.

Nia noticed the wording.

The interview instructions emphasized collaboration, public relevance, and evidence of revision. Fellowship judges would examine not only the final model but how the team responded when assumptions failed.

"We have abundant failure evidence," Julian said when they met.

"Finally, a competitive advantage," Keisha replied.

Nia laughed, but fear moved beneath the joy. She had admitted how badly she wanted this. Now the desired outcome was close enough to imagine losing in detail.

## Sunday, March 8: No Single Hero in the Frame

Julian rebuilt the route animation.

The first version opened on Nia speaking beside a glowing map. Her narration drove every transition. Keisha's code appeared as a small technical label. Community interview clips functioned like supporting evidence in Nia's explanation.

Julian watched the sequence once and deleted the opening.

"What are you doing?" Nia asked.

"Fixing the point of view."

The revised version began with three contradictory warning experiences, used with permission. Keisha then showed how those differences became scenario inputs. Nia explained how route recommendations changed. Julian's visual sequence connected each update to its source and uncertainty.

No one person appeared as the project.

"You removed my strongest introduction," Nia said.

"Your introduction made everybody else's work arrive as proof that you were insightful."

Nia winced. "Accurate and rude."

"Accurate and billable."

Keisha reviewed the animation and requested clearer labels for the software's decision states. Nia shortened her narration. Julian added credits by role rather than placing three names beneath the vague phrase *project team*.

The result was easier to understand because the audience could see where each claim originated.

Nia still served as lead presenter. Leadership no longer required the camera to treat everyone else as scenery.

## Monday, March 9: The Pep-Bus Deadline

McCall-Hart's women's basketball team reached the conference final.

The student pep group organized an overnight bus. Keisha wanted to go. She also owed the research team a final code review before the fellowship-interview materials were locked.

Old Nia would have suggested Keisha skip the trip, then praised her sacrifice as commitment.

Current Nia asked, "What do you need from us to finish before departure?"

They divided the review. Nia checked whether every visual matched the correct scenario output. Julian verified that consent restrictions followed each interview clip. Keisha ran automated tests, examined the route-history logs, and froze the presentation version at 3:12 p.m.

At 3:20, she left for the pep bus wearing an indigo sweatshirt and carrying a statistics worksheet.

"You are taking homework to a championship game?" Julian asked.

"The bus has seats."

"This university does not understand leisure."

Keisha completed the worksheet before the bus crossed the county line. That evening, she sent one message to the team: **Code review clean. I am now unavailable unless the model catches fire. If it catches fire, document it.**

Nia did not contact her.

Shared ownership meant trusting the work after the owner stepped away.

## Tuesday, March 10: A Future After the Buzzer

The conference final stayed close until the final minute.

Keisha's voice disappeared somewhere in the third quarter. She texted score updates to the group, each one containing fewer vowels as tension increased.

McCall-Hart tied the game with twenty seconds left. Their opponent answered with two free throws. The Herons' final shot rolled around the rim and out.

This time, the loss did not end the season.

During the trophy ceremony, the conference announced McCall-Hart had earned a postseason invitation. The players were devastated by the final and proud of the opportunity, sometimes within the same expression.

On the bus home, Keisha joined the research team's call.

"Do not ask me to describe the last shot," she said.

"We will not," Nia replied. "How is the team?"

"Sad. Also already discussing film tomorrow."

The future did not erase the immediate result. It gave the result somewhere to go.

Nia looked at the symposium acceptance taped above her desk. If the fellowship interview went badly, other work could still follow. That did not mean she needed to reduce her hope in advance.

She could prepare for the next round and still admit the current one mattered.

## Wednesday, March 11: The Opportunity Gets a Voice

Simone's London interview lasted forty-seven minutes.

Malik waited in the Porter Center lobby with two coffees growing cold. He had helped her practice case questions, but she entered the interview alone.

When she emerged, her face revealed nothing for three steps. Then she smiled.

"It went well."

Malik stood and hugged her. "Tell me everything."

She described the panel, the market-risk case, the question about adapting to a new city, and the program director's detailed explanation of summer placements. The opportunity had offices, mentors, transit routes, and a welcome week. London was no longer a word in an application. It had acquired a voice.

Malik congratulated her sincerely.

Then he said, "Part of me hoped the interview would make it less real."

Simone went still.

"Not that you would fail," he added. "I hoped you would dislike the program, or they would describe something you did not want. I wanted the problem to disappear without either of us choosing."

"Thank you for telling me," she said. "I do not like hearing it."

"I know."

She accepted one of the cold coffees. They sat in the lobby while students moved around them.

His honesty did not repair the conflict. It prevented false enthusiasm from becoming another one.

## Thursday, March 12: Not Yet Is Still an Answer

Malik and Simone met again before spring break.

They could break up now, protecting themselves from an offer that might never come. They could promise long distance now, protecting themselves from admitting they might choose differently later. Both decisions would convert uncertainty into action.

Neither felt honest.

"I do not want to end something meaningful because I am afraid of a possible email," Simone said.

"I do not want staying together this month to become an automatic promise about summer."

"Then it does not."

They agreed to continue the relationship without pretending it was uncomplicated. Simone would not minimize London to reassure him. Malik would not make every difficult feeling a forecast of their ending. If an offer arrived, they would decide from the life in front of them, not a hypothetical version.

Malik took her hand. "I still want this."

"So do I."

The words were true and not sufficient to solve everything.

Across campus, Nia, Keisha, and Julian finalized plans to remain for the first two days of spring break. The symposium presentation needed revisions, interviews needed editing, and the route animation still moved too quickly for viewers to read its uncertainty labels.

Malik saw their shared calendar notification.

He told himself it was project work.

That was true too.

Spring break opened with several honest answers and no final conclusions.

---

# Week Thirty-Three: Spring Break, Old Angles

By Friday afternoon, McCall-Hart sounded abandoned.

Residence-hall doors slammed. Suitcases rolled over brick paths. Cars filled with students, laundry, snacks, and the confidence that every item would fit if somebody pushed hard enough.

Nia stayed.

So did Keisha and Julian. They had two days before Nia left for Huntsville, Keisha joined family outside Bellwether, and Julian traveled with the Marching Herons' postseason group.

The Porter Center lab felt enormous with most of campus gone. Their footsteps echoed. A vending machine offered six varieties of chips and rejected Julian's dollar twice.

"Spring-break luxury," he said.

"We have unrestricted access to the good whiteboard," Nia replied.

"Your standards are concerning."

They opened the presentation files.

## Friday, March 13: Two Days to Rebuild

The symposium version had to do three things at once: explain the mathematics, preserve uncertainty, and remain understandable to someone who had not lived inside the project since January.

Nia's narration ran four minutes too long. Keisha's live demonstration required six setup steps the audience would never see. Julian's animation moved beautifully and labeled nothing long enough to read.

They dismantled all three.

Nia cut historical background she loved but did not need. Keisha recorded a reproducible demonstration with a live fallback. Julian slowed the route transitions and added a visible source for every change.

At lunch, they ate vending-machine crackers and one questionable microwave pizza.

"This is what fellowship candidates eat?" Julian asked.

"The funding begins after selection," Nia said.

Keisha pointed at the pizza. "This may affect our survival to selection."

The work was tiring and unexpectedly fun. Without the pressure of ordinary classes, they argued through details, played music too loudly, and celebrated every removed slide as if subtraction were a sport.

Nia and Julian fell into an easy creative rhythm. He asked what the audience should feel. She translated the mathematical stakes. Keisha interrupted whenever either of them attempted to make uncertainty visually inconvenient.

Their old romance existed in the room as history, not instruction.

## Saturday, March 14: Elegant Models, Messy People

Julian recorded Nia's interview against a plain cream wall.

He asked her to describe the moment the project changed.

"Which one?" she said. "We have changed it every four business days."

"The deepest one."

Nia thought about the community interviews, the delayed baseball alert, and Keisha answering the reviewer more honestly than she had.

"I began with an elegant model of roads," she said. "Roads were easier than people. They had capacities and distances. Then people kept making choices the map did not contain. They waited for family. They trusted different sources. They understood the same message differently."

"So was the elegant model wrong?"

"Not useless. Incomplete. The danger was treating elegance like proof that we had included what mattered."

Julian remained behind the camera. "Does that happen outside mathematics?"

Nia looked directly at the lens.

They both understood the question.

"Yes," she said. "You can have a beautiful picture of a relationship. You can have a story about who two people are together. If the picture becomes more important than how they actually feel, the story stops helping."

Julian lowered the camera after the answer.

"That was about us," he said.

"Partly."

"Fair."

There was no accusation in his voice and no invitation in hers. They had made mistakes without making their entire relationship one.

Keisha returned with lunch and found them laughing because Julian had recorded the first half of the interview with a strip of tape visible on his sleeve.

She took a still image for the production log.

## Sunday, March 15: The Cropped Story

The still image reached Malik through a student-media post.

Nia and Julian sat close beneath the interview lights, laughing over the camera screen. The caption read: **Spring break does not stop symposium season.** Keisha was not visible. Neither were the consent sheets, route maps, or twelve hours of work surrounding the moment.

Malik looked at the image too long.

He knew the facts. Nia and Julian were collaborators. Keisha had been present. The project needed media work. Nia had directly said the relationship was over.

His jealousy arrived anyway.

It offered an old story: Nia and Julian had discovered that safe collaboration was actually reunion. Malik had moved forward only to learn that the possibility he abandoned had become available after he stopped looking.

He did not message Nia.

He did not ask Julian.

He also did not tell Simone what he was feeling.

Instead, he closed the post and carried the silence into their interview-practice call.

Simone noticed within three minutes.

"You are reading questions from a list," she said, "but you are not listening to the answers."

"I am tired."

That was true.

It was not the truth he owed her.

## Monday, March 16: The Cost of Saying Nothing

Spring break officially began Monday, and campus offices shifted to limited hours.

Malik joined Simone's practice session from Gulfport, where he was helping his parents review repair estimates. Simone called from Bellwether before leaving to visit family.

She answered a case question about detecting bias in a credit-risk model. Malik responded, "Good," and moved to the next question.

"What was good?" she asked.

"The structure."

"Which part?"

He could not say because he had barely heard it.

The image of Nia and Julian kept returning, followed by irritation at himself, followed by more distance. Simone had done nothing. Still, she was receiving the shortened answers, flat voice, and missing attention.

"We should stop," she said.

"We have six questions left."

"I am not an item on your schedule. Something is wrong, and you are making me work around it without telling me what it is."

Malik almost said family stress. His father's recovery and the repair estimates were real pressures. Using them to hide another feeling would make truth into cover.

"I saw a picture of Nia and Julian working together," he said. "I am jealous."

Simone went silent.

The confession came late enough that she had already paid for it.

"We will talk tomorrow," she said. "Not while you are still deciding whether to be honest."

She ended the call.

## Tuesday, March 17: The Direct Question

Simone called at noon.

"Are you jealous of Julian because you think Nia wants him," she asked, "or because you still want Nia?"

Malik had spent the morning constructing distinctions. Old feelings. Unresolved possibility. Fear of replacement. None relieved him of the answer.

"Both may be true," he said. "I do not know what Nia wants. I know I still have feelings I did not resolve."

"And yesterday you punished me for them."

"Yes."

He did not say he had not intended to. Intention would not return Simone's wasted practice session or remove the experience of being treated as the obstacle between him and another possibility.

"I chose this relationship," Malik said. "But I have been acting as if choosing should have erased everything else. It did not."

Simone looked hurt rather than surprised.

"I have felt you comparing," she said. "Not every day. Not in every room. But enough."

Malik wanted to deny it. He remembered the basketball game, his jealousy over Julian, and the private relief he sought whenever Nia seemed emotionally available.

"You are right."

"I do not need another apology that becomes a promise to control your feelings better. I need you to understand what relationship you are actually in."

"I will."

"That is still a future answer."

The call ended without resolution. Honesty had stopped the hiding. It had not made the truth harmless.

## Wednesday, March 18: Safe Because It Ended

Nia went home to Huntsville and found her mother reorganizing a kitchen cabinet that had survived perfectly well for years.

Denise Reynolds asked about the symposium, Keisha's track season, Malik's family, and Julian, in that order.

"Julian and I worked together all weekend," Nia said.

Her mother placed a stack of bowls on the counter. "How did that feel?"

"Safe."

Denise waited.

"Not safe like I want him back," Nia added. "Safe because I do not. We can remember what was good without testing whether it should restart."

"Does he know that?"

"Yes. I think so. We interviewed each other about elegant models and accidentally discussed our breakup on camera."

"That sounds like a terrible documentary."

Nia laughed. "The research part is better."

She explained that Julian had changed the animation so she was not the sole hero, that Keisha controlled the code decisions, and that the project had become stronger as Nia owned less of it alone.

"And Malik?" Denise asked.

Nia's smile faded. "He is with Simone. I care about him. I am respecting that."

"Respect is not only what you avoid doing," her mother said. "It is also refusing to build private hope out of somebody else's difficulty."

Nia thought of London.

"I know."

This time, she meant that she was practicing, not that she had mastered it.

## Thursday, March 19: Return With What Changed

Students began returning to McCall-Hart Thursday evening.

Nia carried a revised script. Keisha returned with a clean software build. Julian brought edited interview clips and a signed record of every participant's permissions.

The project was stronger because each person had protected a different truth.

Malik returned from Gulfport carrying repair documents and the knowledge that Simone no longer trusted his choice as completely as before. Simone returned with a stronger interview performance and a clearer understanding that London was not the only pressure on their relationship.

They met outside the Porter Center.

"How was home?" Malik asked.

"Good," Simone said. "How is your father?"

"Improving. The contractor thinks the back rooms may be finished by summer."

They exchanged facts because facts were safer than the conversation still waiting.

Across the courtyard, Nia and Julian carried equipment toward the lab. Malik saw them and made himself remain present.

He did not confront Nia. She had done nothing requiring explanation.

Nia noticed the strain between Malik and Simone but did not ask for private details. Their relationship was not an opening in her calendar.

Dr. Price sent the team its next itinerary: a Delta National Laboratory preview visit after break, with research sessions, communication workshops, and a joint review of symposium projects.

The trip would place Nia, Keisha, Julian, Malik, and Simone inside the same professional space.

They returned with a stronger project, a strained relationship, and old feelings stripped of their easiest excuses.

No confrontation came that night.

The silence was no longer empty. It was carrying everything the next trip would force them to name.

---

# Week Thirty-Four: Radius of Influence

Nia replaced one expensive calculation with a power-series approximation and watched the model run faster.

The improvement was dramatic. A route update that had taken several seconds now appeared almost instantly.

"We fixed it," she said.

Keisha did not celebrate. "Where does the approximation converge?"

Nia looked at the expression again.

They had expanded the function around a typical congestion value. Near that center, the first few terms approximated it well. Farther away, the error grew. At extreme congestion levels, the shortcut could produce a route cost less reliable than the slow calculation it replaced.

"We accelerated the model inside one neighborhood of values," Nia said.

"Then label the neighborhood."

On the board, Nia wrote the center, radius of convergence, and error bound. Speed was useful only where the approximation deserved trust.

## Friday, March 20: Valid Here, Not Everywhere

Spring break ended with students returning to Dr. Delgado's classroom in various stages of denial.

He introduced power series as functions represented by infinite polynomial expressions. Around a chosen center, a complicated function could become easier to calculate term by term. But the representation did not necessarily work for every input.

"A formula can be excellent inside its interval and disastrous outside it," he said. "State the domain of your confidence."

Nia tested her approximation across possible congestion values. The first few terms matched the full function closely near ordinary conditions. As traffic approached the extreme scenarios, the difference widened.

She added a safeguard: outside the validated interval, the software would revert to the slower calculation rather than continue producing quick uncertainty disguised as precision.

Malik reviewed the logic before the Delta trip.

"You could also display the expected approximation error," he said.

"Keisha already demanded it."

"Naturally."

They spoke easily about the model, though both knew the last weeks had made ease complicated. Nia did not ask about Simone. Malik did not mention the spring-break image.

Professional limits were still limits.

That evening, the five students packed for Delta National Laboratory: Nia and Keisha for the symposium project, Julian for communication design, Simone for data-governance sessions, and Malik for risk analysis.

They were traveling together because their skills intersected.

Nothing about the trip promised that their feelings would converge as neatly.

## Saturday, March 21: Behind the Glass

Delta National Laboratory's supercomputing floor lived behind thick glass and a security door that opened only after orientation, identification checks, and a lecture about devices nobody was allowed to connect.

Rows of dark cabinets stretched beneath white lights. Cooling systems filled the room with a low mechanical roar. A scientist explained that the computers could run thousands of storm scenarios, but larger capacity did not remove uncertainty. It allowed teams to examine more versions of what might happen.

"Computation multiplies assumptions," she said. "It does not purify them."

Keisha wrote that down immediately.

During an uncertainty-communication talk, researchers compared three maps built from the same forecast. One used a single sharp flood boundary. One displayed several possible boundaries without explanation. The third connected probability ranges to specific planning decisions.

Most viewers preferred the first because it looked decisive.

Emergency planners found the third more useful.

"The clearest image is not always the most truthful," Julian whispered.

"Please frame that above your editing station," Nia said.

The speaker demonstrated how color, animation speed, and label placement could make low-confidence outcomes look inevitable. Julian asked about testing visuals with residents rather than technical teams. Simone asked who approved communication standards when agencies disagreed.

Nia watched each person enter the discussion from a different direction.

The project belonged in this kind of room, not because it was finished, but because its weaknesses required more than one discipline to see.

## Sunday, March 22: Pricing the Rare Event

Malik attended a session on extreme-event risk.

An actuarial researcher described the difficulty of estimating events that were both rare and enormously costly. Historical records were limited. Climate conditions changed. Property data favored documented financial loss over losses that insurance never recorded.

Malik raised his hand.

"How do agencies price a rare risk without presenting the estimate as more certain than it is?"

The researcher distinguished between expected loss, tail risk, scenario ranges, and decisions made under uncertainty. A premium or reserve was not proof that analysts knew the future. It was a policy choice informed by models, available evidence, and tolerance for failure.

"The ethical problem begins," she said, "when the number's precision is allowed to hide the judgment inside it."

Malik thought of his family's insurance claim after the Gulf storm. Every damaged item had required documentation. Emotional loss, unpaid recovery time, and rooms his parents could not safely use had entered the calculation unevenly, if at all.

After the session, Nia asked what he had learned.

He explained the difference between a rare-event estimate and the decision built from it. She connected it to route instability near uncertain roads.
