Simone joined them and expanded the question to financial institutions deciding which communities received resilience investment.

For several minutes, the conversation was balanced among three people.

Then a scientist approached with a question that narrowed the room.

## Monday, March 23: The Old Ease

The scientist had reviewed Nia's poster abstract.

"If your warning updates and route calculations change at the same time," she asked, "how do you distinguish instability caused by communication from instability caused by road capacity?"

Nia began with the scenario matrix. Malik entered at the exact point where risk decomposition mattered.

"Hold one uncertainty source fixed," he said, "then vary the other across its plausible range. After separate sensitivity runs, test interactions."

"But holding communication fixed is not realistic during an actual event," Nia added.

"It is not a forecast. It isolates influence. The combined scenario comes after."

"And the display must show which run the audience is seeing."

"Otherwise interaction looks like one unexplained risk score."

They passed the answer back and forth without interrupting. Nia drew the scenario grid. Malik named the assumptions. Each anticipated the other's missing step.

The scientist nodded. "That design could work. Document the artificial separation so no one mistakes it for reality."

Only after she left did Nia realize Simone had been watching.

The exchange had felt like Summer Bridge: two students discovering that the other person's mind moved differently but arrived at the same problem. The ease was real. So was the history attached to it.

Malik returned to Simone and asked about her afternoon session.

He was present.

The fact that he could be present afterward did not erase where part of him had just gone.

## Tuesday, March 24: The Part Not Available

Simone did not blame Nia.

She waited until she and Malik were walking from the guest residence to breakfast.

"Yesterday, when you answered that scientist with Nia, you looked relieved," she said.

"Relieved?"

"Like you had returned to a conversation that never required translation."

Malik searched for a less dangerous description. "We know how each other thinks about these problems."

"I know. I watched it."

Simone stopped near a courtyard bench. "Has part of you remained unavailable all spring?"

He could have answered with actions. He had chosen Simone. He kept dates, supported London, attended her panel, and told the truth when it was difficult. All of that mattered.

It did not fully answer her.

"I thought choosing you meant the old feelings would become irrelevant," he said.

"That is not a no."

"No."

Simone absorbed the word without turning toward Nia's building or asking what Nia had done. "Then London is not the only uncertainty between us."

"No."

"I need you to stop acting as if geography created a problem that was already here."

Malik nodded.

The walk to breakfast continued. Neither reached for the other's hand.

## Wednesday, March 25: Strong Splits and One-Run Games

Keisha opened outdoor track season with her strongest relay split of the year.

She received the baton in third, closed the gap on the backstretch, and handed off nearly even. Her team finished second, but the coaches replayed her split on a tablet and showed how her curve mechanics had improved.

"No medal for one leg," Keisha told Nia afterward.

"Still your work."

"Yes. Both things."

Imani's softball team played two road games decided by one run. She did not produce a dramatic highlight. She moved a runner with a sacrifice bunt, made a difficult backhand stop, and called a defensive shift that prevented a tying run.

McCall-Hart swept the series through accumulated decisions that would look small in a summary.

The friends followed both events through updates because the Delta trip and travel schedules prevented everyone from attending. No one confused distance with lack of support.

Malik saw the relay video while sitting beside Simone on the return bus. They spoke politely about sports, the lab sessions, and work due Friday.

Politeness had become a boundary around the conversation they could no longer postpone.

## Thursday, March 26: Honest Is Not Harmless

Simone asked Malik one final question after they returned to campus.

"What does your connection with Nia mean?"

They sat in an empty Porter Center classroom. The projector fan hummed above them.

Malik could name what it had meant: friendship, competition, attraction, waiting, resentment, shared academic ambition. He could not name what it meant now without reaching beyond what Nia had said or what he had chosen.

"I do not know," he answered.

It was honest.

Simone's face tightened. "And you have been dating me while not knowing."

"I know what you mean to me."

"Do you? Or do you know that I am the person you chose when you decided waiting for Nia was unhealthy?"

"That is not all this is."

"I believe you. That is why this hurts."

Simone did not accuse him of using her. Their relationship had contained real affection, attention, humor, and growth. It had also contained a room he kept comparing with another room he had never entered.

"I need time," she said.

Malik did not ask how much. He had turned enough feelings into schedules.

She left the classroom alone.

His honesty had ended one kind of hiding. It had also given Simone information she could use to make a choice he might not like.

Truth did not promise mercy. It promised that the person affected could finally decide with the real evidence.

---

# Week Thirty-Five: Approximation Is Not Arrival

Nia began the symposium rehearsal with a power series, three convergence intervals, and a sentence containing the phrase *heterogeneous notification latency*.

Six minutes later, the community reviewer raised one hand.

"I understand that the model changes," she said. "I do not understand what decision you want anybody to make."

Nia looked at the slide behind her. It contained two equations, a route network, a color key, and enough footnotes to qualify as a separate presentation.

"The decision is whether a route remains reliable under changing conditions," she said.

"Reliable for whom? And who is deciding?"

Nia opened her mouth.

Dr. Price stopped the timer.

## Friday, March 27: Correct and Unclear

The reviewer, Ms. Elaine Carter, worked with a Bellwether neighborhood preparedness coalition. She had agreed to evaluate whether the team's presentation communicated anything residents or local organizers could use.

Nia had prepared for mathematical questions. Ms. Carter asked where a parent without a car should look on the map. She asked whether one changing route meant everyone should leave differently. She asked what the warning colors instructed people to do.

The presentation contained answers to some of those questions, but they were buried beneath method names and validation language.

"I wanted to prove the model was rigorous," Nia said.

"To whom?" Ms. Carter asked.

The fellowship judges mattered. So did technical reviewers. But Nia had presented as if complexity itself demonstrated readiness.

Keisha's live model performed correctly. Julian's animation displayed every uncertainty. Neither mattered if the audience could not connect the output to a decision.

Ms. Carter pointed to one route change. "If I am watching this, what should I know now that I did not know before?"

Nia finally answered without jargon. "That a recommended road can stop being the safest choice when warnings arrive late and traffic concentrates there. The map should show why the recommendation changed and who may not have received the same information."

"That," Ms. Carter said, "sounds like your talk."

The talk Nia had given was technically correct.

It had not arrived where the audience stood.

## Saturday, March 28: Unfinished at Minute Six

Dr. Price replayed the first six minutes the next morning.

Nia watched herself define every term before stating the decision. She sounded prepared, intelligent, and almost impossible to follow.

At minute six, Dr. Price paused the video.

"Correctness without audience understanding is unfinished work," she said. "Do not confuse density with seriousness."

Nia felt defensive. Unlike last month, she recognized the feeling before it became an argument.

"What must stay?" she asked.

Keisha named the scenario comparison, uncertainty limits, and data gaps. Julian named the changing route and the source of each warning. Ms. Carter wanted one household's choices visible from alert to departure.

Nia wrote all four lists.

Dr. Price compared the current talk to a Taylor polynomial. A limited set of terms could approximate a complicated function near a useful center. Adding more terms might improve accuracy, but only if the audience could still use the representation and the error remained visible.

"Your presentation is an approximation of the full research," she said. "It is not the full research read aloud."

Nia deleted the opening definition slide.

Then she deleted eleven more.

Each removal hurt for approximately four seconds.

## Sunday, March 29: Less Motion, More Meaning

Julian removed half the animation.

The old version displayed alerts pulsing, roads changing color, probability bands expanding, and route markers moving simultaneously. It looked impressive. It required the viewer to watch four events with one pair of eyes.

The new version followed one household.

An official alert reached late. The household waited for a family member. Traffic increased on the initial route. The recommendation changed, and a caption explained which assumptions drove the update. Only then did the view widen to show other scenarios.

Keisha rebuilt the demonstration around the same sequence. Instead of clicking through every software feature, she changed one input at a time and showed the stored history.

"We are not saying this household represents everybody," she said.

"We say that aloud," Nia replied.

Ms. Carter reviewed the revision and immediately identified the decision: do not publish one route without showing when and why it may change.

"Now I can disagree with you," she said.

Nia smiled. "That is good?"

"It means I can understand enough to challenge the recommendation."

The team added her criticism to the notes.

Communication was not successful because the audience nodded. It was successful when the audience could locate the claim, inspect its limits, and ask a better question.

## Monday, March 30: The Offer Is Hers

Simone received the London offer at 8:14 Monday morning.

The internship included placement with a financial-data team, housing assistance, professional mentoring, and eight weeks in a city she had imagined since high school.

She read every condition. She checked the dates against McCall-Hart's calendar. She called her mother. Then she accepted.

She told Malik that afternoon.

"You already accepted," he said.

"Yes."

He was proud of her. He was also hurt that the decision had happened without him.

"I thought we would discuss it first."

"We discussed whether I wanted it. I told you that if I received the offer, I was going."

"Discussing the offer is different from deciding without me."

Simone held his gaze. "The career decision is mine. The relationship decision is ours. I did not need permission to accept London."

Malik knew she was right. Knowing did not remove the feeling of being outside an important moment.

"I did not want you to ask me to wait while we evaluated us," she continued. "I have waited too often to name vulnerable things. This time I decided what I wanted."

"I would not have asked you to reject it."

"Maybe not. But you wanted consultation to prove I placed the relationship inside the decision."

Malik had no honest denial.

The offer removed the shelter of *if*.

London was now on the calendar.

## Tuesday, March 31: Consultation Is Not Commitment

Malik walked with DJ after football conditioning.

"She accepted before telling me," he said.

DJ adjusted the strap of his equipment bag. "Would you have told her not to?"

"No."

"Did she say she would go if she got it?"

"Yes."

"Then what did you want the conversation to do?"

Malik considered the question.

He had wanted Simone to bring the offer to him before accepting so they could examine dates, distance, and consequences. Beneath that reasonable desire was another: he wanted her pause to demonstrate that their relationship had enough authority to delay her answer.

"I treated consultation as proof of commitment," he said.

"That sounds like something Dr. Bennett would make you write in a reflection."

"It feels worse than that."

Malik messaged Simone. He congratulated her again without adding the relationship question. He apologized for making her acceptance sound like a procedural failure.

She thanked him.

She did not reassure him about summer.

Pride and hurt remained together. Malik allowed both without turning the hurt into evidence that Simone had chosen wrongly.

## Wednesday, April 1: The Next Two Plays

Imani misplayed a ground ball in the softball rivalry opener.

The error extended the inning. McCall-Hart lost by two.

The story seemed prepared to offer her a dramatic correction in the next game. It did not.

Imani went hitless Saturday but fielded every routine ball cleanly. She communicated a coverage shift that prevented an extra base. In the final game, she made a disciplined cutoff throw instead of attempting a spectacular play at home.

The Herons won the series two games to one.

A teammate teased her about not producing a redemption home run.

"Redemption is expensive," Imani said. "I chose defense."

Nia attended the final game after rehearsal. She watched Imani handle ordinary chances without trying to manufacture one heroic moment that would erase Friday.

Afterward, Imani asked about the presentation.

"I removed twelve slides."

"Did they deserve it?"

"Eleven did. I am grieving one."

"You will survive."

Improvement did not always announce itself through spectacle. Sometimes it was the next routine play handled with care.

## Thursday, April 2: Strong Is Not Guaranteed

Nia's second rehearsal began with one household receiving a delayed alert.

Within thirty seconds, Ms. Carter understood the stakes. Keisha changed one assumption and showed the route history. Julian's visual slowed at each decision. Nia explained the mathematics after the audience knew why it mattered.

At minute six, Dr. Price did not stop the timer.

The full talk ended with twelve seconds remaining.

Questions came quickly. Nia admitted where interview data was too limited. Keisha distinguished software behavior from human prediction. Julian explained why the animation displayed uncertainty instead of smoothing it away.

Ms. Carter could name the decision, the limitation, and the question she still wanted answered.

The rehearsal had landed.

Nia expected relief. Dr. Price gave her perspective.

"This is strong work," she said. "Strong work does not guarantee the fellowship. Other teams will be strong. Some will have more data, more experience, or methods that better match the judges' priorities."

"I know."

"Knowing before the result is different from living it afterward. Prepare to present what you can defend, not to control what they choose."

Nia looked at the final slide.

The talk approximated months of work. The judges would approximate the team's potential from one brief encounter. Both processes had error.

She wanted the fellowship anyway.

Wanting it did not make the outcome hers.

---

# Week Thirty-Six: Different Parameters

Dr. Delgado drew two parametric curves.

Both used time as the parameter. At every moment, each curve had its own horizontal and vertical position. For several values, the points appeared close. Then one curve turned while the other continued forward.

"Sharing a parameter does not mean sharing a path," he said.

Nia calculated velocity components. Malik found where one curve slowed. Amara identified the moment their directions diverged.

Outside class, Friday was the course-withdrawal deadline. Students made decisions about classes they had entered months earlier with different information. Staying was not automatically brave. Leaving was not automatically failure. The question was which path remained honest under current conditions.

Malik copied both curves into his notes.

Two people could share time, affection, and important experiences without moving toward the same destination.

## Friday, April 3: Same Time, Different Motion

Dr. Delgado asked the class to distinguish speed from direction.

Two objects could move at the same speed and travel apart. One object could change direction without stopping. Arc length could measure the distance along a path without explaining why the path had turned.

"Do not let one shared quantity persuade you that two motions are identical," he said.

After class, Malik met Simone at the Porter Center.

They had spent the week discussing London in careful pieces. Underneath every practical question was the larger one neither could postpone: was distance creating their incompatibility, or revealing it?

Simone carried the copper notebook. Many pages now contained answers, crossed-out thoughts, and new questions.

"I want to talk tomorrow," she said. "Not between meetings. Not during interview practice."

Malik understood that she had chosen the time deliberately.

"Okay."

They did not fill the silence with one more pleasant evening.

Across campus, Nia reviewed the final symposium materials with Keisha and Julian. She knew Malik and Simone were strained. She did not ask whether the strain had created an opening.

Respect, her mother had said, was something active.

Nia practiced it by remaining on her own path.

## Saturday, April 4: London Is Not the Whole Reason

Simone met Malik beneath the covered terrace behind Porter Center.

"London matters," she began. "I want the internship. I do not want to begin it by promising that every free hour belongs to maintaining a relationship across an ocean."

Malik listened.

"But London is not the whole reason," she continued. "I have felt you comparing us with an unfinished possibility. Sometimes when Nia enters a room, you become more aware of what our relationship proves. When Julian is near her, you become more afraid of what you lost."

"I did not choose you to prove something."

"I believe that. I also believe you entered this relationship partly because moving forward felt healthier than waiting."

Both statements could be true.

Simone opened the notebook to one of Malik's original questions: *What does support feel like when you are disappointed?*

"Support is not asking me to remain inside a comparison until you resolve it," she said. "And care is not pretending London alone is taking me away."

Malik's instinct was to promise he would stop comparing. The promise would demand that Simone trust a future correction she had already spent spring waiting to see.

He did not make it.

"You deserved my full presence," he said.

"I deserved honesty about what full meant."

They sat with the difference.

## Sunday, April 5: The Apology Without a Request

Malik returned Sunday with no argument for why Simone should stay.

"I am sorry," he said. "I thought moving forward meant never looking back. I treated choosing you as proof that everything unresolved had disappeared. Then whenever it came back, I hid it because admitting it felt disloyal."

Simone listened.

"That made the feeling more powerful," he continued. "And it made you carry consequences for something you did not cause."

He apologized for the distant interview practice, the private comparisons, and the expectation that consultation would prove commitment. He did not describe every good thing he had done as a counterweight. Care was not a credit balance.

"Our relationship was real," Simone said.

"Yes."

"I do not want your apology to rewrite it as a mistake."

"I do not either."

They remembered the food-distribution station, the road trip, her panel, the copper notebook, and the many ordinary lunches that had never needed to become lessons.

Malik did not ask her to give him more time. He did not promise that Nia would become irrelevant. He apologized without converting accountability into a request for another chance.

For Simone, that made the apology kinder.

It did not change her decision.

## Monday, April 6: No Promise After London

They ended the relationship where it had grown: the Porter Center.

Students moved through the lobby carrying laptops and coffee. Somewhere upstairs, a finance club meeting began. The ordinary building refused to become a private stage.

"I am leaving for London," Simone said. "But even if I were staying in Bellwether, I would need to step out of this comparison."

"I understand."

"Do not tell me we can try again after summer."

Malik swallowed. "I will not."

They made no promise to resume when she returned. They did not call the relationship a failure because it ended. Simone had learned that she could name ambition without apology. Malik had learned that choosing someone required more than stopping pursuit of somebody else.

Simone returned the archival-photo sleeve she had borrowed for a presentation. Malik told her to keep the copper notebook.

"The questions are yours now," he said.

She held it against her bag.

They hugged once. Neither hurried away afterward. Sadness occupied the lobby with them until Simone finally turned toward the stairs and Malik walked outside.

No one was humiliated. No one had won.

Two paths that had shared a season separated with both histories intact.

## Tuesday, April 7: Hope Arrives Too Fast

Malik told Nia himself.

He asked her to meet on Cypress Walk in the afternoon. His face told her the news before the sentence did.

"Simone and I ended our relationship yesterday."

Hope rose inside Nia with terrible speed.

It was immediate, bright, and followed by guilt so sharp that she almost apologized aloud.

Malik looked exhausted. "She chose London. But that was not the only reason."

Nia did not ask whether she was another reason. "I am sorry."

"Thank you."

They stood beneath new spring leaves. Nia wanted to comfort him. She also knew that part of her had already turned his pain into possibility. The knowledge made every instinct suspect.

"Do you want company?" she asked.

"Not right now. I wanted you to hear it from me rather than through the group."

"Okay."

She let him leave.

Back in North Star, she told Keisha about the hope.

"A feeling can arrive before your values," Keisha said. "What matters is whether you let it drive."

Nia opened the symposium script instead of Malik's messages.

Hope had arrived.

It did not receive permission to act.

## Wednesday, April 8: No Shortcut Through Grief

Nia found Malik after calculus.

Dr. Delgado had assigned an arc-length problem. Nia used the mathematics as an excuse to begin talking, then heard herself drifting toward the conversation she wanted.

"We never really discussed fall," she said. "Not directly."

Malik closed his notebook.

"No," he said. "We did not."

The quiet invited her to continue.

"Maybe after the symposium, we could--"

"Nia."

He did not sound angry. That made the boundary clearer.

"I am not turning your breakup or mine into a shortcut," he said. "Julian mattered to you. Simone mattered to me. We both need to understand what happened without using each other to prove we are ready."

Embarrassment heated Nia's face. "I was not asking you to date me tomorrow."

"I know. But you were asking us to begin moving in that direction. I cannot."

She wanted to argue that her relationship had ended months ago, that she had respected his, that waiting now felt unfair.

Respect was not a ticket that matured into a claim.

"You are right," she said.

"I care about you. That is why I need the boundary."

The sentence hurt and steadied her at once.

They left separately.

## Thursday, April 9: Submit Without the Answer

The research team completed its final symposium submission at 6:42 Thursday evening.

Keisha verified the software package and checksum. Julian confirmed every media permission and caption. Nia uploaded the presentation, methods summary, limitations statement, and fellowship-interview materials.

Dr. Price required each person to approve the final version.

Keisha clicked first. Julian clicked second. Nia clicked last.

The portal displayed **SUBMISSION COMPLETE**.

They celebrated with takeout in the lab. Simone sent a brief message congratulating all three. Nia replied with thanks and did not mention Malik. Julian discussed the documentary cut. Keisha made everyone close their laptops for twenty minutes.

Later, Malik arrived to return a risk-analysis article Nia had lent him. They stepped into the hallway.

"The submission is done," she said.

"Congratulations."

"I meant what I said yesterday. You were right."

Malik nodded. "Through the presentation, we need distance. No private late-night preparation. No using the research as a reason to have the conversation early."

"Agreed."

The boundary protected both of them. It also removed the person Nia most wanted to use as evidence that she would be okay no matter what the judges decided.

She returned to her team.

The symposium would ask her to stand behind the work without certainty, romance, or reconciliation as emotional support beams.

For the first time all year, Nia would have to enter the most important room wanting something deeply and carrying no private promise about what waited after the answer.

---

# Week Thirty-Seven: The Presentation and the No

Dr. Delgado placed a map of concentric warning zones on the screen.

Every point within the outer circle lay the same maximum distance from the broadcast tower. The picture suggested equal access.

Nia raised her hand. "Distance from the source is not distance to a safe route."

"Explain."

She walked to the board and marked two households at the same radius. One had a nearby road leading away from the flood zone. The other sat beyond a rail crossing and a low underpass. In polar coordinates, both shared the same value of (r). In the road network, their choices were not remotely equal.

"A coordinate system makes some relationships easy to see," she said. "It can make others disappear."

Dr. Delgado nodded. "Then choose the system that serves the question, and state what it hides."

The symposium presentation waited the next morning.

Nia wrote the sentence at the top of her final notes.

## Friday, April 10: Equal Radius, Unequal Access

The class calculated polar area for regions shaped like petals, loops, and overlapping circles.

Nia loved how a complicated Cartesian boundary could become a simple radial description. She also recognized the temptation to confuse mathematical convenience with human truth.

For the symposium, the team used a polar overlay to show distance from a warning source. Then they placed the real road network above it. Households at equal radial distance faced different travel times, bridge access, vehicle availability, and warning delays.

Keisha tested the live model one last time. Julian checked the order of every visual. Nia rehearsed the opening until she could say it without rushing.

Malik stayed away, honoring their agreement. He sent one message to the full team: **You have prepared the evidence. Trust each other in the room.**

Nia appreciated the message because it did not become a private promise.

That evening, she packed her conference clothes, calculator, backup drive, printed slides, and enough snacks for a small emergency shelter. Keisha removed half the snacks.

"We are traveling for three days, not establishing a colony."

"Preparedness is part of our research identity."

"Preparedness has weight limits."

Nia finally closed the suitcase.

She had done everything she knew how to do. The next step was not more preparation.

It was entering the room.

## Saturday, April 11: The Other Posters

The Southeastern Undergraduate Mathematics Symposium occupied a university conference center two hours from Bellwether.

The first poster Nia saw used a dataset with millions of records. The second listed a federal grant. A third belonged to seniors who had worked together for two years and already had a journal manuscript under review.

Nia looked at their foam-board poster and suddenly saw every taped edge.

"Do not compare our January to somebody else's second year," Keisha said.

"I am comparing our printer budget to federal funding."

"That comparison is valid and unhelpful."

They attended presentations before their own session. One team predicted hospital demand using carefully anonymized records. Another modeled wildfire evacuation with a cleaner road dataset than anything Nia had imagined possible. The presenters answered questions with practiced ease.

Julian noticed what Nia did not. "That hospital team has no public-facing explanation of their uncertainty."

Keisha noticed that the wildfire model stored only final outputs, not scenario histories.

Every project had strengths and omissions. More funding did not make a team flawless. Less funding did not make McCall-Hart's work secretly superior.

Nia stopped sorting the room into threats and proof.

They pinned up the poster, connected the laptop, and tested the backup.

Their work had its own question to answer.

## Sunday, April 12: The Talk They Built Together

Nia began with one household receiving a delayed warning.

Julian advanced the visual sequence. The audience watched the initial route fill as other households departed earlier. Keisha changed one road-capacity assumption in the live model. The recommended path shifted, and the history panel displayed why.

Nia explained the mathematics only after the decision was visible.

Questions came from every side.

How were trust rates estimated?

"They are not population estimates," Nia said. "We use bounded scenarios informed by a small approved interview set and published studies. The local interviews identify mechanisms, not prevalence."

Could the system predict individual behavior?

"No," Keisha answered. "It tests how different patterns affect network recommendations."

Why animate the changes?

Julian explained that a still map hid the sequence of assumptions, then acknowledged that animation could imply false authority. He showed the source labels and pause controls added after audience testing.

A judge asked whether officials should use the model now.

Nia felt the fellowship hanging inside the question. She could make the work sound ready.

"Not for operational routing," she said. "It has not been validated for that. It can help identify which data gaps and communication assumptions make a route recommendation unstable."

The answer limited the project.

It also told the truth.

When the session ended, Keisha squeezed Nia's hand. Julian whispered, "No one fainted, including the software."

They had given the talk they built together.

## Monday, April 13: Praise and the Gap

The fellowship interview took place in a smaller room.

Three judges asked about public value, technical design, team roles, and future work. They praised the project's integration of mathematics, software, and communication. They praised the decision to expose assumptions rather than publish one authoritative route.

Then they identified the gaps.

The model lacked validation against a real event or exercise. The community interview sample was too limited. Transportation access remained underdeveloped. The route network covered too small an area to establish scalability. The team had not yet tested whether repeated route updates confused users.

Nia answered without defensiveness.

"Those are the next validation questions," she said. "We would not expand the tool before testing them with community and emergency-management partners."

One judge asked who would own the project if the fellowship funded it.

"No one student," Nia replied. "Keisha and I are co-leads. Julian owns the approved media workflow. Community participants control how their contributions are used. Any operational partnership would require shared governance beyond our team."

Afterward, Dr. Price told them they had represented the work well.

Nia wanted to ask whether that meant they would win.

She knew better.

Praise described what the judges valued. It did not erase what remained unresolved or predict their final choice.

## Tuesday, April 14: The Other Name

The awards ceremony began at four.

Nia sat between Keisha and Julian. Her hands were cold despite the crowded ballroom.

Several project awards came first. She applauded each team. When the fellowship category appeared on the screen, the room seemed to narrow around the presenter holding the envelope.

The fellowship went to a team studying heat-risk alerts for outdoor workers.

For one fraction of a second, Nia waited for another name.

None came.

She applauded.

The winning students stood in shock, then hugged. Their work was strong. Their joy did not insult her. Nia smiled when they passed her row.

As soon as the ceremony ended, she told Keisha she needed the restroom.

She walked past it and found a concrete stairwell between conference floors.

The door closed behind her.

Nia sat on the second step and cried.

She cried because she had wanted the laboratory placement, the mentors, the money, the title, and the proof that the better version of herself could receive a better ending. She cried because the judges' gaps were real and because she had still hoped the strength of the talk would outweigh them.

No audience watched. No one needed her to be gracious or inspirational.

The fellowship belonged to another team.

The no belonged fully to her.

## Wednesday, April 15: Wait Outside the Door

Keisha found Nia's location from a short message: **Stairwell B. I am safe. Not ready.**

She entered, sat two steps below, and said nothing.

Julian stayed in the hallway with their bags. Dr. Price handled checkout. The team protected Nia's privacy without pretending disappointment was an emergency to solve.

Malik had traveled to the symposium for the final day with Dr. Bennett and several actuarial students. He heard the result, found Julian outside the stairwell, and stopped.

"Is she in there?"

"Yes."

Malik did not enter.

He waited against the opposite wall. The boundary from last week still existed. Grief did not cancel consent.

Keisha eventually came out.

"She asked if you are here," she said. "I told her yes. She wants to talk to you."

Only then did Malik open the door.

Nia had washed her face, but her eyes were swollen. She shifted to make room on the step.

"I did not win," she said.

"I know."

Malik sat beside her without touching her.

The stairwell remained quiet enough for the truth to stay unperformed.

## Thursday, April 16: No Lesson Tonight

"I did everything better," Nia said.

Her voice broke on the final word.

"I shared ownership. I fixed the model. I admitted uncertainty. I listened to criticism. I gave the best presentation I have ever given, and I still lost."

Malik did not tell her the experience would open another door. He did not say rejection built character, the project still mattered, or the judges might regret their choice.

"Yes," he said. "You did everything better, and you still lost."

Nia looked at him, almost angry that he agreed.

"That is all?"

"The loss is real. It does not need a lesson tonight."

The sentence removed the work of finding a silver lining before she was ready.

Nia leaned forward with her elbows on her knees. "I wanted it so much."

"I know."

"I do not want to be mature about it."

"Then do not be mature in a stairwell."

She laughed once through tears.

Malik waited until she asked before holding her hand. The gesture was comfort, not a promise. Keisha remained near the door. Julian guarded the luggage and told anyone approaching that the stairwell was temporarily experiencing advanced mathematics.

Eventually, the team left together.

The project was still theirs. The fellowship was not.

Nothing that night required Nia to pretend those facts weighed the same.

---

# Week Thirty-Eight: What Remains

Nia opened the project folder and selected everything.

The computer displayed one question: **Move 487 items to Trash?**

She hovered over the button.

The fellowship version contained months of code, maps, interviews, drafts, mistakes, permissions, and the presentation that had failed to produce the result she wanted. Deleting it would not change the ceremony. It would remove the evidence that she had cared enough to be hurt.

Nia canceled.

She created an archive folder named **SYMPOSIUM_FELLOWSHIP_VERSION_FINAL**. Then she opened a new project file.

For the title, she typed:

**COMMUNITY VALIDATION**

She did not feel hopeful.

She simply was not finished.

## Friday, April 17: Archive, Do Not Erase

Keisha found the new file during their final full class week.

"You want to continue?" she asked.

"I want to find out whether the model can become useful. I do not want to act like that makes losing okay."

"Good. Because it does not."

They wrote no triumphant comeback plan. Instead, they listed validation needs from the judges: more representative community input, transportation constraints, repeated-message testing, larger-network performance, and governance beyond the student team.

Julian joined later and separated the fellowship media package from raw interview materials. Every participant's permissions remained attached. Reuse would require the same limits, not a vague claim that the project had evolved.

Nia changed the project status from **FELLOWSHIP FINALIST** to **RESEARCH IN REVISION**.

The new label felt smaller.

It was also accurate.

At calculus, Dr. Delgado reviewed polar coordinates, integration methods, and series. He asked students to explain not only how to solve a problem but when the chosen representation stopped helping.

Nia understood the question differently now.

The fellowship version had taken the project as far as it could. Archiving it preserved the work without requiring the future to remain inside its boundaries.

## Saturday, April 18: Useful After Ownership

The guest seminar on public mathematics drew a smaller crowd than the free-lunch announcement deserved.

Dr. Laila Monroe described a rejected transit study she had carried for years. The original research belonged to an academic team, used inaccessible language, and assumed public agencies would adopt the model because its predictions were accurate.

They did not.

The work became useful only after researchers shared decision-making with riders, drivers, disability advocates, and local planners. Those partners changed the questions, removed features they did not need, and refused several recommendations the academic team considered elegant.

"Relinquishing ownership did not mean abandoning expertise," Dr. Monroe said. "It meant accepting that being skilled at the mathematics did not make us sole authors of the problem."

Nia asked whether rejection had motivated the change.

"Partly," Dr. Monroe replied. "But do not romanticize it. I would have preferred the grant. Rejection gave us fewer resources, not secret wisdom. People gave the project a different life because we eventually learned to listen."

That answer mattered.

Nia did not need to thank the fellowship judges for saying no. She could dislike the outcome and still refuse to make it the final authority on the work.

After the seminar, she added community decision rights to the validation plan.

The project could continue only by becoming less exclusively theirs.

## Sunday, April 19: A Question Julian Is Allowed to Ask

Julian met Nia in the radio studio to finish the symposium documentary archive.

They watched the final interview clip, the one where Nia compared elegant models with beautiful relationship stories. When the screen went dark, Julian remained quiet.

"Can I ask something personal?" he said.

"Yes."

"Working together went well. Better than I expected. Does any part of you think we should try dating again?"

Nia felt no panic, anger, or secret relief. Only affection for someone who deserved a direct answer.

"No," she said. "What we had mattered. What we built this spring matters. I do not want to restart the relationship."

Julian nodded slowly. "I thought that was the answer. I needed to hear it rather than turn collaboration into a sign."

"Thank you for asking instead of producing a documentary called *Signs Nia Missed*."

"The working title was stronger."

They laughed.

Nia did not punish him by withdrawing warmth. Julian did not bargain with the success of the project or invoke their good memories as evidence. They finished the archive, confirmed future media responsibilities, and walked out as collaborators whose past no longer required a sequel.

Closure was not silence. Sometimes it was a question answered clearly enough that neither person had to keep interpreting.

## Monday, April 20: Return Without Rewriting

Simone and Malik exchanged the remaining pieces of spring.

He returned a finance book and a scarf she had left in North Star. She returned his museum ticket, which had been tucked inside the copper notebook, and a folder of family-photo scans she had backed up.

They met in the Porter Center lobby again.

"London orientation starts after commencement," Simone said. "I leave for the program in May."

"Do you need the international phone-plan research?"

She smiled faintly. "Send me the comparison without the relationship schedule."

"Agreed."

They discussed practical details and mutual friends. Neither used friendliness to suggest reunion. Neither turned the breakup into evidence that dating had been a mistake.

"I am glad we had spring," Simone said.

Malik felt sadness arrive without emergency. "So am I."

She kept the notebook. He kept what its questions had taught him.

When they parted, Malik did not watch her leave and imagine Nia waiting somewhere beyond the door. He walked to Dr. Bennett's office to prepare for finals.

Meaning did not require continuation.

## Tuesday, April 21: One Finale Wins, One Series Does Not

Imani's softball team needed one victory in its home finale to secure a conference-tournament place.

They won behind disciplined pitching, two sacrifice bunts, and a sixth-inning double that scored the decisive run. Imani fielded the final ground ball and threw to first. The team rushed the circle as the tournament berth became official.

Across campus, baseball lost its home series and fell out of realistic postseason contention.

The two outcomes arrived within an hour.

The friend group celebrated softball at the field, then visited baseball friends packing equipment after the loss. No one asked the baseball seniors to become inspirational about it. No one told Imani to mute her joy.

"This is becoming a recurring campus lesson," DJ said.

"Apparently people keep needing it," Imani replied.

Nia cheered until her throat hurt. The softball win did not repair the fellowship rejection. That made the celebration easier, not harder. It asked nothing from her story.

For one evening, she could be happy because her friend had earned a future game.

## Wednesday, April 22: Different Times Are Not Enough

Malik and Nia met in daylight, on purpose, with no research materials between them.

"I wanted you in fall," Malik said. "You were with Julian."

"I wanted you in spring," Nia replied. "You were with Simone."

The symmetry sounded neat. Their lives had not been.

Malik had waited too long and treated patience as a private future claim. Nia had mistaken his availability for permanence. Both had formed real relationships with other people. Both had used jealousy to discover feelings they should have examined directly.

"If timing were the only problem," Nia said, "we would be together now."

"Timing is not enough."

Trust had changed. So had their understanding of themselves. Malik did not know whether he could choose Nia without making her the resolution of spring. Nia did not know whether she wanted Malik as he was or as proof that the reversed year had finally become fair.

"I do care about you," she said.

"I care about you too."

Neither converted the admission into a plan.

They had finally arrived at the same emotional moment.

That did not mean they were ready to travel from it together.

## Thursday, April 23: Nobody Waiting in the Wings

Nia and Malik chose finals.

The decision sounded boring enough to be healthy.

They would not schedule a first date after exams, promise to revisit the relationship in summer, or wait for the other person to become ready. They would study, finish their responsibilities, and allow the next semester to reveal who they were without romantic suspense organizing every interaction.

"No private promises," Malik said.

"No hidden countdowns," Nia added.

"No asking Keisha or DJ for intelligence reports."

"I have never called them intelligence reports."

"Your face has."

She laughed.

They returned to Dr. Delgado's review session and sat at different tables. Nia studied polar area with Amara. Malik reviewed integration methods with DJ. Keisha joined both groups only after completing track recovery.

No one waited in the wings.

The unresolved future remained open without becoming anyone's assignment.

Finals were next.

For once, the exam was the simpler uncertainty.

---

# Week Thirty-Nine: No Shortcut Through Finals

The Calculus II final began with a page titled **METHOD SELECTION**.

Before computing anything, students had to identify a technique for six problems and justify each choice. Substitution. Integration by parts. Partial fractions. A convergence test. Parametric arc length. Polar area.

No answer from a later page could rescue a method chosen without reasoning.

Nia read the full set before writing. Malik did the same.

All year, both had tried to use action as reassurance. Start calculating. Build the schedule. Enter the relationship. Give the presentation. Movement felt safer than uncertainty.

The exam demanded a commitment before computation offered comfort.

They began.

## Friday, April 24: Choose Before the Work Begins

Nia labeled each method in the margin.

For the rational function, she factored the denominator before choosing partial fractions. For the series, she noted that factorial growth made the ratio test efficient. For polar area, she sketched the interval to avoid tracing the region twice.

Malik identified integration by parts, then checked which factor should become (u). The obvious-looking choice was not always the one that simplified under differentiation.

Dr. Delgado moved silently between rows.

The last full spring class week ended with no speeches. Students carried notes, snacks, and exhaustion toward finals. Outside, organizations advertised move-out drives and summer storage. The campus had begun preparing to empty while everyone still had work left.

Nia saw Malik at the water fountain during a break. They exchanged one nod.

No whispered predictions. No emotional check-in. Their agreement held.

The final was not a metaphor they could solve for their relationship. It was an exam, and each needed to complete it independently.

That fact felt almost restful.

## Saturday, April 25: Restart While the Clock Moves

Malik reached the integration-by-parts problem and moved too fast.

He selected (u) poorly, differentiated, integrated the remaining factor, and produced a new integral worse than the original. In October, he might have forced the method forward rather than admit the first choice had failed.

The clock displayed fifty-two minutes remaining.

He crossed out the setup.

Restarting cost time. Continuing would cost the problem.

Malik chose a logarithmic factor for (u), simplified correctly, and completed the integral. He marked the abandoned attempt clearly so Dr. Delgado could see the correction rather than mistake it for competing work.

The restart changed his pace. For the next problem, he paused before moving.

He thought briefly of Simone, then returned to the page. Their relationship could not be restarted with a better method. The lesson was not that every wrong choice deserved another attempt. It was that honesty about a failing process mattered before the clock decided for you.

Malik completed the final line with nine minutes left.

He used all nine to check assumptions.

## Sunday, April 26: Distrusting the Right Instinct

Nia recognized the divergent series immediately.

The terms did not approach zero. The nth-term test settled the question.

She wrote the conclusion, then doubted it.

The fellowship rejection had made confidence feel suspicious. She had given a strong talk and still been judged incomplete. Now every clear instinct seemed like possible evidence that she had missed something.

Nia attempted a comparison test. Then she considered the ratio test. Neither was necessary. Minutes disappeared while she tried to prove the result three different ways.

Finally, she heard Dr. Delgado's review-session warning: use the simplest test that resolves the question.

She crossed out the extra work and restored the original argument. If the terms of an infinite series failed to approach zero, the series diverged. No elaborate proof made the conclusion more true.

Confidence was not certainty that every instinct was right. It was the ability to test an instinct against an appropriate standard and stop when the evidence was sufficient.

Nia moved to the next problem with less time than she wanted and enough time to finish.

## Monday, April 27: Work Without Points

When the exam ended, students flooded the hallway and began reconstructing questions from memory.

"Was the polar interval zero to pi or zero to two pi?"

"What did you get for the ratio limit?"

"Did anybody finish the arc length?"

Dr. Delgado stepped into the doorway. "I will not grade by hallway vote. Stop conducting statistical analysis on a sample selected by panic."

He refused every request to estimate scores.

Instead, he asked one final question.

"What work from this course would you still choose if no points were attached?"

Amara named tutoring another student through identities because teaching had made her less afraid to speak. Malik chose risk problems where the method changed a decision. Nia chose the evacuation research, though the fellowship had assigned it no reward.

"Then protect that answer from grades," Dr. Delgado said. "A course evaluates specific work under specific conditions. It does not own every reason you learned."

Outside, Nia did not ask Malik how he performed. He did not ask her.

They left the result where it belonged: with the exam.

## Tuesday, April 28: One Uncomplicated Celebration

The softball rivalry game reached the bottom of the seventh tied.

McCall-Hart needed the win to secure a stronger conference-tournament seed. Two outs. Runner on second. Imani at the plate.

The first pitch missed outside. The second became a foul. The third came low and fast.

Imani drove it through the left side.

The runner rounded third. The throw arrived late. Safe.

The dugout emptied.

Imani disappeared beneath teammates, dirt, and an indigo sports-drink shower. The crowd shouted until every difficult conversation, rejected fellowship, final exam, and uncertain summer briefly lost volume.

Nia hugged Keisha. DJ nearly climbed the fence. Malik laughed when the celebration carried Imani's helmet in one direction and Imani in another.

The walk-off did not teach them how to handle loss. It did not symbolize convergence, trust, or second chances.

It was simply wonderful.

For one night, nobody translated it.

## Wednesday, April 29: Continue Under New Conditions

Dr. Price invited Nia and Keisha to her office.

The summer Quantitative Scholars program had space for the evacuation project to continue. The invitation came with conditions.

The team would not repackage the symposium model and pursue another award. They would build a community-validation plan, obtain appropriate approvals, expand transportation assumptions, and share governance with local partners. Dr. Renee Okafor would oversee the mathematical work alongside Dr. Price.

"This is not the fellowship," Dr. Price said.

"I know," Nia replied.

The program was campus-based, paid less, and offered no national title. It also provided time, mentoring, and a path to make the rejected work useful.

Nia looked at Keisha. "Co-leads?"

"In writing," Keisha said.

Dr. Price slid the draft appointments across the desk. Nia was project lead. Keisha was computation co-lead.

Keisha raised one eyebrow.

Nia did not accept.

"Both titles need to say co-lead," she said. "Different responsibilities, equal project authority."

Dr. Price nodded and marked the revision.

They took the offer home to consider.

The work had continued because its questions remained. It had not transformed the no into a yes.

## Thursday, April 30: A Day Before the Answer

Dr. Bennett invited Malik to join the same summer project as a risk-modeling assistant.

"Your actuarial perspective could strengthen the uncertainty and loss assumptions," he said. "You would report to the project co-leads and Dr. Okafor."

Malik understood immediately that Nia and Keisha were the co-leads.

He also understood what accepting would mean: daily work with Nia, unresolved feelings, and a project tied to storms like the one that had damaged his family home.

"I am interested," he said. "Can I have a day before answering?"

Dr. Bennett smiled. "That is why I did not hand you a pen."

Malik reviewed the role rather than the romantic possibility. The project needed scenario risk, documentation of uncertain loss, and validation that did not privilege only insured property. His family experience could inform questions, but he would not turn private damage into evidence without their permission.

He called his parents. He spoke with DJ. He did not call Nia.

Across campus, Nia reviewed her own offer with Keisha. Neither knew whether Malik had been invited.

The summer path offered proximity.

It offered work.

It offered no romantic resolution, and for the first time, Malik waited a day because the decision deserved more than the answer he wanted fastest.

---

# Week Forty: The Space Between Semesters

Spring grades arrived before breakfast.

Nia opened hers under a blanket, as if fabric could protect her from a number already stored in the university system. Malik checked his at the North Star front desk between returning loaner calculators.

Both had passed Calculus II strongly.

Nia stared at the grade and waited for it to change how she felt about the fellowship. It did not. Malik waited for his grade to make the summer project decision obvious. It did not.

A course result could confirm that they had learned integration methods, series, parametric equations, and polar coordinates. It could not evaluate whether Nia's work deserved national funding. It could not determine whether Malik was ready to work beside her without treating proximity as a promise.

They sent each other brief congratulations.

No hidden message traveled underneath.

## Friday, May 1: The Grade Stays in Its Lane

Final examinations ended, and McCall-Hart changed moods in one afternoon.

Students who had moved silently through the week now shouted across courtyards. Library tables emptied. Residence halls filled with open boxes, discarded hangers, and arguments about whether one microwave belonged to a roommate or to "the room as a community."

Nia celebrated her grade with Keisha and Amara over melting ice cream. Keisha had passed her courses while racing, co-leading research, and protecting her time from Nia's scheduling ambitions. Amara had earned one of her strongest math grades after beginning the year afraid to speak at the board.

"We survived Calculus II," Nia announced.

"We learned Calculus II," Amara corrected.

"That is a much more academic slogan."

Malik called his parents. Marcel joked that two semesters of calculus should qualify Malik to calculate when the insurance contractor would finally finish. Celeste reminded both of them that no equation could make a contractor answer the phone.

The good grade belonged to the course. The unfinished home belonged to another problem. The ended relationship belonged to a third.

For once, Malik did not force the results into one story about whether his life was improving.

Each stayed in its lane.

## Saturday, May 2: Walking Past the Fellowship

Spring commencement filled Founders Lawn with cream chairs, family umbrellas, camera phones, and graduates trying not to lose decorated caps in the wind.

Nia watched several student researchers cross the stage. One had presented at the symposium. Another had worked at Delta National Laboratory the previous summer. Their faculty mentors cheered from the side.

The sight reopened the fellowship grief.

Nia imagined the professional photograph she would not take, the laboratory access she would not receive under that award, and the title that would not appear beside her name. The pain arrived without making her feel fraudulent among mathematicians.

That difference mattered.

In the stairwell after the ceremony, she had felt as if rejection might define the edge of her ability. Now she could recognize the fellowship as a door she had not entered, not a verdict that she did not belong in the building.

Dr. Price passed with a group of graduates.

"Are you considering the summer offer?" she asked.

"Yes. I am still disappointed."

"Those facts do not compete."

Nia watched another mathematics student pose with three generations of family beneath the Founders' Bell.

She wanted a future in this work.

The no had changed its route, not its direction.

## Sunday, May 3: The Semifinal

Residence halls closed while Imani's softball team played its conference semifinal.

Nia and Keisha loaded half their room into Denise Reynolds's car, watched the first innings on a phone balanced against a storage bin, and yelled whenever the screen froze at the worst possible moment.

McCall-Hart entered the seventh inning trailing by one. Imani reached base, advanced to second, and was stranded when a deep fly ball died at the warning track.

The game ended there.

No walk-off came. No postseason invitation waited beyond the scoreboard.

Imani's team had produced its most successful season in years. The seniors had changed the program's expectations. They still lost the semifinal they wanted to win.

On the field, players stood in a long line after shaking hands. Some cried immediately. Others remained composed until the coaches gathered them near the dugout.

Nia did not tell herself the season mattered more than the result. The season mattered, and the result hurt.

She understood the grammar now.

Keisha closed the stream only after every senior left the field.

Then they finished moving out of the room where spring had changed both of them.

## Monday, May 4: Pride Without the Trophy

The softball team held a senior dinner on campus before everyone left.

Imani invited the friend group to help decorate. Photographs covered one wall: early losses, rain delays, bus rides, study halls, wins, taped wrists, and seniors laughing beneath stadium lights.

The coach did not call the semifinal a moral victory.

"We wanted the championship," she said. "We did not win it. We also built a season future teams will inherit. Both deserve honest names."

Each senior spoke about one thing she was proud of and one thing she wished had ended differently. The room held both without correcting either.

Nia wrote the sentence on the back of a program: **Pride does not require pretending the trophy was irrelevant.**

Imani saw it. "Are you turning our banquet into research?"

"Personal reflection."

"That sounds worse."

They laughed, took too many pictures, and danced when the formal program ended. The seniors carried no trophy out of the room. They carried their own records, friendships, and the knowledge that the next team would begin from a higher standard.

Nia left still wishing they had won.

She also left proud.

## Tuesday, May 5: Do Not Keep It Half-Alive

Simone left for international internship orientation with one suitcase, one garment bag, and the copper notebook in her backpack.

Malik met her at the shuttle stop. They had already returned personal items and ended the relationship. This goodbye was not another breakup.

"I want to ask one thing," Simone said.

"Okay."

"Let spring remain meaningful without keeping it half-alive. Do not send messages designed to make me remember us. Do not wait for London to fail. Do not treat friendship as a holding pattern."

Malik felt the precision of every request.

"I will not."

"And I will not send you loneliness as if it means we chose wrong."

They hugged. Simone stepped onto the shuttle and chose a seat near the window. She did not make a dramatic final gesture as it pulled away. Malik did not chase the bus with one more thought.

He watched until it turned beyond the Porter Center, then left.

Their relationship had an ending. Respecting it meant not stretching affection into an unofficial continuation neither had chosen.

London belonged to Simone now.

Spring belonged to both of them as history.

## Wednesday, May 6: Roles in Writing

Malik accepted the summer risk-modeling role.

He told Dr. Bennett that he understood Nia and Keisha would be project co-leads and that his family experience could not be used without consent.

"Good," Dr. Bennett said. "You are joining a research team, not a personal narrative."

Across the hall, Dr. Price handed Nia and Keisha revised appointment letters. Both now held co-lead authority. Nia led mathematical modeling and community integration. Keisha led computation, data governance, and reproducibility. Neither title reduced the other's decisions to support.

Keisha signed first.

Nia signed second.

Only afterward did Dr. Price tell them Malik had accepted.

Nia's heart reacted before her face. She let the feeling pass.

"What is his scope?" she asked.

Risk metrics, validation, loss assumptions, and documentation. A professional role with edges.

"That can strengthen the project," Keisha said.

"Yes," Nia agreed.

Neither discussed what it might mean romantically because the appointment letters promised work, not resolution.

## Thursday, May 7: Leave Without a Promise

Before the short break, Malik and Nia met on Founders Lawn.

Campus looked temporary. Chairs were gone. Move-out bins waited near residence halls. Summer students had not yet arrived.

"I accepted," Malik said.

"So did Keisha and I."

"I know."

They reviewed what that meant. They would take Calculus III together, work on the same project, and spend more time in Eliza Moss Hall than either considered medically advisable.

"No private promises before the work begins," Nia said.

"No assuming proximity is progress."

"No pretending we do not have feelings."

"And no letting the feelings make team decisions."

They agreed.

Nia left for Huntsville. Malik left for Gulfport. Keisha traveled to family with her laptop closed for the first forty-eight hours under threat from her track teammates.

For one brief week, no class met and no model ran.

The space between semesters did not resolve anything.

It gave each person room to return by choice.

---

# Week Forty-One: Vectors and Boundaries

Malik returned from Gulfport carrying photographs of new framing inside the Baptiste home.

Fresh lumber outlined the back rooms damaged by the storm. Electrical work had passed inspection. Drywall waited in stacks.

The pictures looked like progress.

They did not contain a move-back date.

One contractor still needed to finish roof work. Insurance reimbursement remained incomplete. Marcel's injured leg had improved, but he could not yet handle every repair task himself.

Malik placed the photographs in archival sleeves. He labeled dates without labeling the family recovered.

The house had a direction.

Its remaining distance was unknown.

## Friday, May 8: Framing Is Not a Finished Room

Celeste showed Malik where the repaired wall would divide the back bedroom from the hall.

"By July, maybe," she said.

Marcel responded from the doorway. "Do not give the contractor a date he did not give us."

They had learned to distrust hopeful estimates spoken as promises.

Before returning to Bellwether, Malik photographed the framing, moisture barriers, and uncompleted ceiling. He asked his parents whether he could show the images to the summer research team if the discussion required a real example of documented and undocumented loss.

"Not yet," Celeste said. "Tell them the story first. Let us decide about pictures after we know why they need them."

"Okay."

The permission boundary was clear.

On the drive back, Malik considered how insurance records would price the repaired materials. The records would not capture months of living around closed rooms, his mother's caution about dates, or his father's frustration at being unable to finish work with his own hands.

Risk metrics turned experience into quantities.

The quantities needed to remain answerable to the people whose experience they represented.

## Saturday, May 9: Carry the Label

Nia returned to campus with the rejected symposium poster rolled beneath one arm.

Keisha saw the fellowship-finalist label printed across the bottom.

"We can trim that off," she said.

Nia considered it. The label made the loss visible. It also described the version they had actually built.

"No," she said. "We archive honestly."

They mounted the poster in the summer project room beside a blank board titled **COMMUNITY VALIDATION QUESTIONS**. The old project and the new work occupied the same wall without pretending they were the same stage.

Julian would contribute during scheduled media sessions around his summer production work. He was not enrolled in the full quantitative program. Imani was preparing for tournament travel. DJ began offseason training. Simone sent the group a photo from orientation, standing beneath an airport departure board with the copper notebook visible in one hand.

Everyone's summer had a different direction.

Nia unpacked her route maps. She felt nervous about seeing Malik and refused to disguise the feeling as research intensity.

"I have feelings," she told Keisha.

"Historically verified."

"I am also a co-lead."

"Then lead the project, not the feelings."

The advice lacked poetry and possessed excellent timing.

## Sunday, May 10: Separate Responsibilities

Summer Quantitative Scholars orientation began in Eliza Moss Hall.

Dr. Renee Okafor welcomed a smaller group than the spring program. Summer moved faster. Calculus III met nearly every weekday. Research sessions occupied afternoons. Students who treated the compressed calendar like ordinary semester time would discover the error before the calendar felt sympathy.

She introduced the evacuation team by responsibility.

Nia: mathematical model and community-integration lead.

Keisha: computation and data-governance lead.

Malik: risk and validation analyst.

Dr. Price supervised the public-mathematics and research design. Dr. Okafor supervised multivariable modeling. No one described Malik and Nia as a natural pair.

During role questions, Malik asked which decisions required co-lead approval. Keisha answered before Nia. Nia supported her answer without repeating it louder.

Progress already.

The team received one shared workspace and separate task folders. Dr. Okafor warned them that interdisciplinary work would create disagreement.

"Professional collaboration does not mean avoiding conflict," she said. "It means making the conflict inspectable."

Nia and Malik exchanged a glance.

They had several kinds of conflict waiting.

Only one belonged in the first design review.

## Monday, May 11: Magnitude Is Not Direction

Calculus III began with vectors.

Dr. Okafor drew arrows in three-dimensional space. Each had magnitude and direction. Two vectors could have equal magnitude while pointing opposite ways. A very large vector aimed badly could move a system far from its goal.

"Students often confuse intensity with progress," she said. "Working harder, caring more, or moving faster tells us nothing about whether the direction is useful."

Nia wrote the sentence beside the rejected fellowship label in her notes.

Malik calculated vector components. Keisha represented a route displacement in three dimensions, adding elevation to the road network. A short horizontal path up a steep grade might impose very different access demands.

During practice, Nia and Malik solved one vector sum almost simultaneously.

The old ease appeared.

Neither treated it as a sign.

After class, Nia went to the project room with Keisha. Malik met Dr. Bennett to review validation standards. They had moved in related directions without attaching themselves to one arrow.

## Tuesday, May 12: Put the Boundaries in the Document

Malik and Nia wrote collaboration rules in a shared document.

Keisha attended as co-lead and delighted witness.

No one-on-one work after nine at night unless the task was urgent and logged with the team.

Private relationship conversations would not occur during project meetings.

Technical disagreement could happen in front of the team. Personal history could not be used to win it.

Either person could request a pause without the pause being treated as abandonment.

No private messages about project decisions that excluded Keisha from co-lead authority.

"That one should be first," Keisha said.

They moved it.

Nia added: no interpreting academic ease as romantic progress.

Malik added: no using distance as punishment after disagreement.

The document felt awkward because it named the exact habits they could otherwise pretend were accidental.

They all signed.

"Congratulations," Keisha said. "Your feelings now have governance."

"Please never say that again," Malik replied.

The rules could not guarantee wise behavior. They could make violations visible before the team paid for them.

## Wednesday, May 13: The Last Road Loss

Baseball's final road series ended with McCall-Hart outside postseason qualification.

The seniors knew before the last out that no tournament invitation was coming. They packed the dugout quietly, then gathered on the field for a team photograph in another school's stadium.

Julian filmed short interviews for campus media. He did not ask what lesson the seniors had learned from losing. He asked what work they wanted younger players to inherit.

One senior named the tutoring schedule they had established. Another named offseason lifting standards. A pitcher described the system for checking on injured teammates after travel.

The season record remained below .500.

The inherited work remained real.

Back in Bellwether, the friend group sent messages without demanding that seniors perform gratitude. A home finale still waited, but postseason hope did not.

Nia thought about the fellowship poster mounted in the project room. A failed desired outcome and useful inherited work could occupy the same wall.

Summer was already asking them to practice that truth again.

## Thursday, May 14: Leave When the Assignment Ends

The first vector assignment took Nia and Malik less than an hour together.

They decomposed forces, found magnitudes, and used unit vectors to express direction. Their approaches fit. Malik checked components. Nia visualized geometry. Each caught one small error from the other.

At 8:17, they finished.

The campus outside Eliza Moss Hall was warm and quiet. In the past, they would have extended the night with another problem, a walk, or a conversation neither was ready to name.

"Good work," Nia said, packing her notebook.

"You too."

They left the building together, reached the courtyard, and separated.

Malik went to North Star. Nia called her mother while walking toward summer housing. Neither turned back to see whether the other did.

The efficient assignment proved they still worked well together.

Leaving on time proved they could decide what that fact was allowed to mean.

---

# Week Forty-Two: Lines That Do Not Meet

Dr. Okafor drew two lines in three-dimensional space.

They were not parallel. Their direction vectors pointed differently. Yet solving their parametric equations produced no shared point.

"Skew lines," Keisha said.

"Correct," Dr. Okafor replied. "Not parallel, not intersecting, and not contained in the same plane."

Nia looked at the board. In two dimensions, nonparallel lines eventually met. The extra dimension allowed them to pass without contact.

"So visual closeness can be misleading," Malik said.

"Frequently. Determine the geometry. Do not infer intersection from appearance."

Their summer roles occupied the same project. Their objectives, they would soon discover, did not yet meet.

## Friday, May 15: Passing in Different Planes

The class built equations for lines and planes from points and direction vectors.

Nia solved intersections by matching components. Malik used normal vectors to calculate distance from a point to a plane. Keisha found a computational method for determining whether two route segments crossed in three-dimensional space or only appeared to cross on a flat map.

That distinction mattered for overpasses, underpasses, and elevated roads. A two-dimensional network could mark two roads as connected when one passed above the other.

"That may explain three false route options," Keisha said.

They corrected the map topology before lunch.

The technical win put everyone in a generous mood. Malik used the opening to propose a new risk metric for the first summer design review.

"Minimize expected travel time across all modeled households," he said. "Include uncertainty penalties for unstable roads."

Nia read the equation. The average outcome improved.

Several neighborhoods with only one or two viable routes remained vulnerable.

"This does not protect people with the fewest choices," she said.

The generous mood left quietly.

## Saturday, May 16: Average Time or Minimum Access

Malik defended the expected-travel-time metric.

It reduced total exposure on the network. It could compare scenarios clearly. It avoided optimizing around rare edge cases at the expense of thousands of other travelers.

Nia objected to the phrase *edge cases*.

"The neighborhoods with one bridge or one elevated road are not errors at the edge of the data," she said. "They are the people most trapped by a bad recommendation."

She proposed an access metric that penalized scenarios leaving any neighborhood without a reliable route choice. Her approach protected the worst-served group, even if average travel time increased.

Malik pointed out that an extreme penalty could reroute large numbers of people inefficiently to improve one area's score by a small amount.

"And your average can look excellent while the same neighborhood loses every time," Nia replied.

Keisha listened from behind her laptop.

Both objectives were mathematically defensible. They optimized different outcomes.

Instead of recognizing the tradeoff, Malik and Nia each began treating the other metric as a disguised moral failure.

The first design review was scheduled for Sunday.

They carried the disagreement into it without translating first.

## Sunday, May 17: The Rejected Proposal Enters the Room

Dr. Okafor asked Nia to explain why her access penalty used the chosen threshold.

Nia cited the symposium model, community interviews, and the need to prevent route isolation.

Malik interrupted. "That threshold was designed for the fellowship presentation. We are supposed to validate the rejected proposal, not preserve it."

The word *rejected* changed the room.

Nia heard more than the technical criticism. She heard that her attachment to the project made her incapable of revising it, that the fellowship loss still defined her judgment, and that Malik had decided he was the person willing to say so.

"I am not preserving anything," she said. "I am protecting the exact households the judges said we had not represented."

"Then test whether the threshold does that. Do not keep it because it carries the values you presented."

"And do not remove it because averages are easier to price."

Dr. Okafor stopped the review.

"Your technical disagreement has become a trial of each other's motives," she said. "No useful design decision will come from that."

She ended the meeting without approving either metric.

The written boundaries had survived less than one week before pressure found their weakest seam.

## Monday, May 18: The People Missing From the Price

Nia arrived Monday still angry.

She reviewed Malik's metric and highlighted every place sparse data reduced estimated loss. Neighborhoods with fewer documented trips, lower insured property values, or inconsistent road records contributed less to the expected total.

"Your objective optimizes away the people whose data are hardest to price," she told him.

Malik went still.

The accusation struck his family history. Insurance records had captured repair materials more easily than displacement, lost work, or months of restricted rooms. Nia did not know that he had spent the weekend thinking about exactly those omissions.

"You think I do not understand missing loss because I use expected value?"

"I think the equation rewards whatever is easiest to count."

"Then criticize the equation. Do not assign me the values inside it."

Nia recognized too late that she had done what Dr. Okafor prohibited. Malik had challenged her threshold as preserved from rejection. She had answered by challenging his care for underdocumented people.

Neither had stayed inside the technical claim.

Keisha shut her laptop with a sound that ended the argument more effectively than shouting.

"Tomorrow," she said. "You are both bringing written work."

"What work?" Nia asked.

"Each other's."

## Tuesday, May 19: Write the Other Objective First

Keisha assigned the exercise at nine.

Malik had to write Nia's access objective accurately, including what it protected, where it could distort decisions, and what evidence would validate the threshold.

Nia had to write Malik's expected-travel-time objective with the same care.

They could not rebut anything until the other person approved the description.

Malik wrote that Nia's metric protected neighborhoods from route isolation and prevented aggregate improvement from hiding repeated harm. He identified its risk: an arbitrary threshold or extreme penalty could worsen systemwide evacuation without enough benefit.

Nia approved the description after changing *small neighborhoods* to *neighborhoods with fewer viable routes*.

Nia wrote that Malik's metric minimized total expected travel and exposure while accounting for uncertain capacity. It enabled scenario comparison and efficient use of the network. Its risk was that sparse or undervalued loss data could make severe local harm nearly invisible inside an average.

Malik approved it after she distinguished a flaw in the data from a flaw inherent to expected value.

Only then did Keisha allow discussion.

The room sounded different. They were no longer arguing over which person cared correctly. They were examining two objective functions with visible benefits and costs.

"We may need both," Malik said.

Nia nodded. "And we may need to show when they disagree."

## Wednesday, May 20: What the Standings Do Not Hold
